#6022. The Backdoor to American Innovation

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

SAM MULLINS is a Professor at the Daniel K. Inouye Asia-Pacific Center for Security Studies.

MICHAEL MAYO is Special Agent and Division Chief for Pacific Operations with the U.S. Naval Criminal Investigative Service and Fellow at the Daniel K. Inouye Asia-Pacific Center for Security Studies.

NIEL KANESHIRO is a Professor at the Daniel K. Inouye Asia-Pacific Center for Security Studies.

How Military Partnerships Make Washington Vulnerable to Its Adversaries

Sam Mullins, Michael Mayo, and Niel Kaneshiro

In late May, a shipment of F-35 stealth fighter jet parts heading for the United States from Australia was misrouted to Hong Kong and into Chinese hands. Since 2007, Chinese intelligence operatives and their proxies had been stealing data to do with the F-35. Now, some of the jet’s closely guarded components, including a cockpit canopy and weapons-bay door, had landed on Chinese soil—handing Beijing stealth technology that only the United States and its allies have access to. China will surely attempt to reverse-engineer the technologies it was lucky to acquire. U.S. officials do not believe there was foul play here and they think the delivery was likely an accident: the result of a shipping delay and a missed email. But even without active foreign interference, the mere logistics of coordinating F-35 production and delivery among Washington, Canberra, Lockheed Martin, and a third-party shipping company exposed defense secrets to the greatest U.S. rival.

The U.S. defense industrial base—the network of domestic and international companies and organizations responsible for the research, production, delivery, and maintenance that undergird American military operations—is growing at a pace not seen in decades, as Washington seeks to place arms manufacturing back on a “wartime footing,” in the words of U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth. The United States is working with allies, notably in the Indo-Pacific, to expand military production and make supply chains resilient to shocks. South Korea has begun performing maintenance and repairs on U.S. naval vessels, Japan is set to quadruple its production of vital antiballistic missiles, and American Precision Strike Missiles will soon be rolling off assembly lines in Australia.

Although this expansion is sensible and necessary, it also carries significant risks. As the U.S. defense base spreads, it becomes more exposed to espionage, theft, and sabotage.

Since the Cold War, U.S. adversaries have targeted the research and infrastructure that give the United States its military advantage. Now, the likes of Russia, China, and North Korea can also learn about American capabilities by mounting espionage campaigns against U.S. allies. Because the U.S. military is more dependent on supply chains that snake around the world, it has become more vulnerable to the disruptive actions of hostile powers.

If the United States fails to safeguard its deepening military partnerships, adversaries will have more opportunities than ever before to at once undermine American defenses and improve their own militaries. Washington must help its partners raise their security standards, share intelligence and best practices more widely, and fortify monitoring programs. If pursued judiciously, this emphasis on security will not hinder cooperation between the United States and its allies but rather strengthen it. But if the United States does not address this threat, one of the country’s great advantages will become a perilous weakness.

In the 1990s, the United States began working more closely with allies to bolster defense production and supply chains amid post–Cold War cuts to military spending. Washington established the National Technology and Industrial Base in 1993, a policy framework that institutionalized cooperation between U.S. and Canadian defense companies. In the following decades, the United Kingdom, Australia, and New Zealand were added to the agreement. But until recently, a number of legal, bureaucratic, and cultural barriers among the five countries hampered this partnership, keeping it from fulfilling its potential.

Over the last five years, however, Washington’s efforts to expand its defense base have begun to speed up. In 2021, the trilateral AUKUS security pact between the United States, the United Kingdom, and Australia—in which the three countries pledged to work together to produce advanced military technologies, including nuclear-powered submarines—was a milestone for defense industrial cooperation. In the 2023 National Defense Industrial Strategy, Washington outlined its commitment to further collaboration with allies, arguing that “the global activity of pacing threats increasingly requires a global approach to defense industrial relationships, concerns, and competition.”

The next year, the United States helped launch the Partnership for Indo-Pacific Industrial Resilience, a forum that brings together some Western and Asian countries to coordinate defense manufacturing and protect important supply chains. Washington has supplemented these multilateral initiatives with bilateral defense production agreements, including pacts with Australia, Japan, South Korea, and Taiwan to partner on critical defense systems, missile production, and shipbuilding. The United States is also planning to accelerate collaboration with India, Indonesia, and the Philippines. Washington has a growing appetite for defense industrial cooperation in the Indo-Pacific, and its partnerships there can thrive—as long as they can be adequately secured.

States have long used espionage to gauge enemy weaknesses and steal cutting-edge technologies. They have also executed sabotage operations to disrupt opponents’ supply chains and hinder their battlefield performance. The Soviet Union, for instance, gained nuclear weapons in part by penetrating the Manhattan Project; more recently, China stole secrets related to the F-22 and F-35 stealth fighter jets and the C-17 cargo plane. Galloping advances in technology invite subterfuge and theft. New breakthroughs in artificial intelligence and quantum computing could have strategic implications on par with the atomic bomb, and U.S. rivals are tenacious in seeking to acquire such technologies themselves.

Today, the American defense base is “under a state of constant, multivector siege” in cyberspace, according to a February report by the Google Threat Intelligence Group. U.S. adversaries are working to steal sensitive information, trade secrets, and intellectual property at every possible level, from recruiting individual employees to hacking manufacturers’ IT networks.

In addition to staging digital attacks, foreign governments such as China and North Korea are cultivating sources inside firms and research institutions focused on defense and dual-use innovation. Governments hostile to the United States, notably China, have sought to establish seemingly legitimate business and academic relationships to gain access to proprietary information. A September 2025 report from the U.S. House Select Committee on China found more than 100 academic partnerships between American universities and Chinese entities that pose a national security risk. State-sponsored actors have also embedded themselves inside American firms and research departments; for instance, North Korean remote IT workers in 2024 infiltrated a U.S. defense contractor and stole data about export-controlled technology. Perhaps most strikingly, U.S. adversaries have used foreign investment, mergers, and even the acquisition of entire companies—sometimes using subsidiaries to mask their involvement—to gain control of critical dual-use technologies. The Times reported that the 2008 buyout of the British-based Dynex Semiconductor by a subsidiary of a Chinese state-owned railway company, for example, gave the Chinese navy access to technology crucial to developing rail guns and electromagnetic launch systems for aircraft carriers.

As the United States has deepened its defense cooperation efforts in the Indo-Pacific, China and North Korea have increasingly targeted U.S. allies in the region. In 2024, South Korea’s National Police Agency revealed that North Korean hackers had mounted all-out cyberattacks against South Korean defense companies for over a year, breaching their networks and stealing technical data. Last January, the agency reported a 40 percent increase in illegal technology transfer cases—that is, of insiders leaking proprietary information on critical technologies to do with semiconductors, batteries, and shipbuilding. Many of those leaks were connected to China.

Japan, which the KGB nicknamed “spy heaven” during the Cold War, remains a hub for espionage. A July New York Times investigation found that members of a Tokyo-based Russian military intelligence unit called the 20th Directorate have spent years posing as diplomats and businesspeople to help Moscow acquire defense technologies that it is banned from importing. This equipment—which includes Japanese components used to guide Russian Kh-101 cruise missiles—has been essential to Russia’s war effort in Ukraine. From 2019 to 2024, the Chinese hacking group MirrorFace conducted over 200 cyberattacks against Japanese defense, national security, and advanced technology targets. And in February 2025, the Japanese military discovered that some of its members had unknowingly circulated for over a year USB sticks that carried malware used by Chinese hacker groups.

In Australia, U.S. adversaries have worked to infiltrate AUKUS in a number of ways. Last year, the Iranian-backed Cyber Toufan hacker group mounted a series of cyberattacks on Israeli defense contractors that gave it access to sensitive material on Australian weapons programs, including the multibillion-dollar Redback infantry fighting vehicle. Mike Burgess, the director general of Australia’s domestic intelligence service, has repeatedly warned that Australia has become a priority espionage mark where “defense personnel are being targeted in person and online.” In 2024, Burgess revealed that a spy ring run by an unspecified foreign government, referred to as “the A-team,” had cultivated and recruited a former Australian politician to assist them. In one scheme, the A-team invited Australian academics and political figures to an all-expenses-paid overseas conference to build intelligence relationships—a tactic often associated with China. The group also offered to pay Australian defense sector employees for information on AUKUS and sensitive technologies such as submarines and missile systems.

American rivals also use sabotage to weaken the broader U.S. defense base. Since 2022, Russian military intelligence has targeted European weapons manufacturers, transportation, and logistics. Moscow even planned to assassinate Armin Papperger, the CEO of the German defense giant Rheinmetall, to undermine the continent’s support for Ukraine. American security services worry adversaries may introduce malicious products into U.S. and allies’ supply chains to steal or alter data, disrupt operations, or interrupt communications. In a wartime scenario, when the defense industrial base is needed most, compromising American production lines or weapons systems could provide opponents with enormous advantages.

The United States and its allies have systems in place to protect against spying and sabotage, but these systems are not always successful. Within the United States, the Defense Counterintelligence and Security Agency is the lead organization for implementing the National Industrial Security Program, which protects the domestic defense industry by governing security clearances, cybersecurity safeguards, and the detection of espionage. But even domestically, many of the most vulnerable parts of the military supply chain—notably, smaller companies that provide specialized software, parts, or services—operate beyond the government’s field of vision and often cannot afford to install sufficient cybersecurity protections. Outside the United States, these problems are magnified.

Although bilateral security agreements establish standards for safeguarding classified military and industrial information, U.S. partners still have vastly different legal regimes, security cultures, and capabilities. Australia is arguably Washington’s most capable Indo-Pacific ally: it is part of the Five Eyes Alliance, an Anglosphere intelligence-sharing agreement; works closely with the American and British militaries through AUKUS; and has a robust, albeit relatively new program focused on securing the defense industry. Still, Burgess has cautioned that the country faces “unprecedented levels of espionage” that will only increase as AUKUS collaboration grows.

South Korea and Japan, while both technologically advanced and capable U.S. partners, have long-standing legal and counterintelligence vulnerabilities that they are only belatedly addressing. Until South Korea passed an expanded espionage statute last February, a loophole in the country’s law meant its government could generally bring espionage charges only in cases related to North Korea, forcing it to rely on different laws to prosecute spies from other countries. As a result, South Korean security services have long been operating with one hand tied behind their back in dealing with Chinese subterfuge; most Chinese nationals caught filming sensitive installations in recent years, for instance, have faced little or no punishment. For its part, Japanese law still does not explicitly prohibit individuals from serving as clandestine agents for a foreign power or accepting payment from a foreign intelligence service, creating a permissive environment for foreign spies to operate in Japan.

Japan is aware of these problems and is reforming its security architecture: Tokyo recently established two bodies to oversee its intelligence agencies and is pursuing a new “anti-spy” law that will finally give authorities an appropriate set of tools to counter espionage. Still, these measures face domestic opposition and will take time to come into effect. Although American allies and partners in the Indo-Pacific are making meaningful progress, regional security standards still lag behind those of the United States.

The United States needs its Indo-Pacific allies to support its military goals in the coming years. But weaknesses in one country’s security architecture create vulnerabilities for all. Washington must help its allies better protect their defense industries. Although the United States has acknowledged this need before, it has not treated it with sufficient urgency and has focused too narrowly on cybersecurity. To be sure, absolute security is impossible, and too rigidly enforcing U.S. standards on allies would slow technological cooperation. Still, the United States must ensure that allies’ security programs are comparable to its own and that any glaring gaps are filled as swiftly as possible.

To launch these efforts, the United States should develop a comprehensive grading system for defense industrial security—a framework that assesses allies’ ability to safeguard critical technologies and identifies areas for improvement. The State Department already uses such a system to evaluate global efforts to counter narcotics and human trafficking. A grading system for defense industrial security would gauge the strength of the entirety of allies’ security apparatuses—including the efficacy of their legal frameworks, cybersecurity, personnel vetting, protection of classified information, foreign investment screening, and supply-chain security—and their resilience against foreign interference and espionage.

At the operational level, Washington should expand existing programs that oversee international defense industrial security. Counterintelligence special agents from organizations such as the Naval Criminal Investigative Service and the Air Force Office of Special Investigations already enforce security standards at home and abroad by guarding critical infrastructure, strengthening cybersecurity, and neutralizing foreign intelligence threats in collaboration with host-country partners. As the defense industrial base expands internationally, more resources will be needed to sustain these operations at scale and ensure that security standards keep pace with joint manufacturing and innovation.

The United States should also expand its information-sharing efforts to alert allies about potential threats and vulnerabilities. Foreign partners currently have limited access, for instance, to Department of Defense cybersecurity information-sharing programs. The United States should consider allowing international defense organizations to access these programs, or else work with allies to develop similar, multilateral information-sharing platforms focused on securing defense production.

Finally, Washington should use the Partnership for Indo-Pacific Industrial Resilience to develop a work stream dedicated to defense industrial security, which would provide a forum for regional partners to establish common security standards and share intelligence best practices. Although nonbinding, policy recommendations from this collaboration would provide authoritative guidance on how to strengthen defense industrial security.

The F-35 parts debacle is a reminder of the inherent vulnerabilities of globalized supply chains, which U.S. adversaries are increasingly eager to exploit. The United States must rely on international partnerships to revitalize its defense industrial base—but true burden sharing is possible only if U.S. partners can keep critical technologies secure. The United States and its allies must strive to stiffen the security of their military research, production, and supply chains. Only with those protections can they realize the gains that come from working together.

How the Search for Economic Security Will End in Geopolitical Calamity

Henry Farrell, Abraham Newman, and Yeling Tan

The Narrow but Feasible Path to Collective Good

Alex Imas, Mohamed A. El-Erian, and James Manyika

#6021. Democrat Haley Stevens endorses GOP Senate nominee Mike Rogers at Detroit church

Dem Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed speaks after Rep. Haley Stevens endorses Mike Rogers

Rep. Haley Stevens, D-Mich., endorsed Republican Senate candidate Mike Rogers on Sunday amid Democratic infighting that has roiled the Michigan Senate race.

The endorsement came on the heels of Stevens' appearance alongside Rogers at Detroit’s Perfecting Church, where Fox News confirmed she attended a service. Stevens lost in the primary to fellow Democrat Abdul El-Sayed, and has been critical of his unwillingness to condemn the overt antisemitism of some of his supporters.

"Hey Michigan, as you may have seen, our Democratic nominee for U.S. Senate, Abdul [El-Sayed] said that I was no longer welcome in his campaign and that I should stay away and he's right. And that's why I'm endorsing Mike Rogers for the United States Senate today," Stevens said in a video posted on X.

Stevens said she remains "a very proud Democrat" who loves Michigan but could not support El-Sayed, citing his backing from political streamer Hasan Piker.

"I'm honored to have Haley Stevens support today. I'm honored to have earned that vote," Rogers told reporters gathered outside the church. "This is more a statement about standing together against hate and the future of this state and this country than it is anything else," he added.

The appearance came after Stevens was disinvited from a planned church event with El-Sayed, the man who narrowly defeated her in Michigan’s August Democratic primary.

The reversal left Stevens at the same church as Rogers, El-Sayed’s Republican opponent.

HALEY STEVENS RIPPED BY FELLOW DEMS FOR REFUSING TO EXPLICITLY BACK EL-SAYED: 'STAY THE F--- OFF TELEVISION'

Democrat Rep. Haley Stevens appears alongside GOP rival Mike Rogers at a Detroit church on Nov. 1, 2026. (Courtesy of Rogers for Senate)

Stevens has pressed El-Sayed to condemn an outside group’s ad linking U.S. support for Israel to higher grocery prices, which she called antisemitic.

El-Sayed said the ad was not produced by his campaign and that he disagreed with its contents.

Rep. Debbie Dingell, D-Mich., who helped organize the planned appearance, said Stevens was asked to skip it after the dispute became a distraction and upset the church’s bishop.

FORMER RIVAL DODGES FULL-THROATED EL-SAYED ENDORSEMENT AFTER BITTER SENATE PRIMARY

Rep. Haley Stevens of Michigan and Abdul El-Sayed faced off in the Aug. 4, 2026, Democratic Senate primary in the Great Lakes battleground state. (Evan Cobb for The Washington Post via Getty Images; Bill Clark/CQ-Roll Call, Inc via Getty Images)

El-Sayed’s campaign also asked Stevens to stay away from its remaining events.

Stevens fiancé, Jason Turkish, has previously endorsed Rogers and appeared at a Rogers campaign event, saying he could not support El-Sayed because of his positions on Israel.

Democratic Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed has faced criticism over his stance on Israel, but some Jewish leaders think members of their community should put aside their differences and vote for him. (USA Today Network via Reuters Connect)

The dispute has put a spotlight on Democratic divisions in a close Senate race that could help determine control of the chamber.

Amanda Macias covers the intersection of business, economics and politics, with a focus on how policy decisions shape markets, businesses and American workers.

#6018. Fame, Fortune, and Murder in 1960s France

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: Fame, Fortune, and Murder in 1960s France

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

It helps if you think of the so-called Markovic affair as an accordion. At the beginning, the story is at its thinnest—small and compact. The year is 1968, and a dead body is found, by accident, in a ditch. The corpse is assumed to be that of a vagabond. He receives a half-hearted autopsy—his skull was smashed in, an obvious cause of death—and gets put in the ground.

Still, the judge investigating the case studies the autopsy report and wonders if there is more to it than meets the eye. Would a random, lowly criminal really have perfectly manicured fingernails? A fingerprint match confirms his hunch: The body belonged to Stevan Markovic, an associate of Alain Delon. That’s where Murder in Paris ’68 really picks up.

It helps if you think of the so-called Markovic affair as an accordion. At the beginning, the story is at its thinnest—small and compact. The year is 1968, and a dead body is found, by accident, in a ditch. The corpse is assumed to be that of a vagabond. He receives a half-hearted autopsy—his skull was smashed in, an obvious cause of death—and gets put in the ground.

Murder in Paris ’68: A True Story of Death and Glamour, Edward Chisholm, Pegasus Crime, 416 pp., $32, November 2026

Still, the judge investigating the case studies the autopsy report and wonders if there is more to it than meets the eye. Would a random, lowly criminal really have perfectly manicured fingernails? A fingerprint match confirms his hunch: The body belonged to Stevan Markovic, an associate of Alain Delon. That’s where Murder in Paris ’68 really picks up.

“Associate” is a loose term, but, as author Edward Chisholm explains over the course of roughly 400 pages, it’s better to be vague than incorrect. It’s tough to describe what the two men meant to each other. Markovic was a young man from Yugoslavia who had come to France illegally in hope of a better life. Delon was a movie star, and one of the most beautiful men in the world. They liked and loathed each other, acting as close friends and betraying one another remorselessly. Before his death, Markovic wrote to his brother saying that, if anything were to happen to him, Delon would be “10,000% responsible.”

The scandal following both Markovic’s death centered on this murky and complex relationship. But, according to Chisholm, it had to do with much more than that. The affair was about the nature of fame and fortune in the 1960s, the influence World War II still had on French politics, and a country desperately trying to move toward the future yet weighed down by its heavy past. There was one man at the center of it, sure, but in Chisholm’s telling, you can easily grab the accordion and open it wide, showcasing its countless folds.

Delon with his wife, Nathalie, and their son in Monte Carlo in 1965.Archive Photos via Getty Images

Really, the story begins a decade earlier. In 1955, a 19-year-old Delon left the army after three years. His military record was far from spotless. He spent nearly a third of his time there in prison—for “[o]perating a vehicle without authorization and without a valid licence, resulting in a serious accident,” “[p]ossession of a prohibited weapon,” and “[c]ausing public scandal while ashore,” among other offences. His file concluded that he was “[a] bad element in every respect.”

Now a civilian again, his first port of call was Toulon, in the south of France. He was there to see an old acquaintance, François Marcantoni, a Corsican gangster and former French Resistance fighter who, like many others at the time, moved relentlessly between worlds.

France in the 1950s and 1960s was still fundamentally shaped by the war—by the Nazi occupation and its collaborators in the Vichy government, and by those who fought back. Marcantoni was “arrested in ’44 in a stolen car, running an op for the Resistance. The Gestapo had tortured him,” Chisholm writes. For someone like him, “the post-war years offered two paths: the police, or Pigalle [a neighborhood in Paris]. For François, the choice was obvious. The cops had collaborated, they were the enemy then—and they still were.”

Pigalle was where not-so-reformed Corsican gangsters set up their dodgy bars in the years following the liberation, and where Delon wound up in the late 1950s, when he landed his breakout role in Purple Noon, an adaptation of Patricia Highsmith’s The Talented Mr. Ripley.

A 21-year-old Markovic, meanwhile, made his escape from Josip Broz Tito’s Belgrade to Western Europe in 1958, the same year that Charles de Gaulle, once venerated then cast into the political wilderness, returned to the French presidency.

After a short prison stint in 1965, Markovic, who sustained himself through odd jobs and low-level crime, was in need of a place to stay. His best friend, Milos Milosevic, had managed to befriend Delon a few years earlier while the actor was filming in Belgrade. (Milosevic had staged a fake mugging, before “stepping in” to rescue Delon, and eventually became part of Delon’s entourage.) Milosevic soon convinced Delon to host his friend.

Protest and radicalization are the answer to an authoritarian, centralized state.

François Mitterrand’s U-turn decades ago holds lessons for today.

What happened afterward is complicated. Milosevic died in Hollywood in 1966, in what looked like a murky murder-suicide involving the estranged wife of actor Mickey Rooney. After that, Markovic became an integral part of Delon’s life. As Chisholm explains, Markovic was “[n]ot quite a secretary, not a bodyguard, not an employee. Delon doesn’t pay him—unless you count the occasional envelope. His job is more…ambient.”

“He’s become the man in the background. Pushing the pram through Parc Monceau, driving [Delon’s wife] Nathalie’s Alfa, standing behind the couple at film galas, cocktail in hand,” Chisholm adds. “He’s always there.”

The situation may seem idyllic from the outside; it wasn’t. The nature of the trio’s dynamic was not entirely clear, but the Delons’ marriage was unstable and violent. Sometimes, Markovic felt like he was part of the family; often, the couple put him firmly back in his place. The letters he sent to friends in those years indicate that he was frustrated by this state of affairs, and by Delon’s frequent refusal to sign his residency papers, keeping his legal status irregular.

In 1967, the couple separated. Nathalie moved out, Markovic followed her, and the pair watched as Delon unilaterally announced the divorce on television. What followed was a short-lived affair between the two, which may have sealed his fate.

Markovic’s body was found just over a year later. While much remains unknown, Chisholm makes it clear that the events surrounding the murder cannot be explained without one man: Marcantoni. He was a friend of Delon’s, still, and knew Markovic. He spent the 1960s arguing that he was no longer a gangster—retired long ago—and merely a “victim of [his] reputation,” which few people bought even then. He’s mentioned by name in the infamous letter Markovic sent to his brother. Was he a murderer?

The book alleges that he is (it can afford to—Marcantoni died in 2010), but the murder was never solved. There are, Chisholm explains, four prevailing theories. The first is that Delon had Markovic killed for sleeping with his wife, probably enlisting Marcantoni’s help. The second is that Markovic planned to sell stories and pictures of the Delons, but they found out and had him killed, likely with Marcantoni. The third is that Markovic’s personal dealings with the underworld got him killed, possibly by Marcantoni, possibly by someone else.

The fourth—watch as the accordion expands—is that, through his association with the Delons, Markovic had made his way into the upper echelons of French society, where politicians, singers, actors, and high-end criminals partied and slept together in secret. Could the young Yugoslav have gone to those events, taking covert pictures of the Parisian elite in compromising positions? We know now that no evidence exists (or remains) of this, but the theory tantalized France for months after his death.

Most shocking of all were snaps that emerged of a woman engaged in a tryst with another woman, which made their way to the Parisian police. The woman was believed to be Claude, the wife of Prime Minister Georges Pompidou. These were so obviously doctored that they did not even become a part of the police investigation, but the story was too salacious to die quietly. De Gaulle, aging, tired, did little to protect the man who had been faithfully at his side since his return to the Élysée Palace a decade earlier. Meanwhile, political opponents, hungry journalists, and at least one Czech spy conspired to ensure that the Pompidou name would keep being embroiled in the Markovic affair.

It helped Marcantoni, as the main suspect, that this merry band of interested parties was invested in turning the story into a political scandal. In 1969, he was arrested and held in custody after investigators had traced the plastic in which the body had been wrapped to packaging used by a mattress brand that Marcantoni had in his house. He spent just under a year behind bars, then left a free man. With no one talking, there just wasn’t enough there for a conviction.

Though the case rumbled on after that, the Markovic affair was essentially over. France had turned a new leaf too. The riots of May ’68, which had united students, workers, and millions of others in protesting for a brighter future, had initially failed to remove de Gaulle from his post. But a year later, despite the success of his party and the support of figures including Delon, de Gaulle resigned. Pompidou, a man who did not fight during the war and whose love of contemporary art and friendships in the entertainment world shocked his predecessors, took his place. Finally, the future of France was taking form. Everyone was moving on.

Marcantoni, now free for good, published multiple books concerning the affair, including the provocatively titled But Who Killed Markovic?. He became famous for claiming that “only three people know the truth. Delon, me and God. And God never snitches.” Delon died two years ago, and though his career continued for decades after the murder, it largely peaked in the 1960s. Maybe there was a reason he supported de Gaulle and not the idealist youths in 1968. On some level, he knew that, despite being in his early 30s, he could only ever be part of the old guard.

Delon (center) leaves court in Versailles in 1973.AFP via Getty Images

That is the book’s thesis anyway. Much of Murder in Paris ’68 tries to convince us that the affair was, in some way, about all of human life. “At first, I thought this was a story about a murder,” Chisholm writes. “Then it became a story about cinema. Then politics, espionage, a cover-up. But somewhere along the way, it became something else. A story about power. About what happens when someone tries to become more than they are allowed to be.”

This is one way to interpret it, but it is equally easy to read Chisholm’s yarn and come to another conclusion altogether. Delon was a cold, unpleasant man with a large ego who relished the company of outlaws. His wife was young, unreliable, and prone to follow her husband’s lead in toying with those she deemed beneath her. Many of the people they socialized with were either low-level swindlers or hardened criminals, always looking to make a quick buck.

Markovic was a man who got lucky and unlucky; who cheated and stole his way through life, then, for a short while, found himself attached to a man who happened to be a god. Always, he returned to what he knew: petty, lazy crime. It’s possible to squint and follow Chisholm in comparing him to Brutus or Iago—Shakespeare’s second men—but, really, he may have just been a minor crook who died in the way minor crooks sometimes did in those days.

By dragging Markovic’s lifeless body back in the spotlight today, Chisholm has merely become the latest in a long line of people, from Pompidou’s detractors to Marcantoni’s publishers, willing to use him for their own ends. It makes for a compelling read, though you should remember that, even as an accordion is at its widest, it’s only ever full of air.

#6016. Only the Gulf Can End the Iran War

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ALLISON MINOR is a Senior Fellow in the Middle East Program at the Center for Strategic and International Studies and served as Deputy U.S. Special Envoy for Yemen from 2021 to 2023.

NATE SWANSON is a Resident Senior Fellow and Director of the Iran Strategy Project at the Atlantic Council. He served as Director for Iran at the National Security Council from 2022 to 2025. In the spring and summer of 2025, he served on the Trump administration’s Iran negotiating team.

The Arab Monarchies Must Help Washington and Tehran Save Face

For everyone involved, the war in Iran has been a strategic failure. The United States and Israel have shown that they can achieve a few military objectives, such as sinking Iran’s navy. But after eight months of fighting and pressure, they have also proved that they cannot forcibly realize their main ambitions, including regime change, destroying Iran’s nuclear program, or even reopening the Strait of Hormuz—a waterway that was not closed when the war began. The Islamic Republic, for its part, has demonstrated that it can survive intense bombings. Yet it cannot break the American military’s economically suffocating blockade and has thus far failed to achieve its goal of putting an end to indefinite threats of war against Iran.

In theory, the war’s descent into a painful stalemate should open the door to diplomatic outcomes. But both sides have proved incapable of making the concessions necessary to strike a deal. Instead, they appear to prefer a mutually destructive status quo that maintains the delusion of achievable victory and which they can justify with the continued pain they inflict on their adversaries. As a result, rather than moving toward a settlement, both sides are threatening renewed attacks.

But there is a face-saving way for Iran and the United States to extricate themselves from this conflict: a cease-fire brokered and managed by the Gulf Arab states. The deal would begin with Gulf countries providing economic relief to Iran in exchange for Iran lifting its restrictions on the Strait of Hormuz. This would, in turn, allow the United States to lift its own blockade. The Gulf states would also need to commit to taking steps to deter Washington from resuming strikes on Iran in the future. But neither the United States nor Iran would need to make direct concessions to the other, and each would avoid the most painful compromises. The United States, for example, would not have to lift its “maximum pressure” campaign, and Iran would not have to agree to limit its nuclear program. Gulf states could then use their leverage to sustain the cease-fire until conditions permit a more comprehensive agreement.

Serving as the guarantor of a cease-fire comes with real costs and risks for the Gulf, but its countries have good reason to accept them. They are part of the battlefield on which the Iran war is being fought, and should the conflict continue indefinitely, it could pose a threat to the very economic and political models on which they are built. The Gulf depends on oil and gas exports to sustain itself, and there is no way it can completely bypass the Strait of Hormuz in shipping its energy resources abroad. Its efforts to diversify away from hydrocarbons, meanwhile, are also vulnerable to renewed attacks from Iran.

Securing a cease-fire will not be easy. It requires that the Gulf countries overcome their own lack of cohesion, that Iran prioritize economic recovery and forgo explicit guarantees about U.S. behavior, and that the United States gives Gulf countries the space necessary to reach a deal. Even if successful, the resulting agreement will not address the underlying issues driving the war. But with scant alternatives on the horizon, it is the best—and perhaps the only—way to ease the pain for everyone.

In theory, a U.S.-Iranian agreement should be straightforward. There is a historical template for deals between Iran and the United States—Tehran agrees to constrain or give up a specific behavior, and Washington agrees to give it money and recognition—that could apply neatly to the current conflict. In the Algiers Accords of 1981, for instance, Iran agreed to release American diplomats it held hostage in exchange for frozen funds and a commitment by the United States not to intervene in Iran’s internal affairs. In the 2015 nuclear deal, Tehran accepted strict limits on its nuclear program in return for sanctions relief and an implicit acceptance of its right to enrich uranium. Today, Iran could stop impeding maritime traffic through the Strait of Hormuz (and perhaps eventually make concessions on its nuclear program), and Washington could grant it some combination of sanctions relief, cash, and acknowledgment of Tehran having a formal role in the waterway.

In practice, however, reaching this understanding will be extremely difficult. One reason is technical: Iran won’t make the nuclear concessions that U.S. President Donald Trump wants, and the United States will not provide the upfront, irreversible financial incentives that Tehran demands. But the bigger problem is reputational. To justify his quixotic war and decision to withdraw from the 2015 nuclear deal, Trump wants sweeping concessions from Tehran. Iran, for its part, is loath to settle for fewer benefits than it received from the United States in their ill-fated June memorandum of understanding, in which Washington pledged significant relief. As a result, both sides feel compelled to sustain the confrontation, even though doing so is mutually detrimental.

The Gulf states cannot resolve the dispute over Iran’s nuclear program. But they can bring about relative peace by helping address other essential issues. The United States may not be willing to pay Iran for merely opening the Strait of Hormuz, but its Gulf partners have the wealth and the motivation to offer the regime immediate economic concessions. Those partners could, for instance, give Iran some form of humanitarian and reconstruction funding. Iran, in turn, would stop attacks on ships transiting the strait and on Gulf states themselves. In response to Iran ceasing its attacks on commerce, the United States would lift its own blockade, and the Gulf states and Iran would establish a temporary deconfliction mechanism for shipping that acknowledges Iran’s role in the waterway without establishing unacceptable precedents for international commerce. This mechanism could require that ships use Iran’s preferred traffic pattern but would not formalize Iranian control over the passage.

This particular arrangement would not resolve Iran’s other core objective—prohibiting the United States and Israel from launching a renewed military campaign. This concern is harder to address. The United States may lack a credible military endgame, and the war might be unpopular domestically, but that has not stopped an erratic Trump from drawing up plans for renewed military action. But the Gulf states can provide Iran with assurances that they would use their leverage with Washington to help deter future attacks. For example, in May, Kuwait and Saudi Arabia temporarily paused U.S. access, basing, and overflight—the approvals that allow the United States military to use Gulf countries’ territory and airspace—because of concerns about the United States’ plans for escorting merchant ships through the Strait of Hormuz. The tactic worked: the United States paused the operation.

The United States might bristle at handing Iran negotiations over to the Gulf countries. But ultimately, U.S. officials should be able to spin this deal as a win. Trump, for instance, could exit the war while telling Americans that he saved the world from high energy prices without making the kind of economic and nuclear concessions to Iran that the Obama administration did under the 2015 agreement. He could likewise boast about the steps he took to weaken Iran, and, in the absence of a nuclear deal, point out that his self-proclaimed “maximum pressure” sanctions remain in place. In fact, in some ways, a Gulf-brokered deal is exactly the kind of “America first” foreign policy Trump promised. It would let the region take on the burden of managing a bad actor, including responsibility for providing most of the necessary concessions. Trump would have stumbled into the outcome. But he would still leave office with an empowered set of allies more capable of managing their own security.

Iran, for its part, would not only get significant economic benefits. It might also advance its goal of reducing the United States’ influence in the region. After all, if Israel or the United States undermines an arrangement down the road, it will only accelerate the decline of Washington’s credibility and influence with its Gulf partners. The United States has already placed its relationship with these countries under stress by going to war with Iran, something they did not ask for and for which all have suffered serious consequences. A Gulf state deal would thus allow Tehran to leverage any future U.S. military campaigns against Iran to further drive a wedge between the United States and its regional partners.

Iran has repeatedly made clear that its top war-related priority is to end the war on terms that reduce the likelihood of a future conflict. To some extent, Iran has already achieved this benchmark with the pain it has inflicted on the global economy. Assuming this war eventually ends, the next time an Israeli prime minister asks an American president to approve an attack on Iran, that president will have to weigh global economic instability against Israeli security interests. The threat posed by Iran, in the absence of a nuclear breakout or massive terrorist attack, may not outweigh the risk to the global economy. Israel might act independently of the United States, but doing so would risk further damaging the U.S.-Israeli relationship and invite Iranian retaliation without American protection.

But this deterrent is effective only once the current iteration of the war ends. If the war continues, Iran will forfeit the future de facto security guarantee that the Strait of Hormuz and geography provide. The longer this conflict goes on, the more it favors those in Israel or the United States advocating for regime change or endless war. Unless there is a solution to high energy prices that allows Trump to disengage from Iran, he will continue grasping, and it could eventually lead him to adopt aggressive kinetic options that have not yet been tried—and for good reason.

Iran and the Gulf countries are already exploring regionally led solutions to the conflict. On October 26, Saudi officials traveled to Tehran to meet with their Iranian counterparts, alongside leaders from Pakistan and Turkey. Gulf countries had also planned to meet with Iran in Salalah, Oman, on September 14 to discuss the Strait of Hormuz. But that gathering never happened, and unless there is a broader regional solution, Gulf countries will instead mostly pursue private, bilateral deals with Iran to help forestall future attacks on their ships or territory. These agreements might help lessen the pain for individual Gulf countries in the short term, but they fail to address the overall risk of Iranian escalation or resolve the global consequences stemming from disputes related to the Strait of Hormuz. Furthermore, they allow Iran to stoke division and distrust between Gulf countries while driving up its own demands.

To get a workable deal, the Gulf states will thus need to improve internal cohesion. This is easier said than done, particularly in light of continued tensions between two of the major Gulf players—Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates. But fixing this issue is wholly within the power of Gulf states, and the Salalah effort demonstrates there is some interest in doing so. Improving cohesion among Gulf states also does not require resolving the larger bilateral tensions and competition among the region’s capitals; it only requires an agreement to isolate the Iran issue from those tensions and address this shared imperative. The Gulf states must therefore collectively agree on redlines for Iranian behavior and coordinate on how to enforce them. If Iran seeks to test the agreement or demand more concessions from Gulf governments, they must collectively push back by pausing economic aid or cooperation—even if it means risking Iranian retaliation.

The Gulf states and Iran will also be tested by the Houthis. Saudi Arabia understandably holds Iran accountable for Houthi attacks on its territory because Iran helped build the group’s capabilities, continues to provide it with significant technical and material support, and helped trigger the re-escalation of the Yemen conflict in July. But Iran does not control the Houthis, so the group is not going to stop attacking Saudi Arabia or call off its campaign inside Yemen simply because Iran says to. Tehran will thus need to extend an olive branch to Riyadh by showing that it is willing to limit its direct involvement in the current Houthi attacks against Saudi Arabia. For instance, Iran could credibly promise Riyadh that Tehran-backed Iraqi militias will stop attacking the kingdom. It was, after all, Iraqi militia attacks that took the kingdom’s valuable East-West oil pipeline offline on September 10, and defending against attacks from both the north (where Saudi Arabia borders Iraq) and south (where it borders Yemen) greatly complicates Riyadh’s already strained air defenses.

These efforts to craft a deal might still come up short. Even if the Gulf states overcome their internal divisions and sideline concerns about Houthi activity, Tehran and Washington could refuse to sign on. Iran could conclude that control of the strait and the ability to throttle energy markets are its best security guarantee, whereas the United States might struggle to even implicitly acknowledge that the war will not achieve the outcome its leaders promised. And even if the cease-fire agreement does work, the result will be far from ideal, for both the United States and its Gulf partners. It could encourage Iran to increase its control over the strait, and it could weaken U.S.–Gulf state partnerships. Worse yet, it fails to address the underlying issues driving the U.S.-Iranian conflict and the tensions between Iran and its neighbors: Tehran’s nuclear program and support for armed groups, respectively.

But a comprehensive Iran deal is not feasible today. A Gulf-brokered outcome on the Strait of Hormuz is. In fact, it may be the only way to stop a mutually hurting stalemate that is poised to last years and cause mounting damage to global markets. The strategies that states have employed to blunt the consequences of global oil and gas shortages will likely start wearing thin in 2027, as governments deplete their strategic reserves, Chinese demand increases, and alternative supply routes out of the Gulf come under Iranian attack. The economic consequences of the conflict could even undermine the domestic stability of the most vulnerable Gulf states or reignite old conflicts, as happened in Yemen this year. A Gulf-brokered deal, then, is the least bad outcome. Such is the irony of this conflict: the bystanders that have already lost the most will need to save the warring parties from themselves.

#6015. 'Squad' rakes in record $2.35M while slamming the door on cop donations

Panelists Meghan Hays and Tiffany Smiley discuss how Democratic socialist candidates receive millions in progressive political contributions on ‘America Reports.'

A joint fundraising committee for four progressive House Democrats has raised a record $2.35 million so far this election cycle, while barring donations from the Fraternal Order of Police, according to federal filings and the committee’s disclosure.

The Squad Victory Fund is authorized by Reps. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, D-N.Y.; Ilhan Omar, D-Minn.; Ayanna Pressley, D-Mass.; and Rashida Tlaib, D-Mich. The fundraising gives the four lawmakers and their allied political groups a shared vehicle to collect and distribute campaign contributions.

ILHAN OMAR’S WEALTH SCANDAL TAKES A TURN JUST ONE DAY AFTER RAID OF HER DC HOME

"Squad" members of Congress Tlaib, Omar and Ocasio-Cortez. (AP Photo/Amanda Andrade-Rhoades, File; Photo by Scott Heins/Getty Images; AP Photo/Angelina Katsanis)

The fund’s donor rules also make clear which groups it says it will not accept money from, and one is the Fraternal Order of Police, the nation’s largest police organization. It also bars donations from corporations, federal lobbyists and fossil fuel interests. The disclosure does not explain the FOP exclusion.

SOCIALIST PRIMARY SWEEP SPARKS LAUNCH OF NEW RESOURCE LAYING OUT WHAT’S AT STAKE IN 2026

The Squad Victory Fund says it will not accept contributions from the Fraternal Order of Police, the nation’s largest police organization. (Tom Brenner/The Washington Post/Getty Images)

The fund’s other participants include four allied PACs. Contributions are generally divided equally among the eight participants, with each slated to receive 12.5%, though donors may designate their contributions for a specific participant.

The fund has raised $2,351,388.02 in the 2025–2026 election cycle, its highest total to date, according to its FEC summary.

Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez departs following a vote at the U.S. Capitol in Washington, D.C., US, on July 22, 2026. (Aaron Schwartz/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Through March 31, 2026, it had spent about $1.6 million and reported roughly $68,000 in cash on hand. The committee remains active, so those figures may change.

Contributions are not tax-deductible, the disclosure notes.

#6014. AI Isn’t a Revolution—Yet

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: AI Isn’t a Revolution—Yet

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

For a time, in the 1980s, the Industrial Revolution lost its status as a “revolution.” New quantitative measures threatened to demote Britain’s 18th-century economic transformation to the status of an unglamorous, long-term “process.” It simply unfolded too slowly and in too few sectors to justify terminology implying total, overnight transformation.

Ultimately, the Industrial Revolution was rehabilitated as a “revolution”—albeit with the recognition that it was more gradual and uneven than the word traditionally implies. The question in this scholarly debate was never whether the steam engine was revolutionary, because people and institutions determine whether a potentially game-changing invention turns out to have truly revolutionary impact. A key factor in the Industrial Revolution’s slow unfolding was British people’s active resistance to changes instigated by steam power and factory organization—to an extent that shook posterity’s very certainty of its standing as a “revolution.”

For a time, in the 1980s, the Industrial Revolution lost its status as a “revolution.” New quantitative measures threatened to demote Britain’s 18th-century economic transformation to the status of an unglamorous, long-term “process.” It simply unfolded too slowly and in too few sectors to justify terminology implying total, overnight transformation.

Ultimately, the Industrial Revolution was rehabilitated as a “revolution”—albeit with the recognition that it was more gradual and uneven than the word traditionally implies. The question in this scholarly debate was never whether the steam engine was revolutionary, because people and institutions determine whether a potentially game-changing invention turns out to have truly revolutionary impact. A key factor in the Industrial Revolution’s slow unfolding was British people’s active resistance to changes instigated by steam power and factory organization—to an extent that shook posterity’s very certainty of its standing as a “revolution.”

CEOs of AI companies who cry that their technology is too intrinsically revolutionary to handle and that they must therefore be exempted from accountability to antitrust and product liability regulations exploit the U.S. public’s loose grasp of this historical truth: that it (and the wider global public) has an enormous role in deciding whether a technology is revolutionary.

Though working-class British people lacked the right to vote and unions were barely legal when steam power appeared, with their organizing efforts they both slowed the technology’s spread and ensured they had some say in setting the terms of its use through laws regulating workers’ hours, safety, and age. They rioted and broke machines, they created underground unions, held mass public gatherings, petitioned and marched, wrote and printed pamphlets. They presented such a threat to the oligarchy that the government spared massive amounts of troops to suppress them even as it fought Napoleon around the world.

They persisted because the stakes were enormous: an entire way of life. A way of life in which work and life were not sharply differentiated, in which customs of trade assured standards of craftsmanship, just prices, and fair wages, and in which those who were not wealthy still had self-respect and a sense of independence. Many ordinary Britons experienced the Industrial Revolution’s threat to this way of life as a violence to human nature worth shedding blood over.

A drawing depicts Luddites destroying textile machines in the United Kingdom circa 1811. API/Gamma-Rapho via Getty Images

Though the term “Luddite” has become an epithet for the irrational rejection of new technology, the machine-wrecking Luddites of the 1810s were not fighting power looms themselves but the exploitative and oppressive relationships governing factory owners’ adoption of them. A tax on power looms, for instance, would have helped equalize competition and fund relief for displaced handloom weavers.

“What was at issue,” the historian E. P. Thompson explained in the 1960s, “was the ‘freedom’ of the capitalist to destroy the customs of the trade, whether by new machinery, by the factory-system, or by unrestricted competition, beating-down wages, undercutting his rivals, and undermining standards of craftsmanship … [T]he ‘free’ factory-owner … who built his fortune by these means, was regarded … as a man engaging in immoral and illegal practices.” To ordinary Britons, laissez-faire capitalism was not freedom but a “foul Imposition”: no “natural law” permitted a man to engage in practices that injured fellow men. The Luddites didn’t look only backward but also forward to a new, democratic industrial society in which pursuit of profit would be subordinated to ethical priorities and collective needs.

The CEOs’ framing of AI as an unstoppable force that will inevitably transform everything overnight exploits the popular understanding of the Industrial Revolution as an overnight transformation caused by the invention of steam power by unfettered geniuses—a fable generated by liberal, and neoliberal, political economic theory. When devotees of this theory do recognize the Industrial Revolution’s slowness, they take that pace for granted as its intrinsic nature, rather than the contingent product of the agency of masses of dispossessed people. Thus, the economist Daron Acemoglu sees our times as “unprecedented,” because “the first phase of the Industrial Revolution lasted for 80 years. Today it’s taking place in one or two years … across many sectors at the same time.” Though helpfully warning of the risks posed by AI, Acemoglu blinds himself to the useful precedent of resistance behind the seeming dissimilarity of the Industrial Revolution.

We have not suddenly departed from set norms of economic transformation; no such laws of history guide the march of economic and technological progress. Britain’s Industrial Revolution in fact depended greatly on government military contracts, just as military contracts drove the Cold War rise of Silicon Valley and have proved critical for the development of AI itself. Technological leaps are not the result of some inexorable historical process; they have been the product of intentional, high-risk investment in innovation made possible by the vast resources of powerful governments. The U.S. government’s passivity toward the harms of AI companies’ products—its ongoing failure to enforce existing laws against distribution of defective and harmful products—is yet another way it is accelerating the spread of AI.

AI companies, the U.S. government, and liberal myths about history have thus set AI up to be disruptive—to be revolutionary in its impact. And it will be, so long as the U.S. public succumbs to the CEOs’ claims about how technological revolutions happen and fails to recognize its capacity to contain them—as ordinary British people reined in the Industrial Revolution. The CEOs’ framing of AI as inherently revolutionary helps elicit special favors from the government and discourage public pushback. The government’s partnership with these companies thus far means that it will not hold them accountable on its own. Public pressure is required.

Indeed, the public has been involved from the outset as the taxpayers who fund the government’s contracts; they can and should demand a role in the development of AI going forward and reprise their past role in asserting democratic oversight over disruptive technologies.

Protesters take part in a “Stop the AI Race” demonstration calling for a slowdown in the development of advanced artificial intelligence systems, seen outside City Hall in San Francisco on Sept. 17.Karl Mondon/AFP via Getty Images

This is not a genie-can’t-be-put-back-in-the-bottle situation. The European Union in 2024 passed laws prohibiting AI systems that threaten human safety and rights, which have already slowed the rollout of AI products there. U.S. policymakers must at the very least apply existing regulations for protecting against environmentally harmful and defective products to AI. Taxpayers must also demand that the government use the leverage of its reputation-enhancing contracts to insist AI companies prioritize the safety of their products over the reckless expansion of those products’ capacities.

Making such demands does not automatically hand disruptive power to China, whose AI products would be held to the same standards and regulations for use in the United States (where many companies already use Chinese open models). The nuclear non-proliferation deals the United States brokered with its arch enemy, the Soviet Union, at the height of the Cold War are historical proof of the possibility of limiting the spread of dangerous technologies through global cooperation.

Beijing has a strategy. Washington has a voluntary pledge and a “Super Intelligence Force.”

A global boom is underway. Are countries ready?

Talk of “super intelligence” distracts from frequent screwups.

But effective pushback requires organizing and effort beyond what a citizenry trained to express itself only through the vote is accustomed to. It requires new kinds of unions in new kinds of workplaces, and old kinds of crowd action. It requires shifting pension investments away from these companies. It requires supporting coalitions of concerned AI scientists—and ensuring no AI CEO hijacks calls for new regulations to propose laws that exempt their products from ordinary liability (such as Anthropic’s draft bill that would allow the government to block its models only if there’s potential for $1 billion in damages, or more than 50 people are at risk of being killed or injured). It means suing companies that release unsafe AI products when their corporate allies fail to, as one nonprofit has now done for the OpenAI hack into Hugging Face.

Public insistence on accountability is clearly effectual—OpenAI has paused the release of its latest model due to security concerns. But the public must organize even without a guarantee that their efforts will succeed. Resistance didn’t stop the Industrial Revolution, but it did slow it down, and that mattered to the generations who lived through it. It also ensured that British workers’ living standards ultimately rose. More importantly, within their collective organizations, ordinary Britons reclaimed the dignity and humanity that they were fighting for. As catastrophic as their sufferings were during the Industrial Revolution, the craftsmen resisting its harms produced what Thompson called “the most distinguished popular culture England has known,” yielding countless inventors, organizers, journalists, and political theorists, and a vision of a new kind of democratic society.

Likewise, in resuming their historic struggle against oligarchic exploitation, Americans stand to gain much culturally and socially, quite apart from the impact they might have on stopping the spread of harmful AI products: These very activities offer the chance at recovering dignity, equity, independence—humanness itself.

The American public will decide whether there is an “AI revolution.”

Priya Satia is the Raymond A. Spruance professor of international history at Stanford University and the award-winning author of Spies in Arabia: The Great War and the Cultural Foundations of Britain’s Covert Empire in the Middle East and Empire of Guns: The Violent Making of the Industrial Revolution. Her most recent book is Time’s Monster: How History Makes History.

#6010. Connecticut Dem Sen Chris Murphy hospitalized Saturday for stroke

Sen. Chris Murphy, D-Conn., suffered a stroke and was taken to a hospital on Saturday. (Kevin Dietsch/Getty Images)

Sen. Chris Murphy, D-Conn., was hospitalized after experiencing a stroke, his chief of staff Allison Herwitt said in a statement released Sunday.

Murphy, who is not running in the midterms but has been stumping for Democrats, began feeling numbness in his arm Saturday afternoon while at his son’s hockey game and was taken to a local hospital before being transferred to Johns Hopkins for further monitoring, according to Allison Herwitt.

WHAT TO DO IF SOMEONE IS HAVING A STROKE, AFTER JILL BIDEN REVEALED DEBATE-NIGHT FEARS

Sen. Chris Murphy, D-Conn., talks with reporters during votes in the U.S. Capitol on Wednesday, Sept. 30, 2026. (CQ-Roll Call, Inc via Getty Images)

"He was taken to a local hospital, where it was discovered he had experienced a stroke. He was transferred to Johns Hopkins for further monitoring and is responding well to treatment," she said in a statement.

"The doctors are encouraged by his progress, he is awake and alert, and we are optimistic he will make a full recovery."

Murphy was first elected to the Senate in 2013 and has been viewed as a potential 2028 Democratic presidential primary candidate.

He has spent the past several weeks on the campaign trail with Democrats as he stumped to help retake control of the Senate majority in the midterm elections. He had been scheduled to campaign for Democrats in New Hampshire on Sunday.

Murphy is 53 years old, born August 3, 1973.
