#6009. Don’t Go Backward on China Policy

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ALI WYNE is Senior Research and Advocacy Adviser for U.S.-China Relations at the International Crisis Group.

RYAN HASS is Director of the John L. Thornton China Center and Chen-Fu and Cecilia Yen Koo Chair in Taiwan Studies at the Brookings Institution.

Nearly halfway through his second term in office, U.S. President Donald Trump has undercut both the United States’ capacity to protect its interests related to China and its role as a provider of regional security in Asia. His undisciplined approach to dealing with Beijing, in which he has focused on building warm personal ties with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and discounted or ignored long-standing sources of tension between the two countries, has reduced Washington’s leverage to shape the relationship. At the same time, Trump’s sustained attacks on allies and partners have driven them to hedge against the United States and strengthen their ties with China. Beijing, in turn, has successfully cast itself as a steady counterweight to an erratic Washington: in 25 of 36 countries that the Pew Research Center surveyed in the spring of 2026—among them Australia, Canada, France, Germany, and Mexico—more respondents had a favorable view of China than of the United States.

Given the failures of Trump’s second-term strategy, many Democrats and even moderate Republicans may try to strike more hawkish postures on China after the midterm elections and during the 2028 presidential campaign. This temptation is understandable: there is a strong desire to provide a contrast to a flailing approach and correct what Trump has gotten wrong. But succumbing to it would be dangerous. If Congress and future presidential candidates reflexively adopt a more aggressive stance toward China, they could both undercut a future administration’s capacity to preserve vital diplomatic space with Beijing and further disquiet allies and partners that do not want to be trapped in a new twilight struggle. Overcorrecting Trump’s China policy would also be unlikely to help candidates get elected or boost their public profile in the run-up to 2028. Recent polling shows that only a small share of Americans in seven battleground states view China as an enemy or consider it to be one of the top threats to U.S. security.

The goal for the next Congress and, eventually, Trump’s successor, should be to prioritize steady competence over performative toughness in dealing with China. Political point scoring and wistful nostalgia for strategies that discount Beijing’s mounting power, and growing domestic support in the United States for great-power calm, would compound Trump’s mistakes rather than mitigate them. A smarter, more coherent China policy—one rooted in disciplined competition with Beijing, patient efforts to build goodwill with anxious allies and partners, and vigorous efforts to refresh the domestic sources of U.S. strength—can make life safer, healthier, and more affordable for Americans.

Trump’s views on China are iconoclastic in Washington, and even at odds with other members of his own administration and party. Whereas most policymakers agree that China is the United States’ foremost strategic competitor, Trump has embraced a narrow conception of competition focused primarily on the size of China’s trade surplus and its export of chemicals used to make fentanyl. He also sees Xi less as a determined geopolitical foe than as a transactional business rival with whom he can negotiate to stabilize bilateral ties.

This judgment—that dealing with China can and should be nonideological and transactional—was also present in Trump’s first term in office. It even survived the COVID-19 pandemic, during which Trump, believing that Xi had exported the virus, empowered senior advisers to implement a hard-line approach to China that included challenging the legitimacy of Chinese Communist Party rule and openly calling for political change. But Trump’s core views did not dictate U.S. policy as greatly then as they do now because he had less command over his own administration during his first term and was less dominant over the Republican Party as a whole.

Trump has also been chastened into a more realistic appreciation of Beijing’s power. China is the only country in the world that stared down Trump’s April 2025 tariff barrage and forced him to retreat. In 2026, Beijing used Washington’s fear of a fresh economic rupture to deter Trump from targeting major Chinese banks as part of his global efforts to squeeze Iran’s economy. Even as the president claims to be ushering in a new era of U.S. dominance, he has had to acknowledge China as a “near peer”—a designation that his administration codified in its 2025 National Security Strategy. Similarly, he has touted the prospect of a “G-2” of the United States and China, making allies and partners worry that he is privileging Beijing’s sensitivities over their concerns.

In some discrete areas, the Trump administration continues to view China through a competitive lens. For example, it is reducing U.S. dependence on Chinese rare-earth exports through initiatives such as Project Vault, an effort to stockpile critical minerals, and pressuring countries to forswear participation in Chinese-led artificial intelligence initiatives. And even as Trump lambastes allies and partners, alleging that they exploit U.S. beneficence, Washington is deepening its security cooperation with treaty allies in Asia, including Australia and the Philippines, and stepping up its training of troops in Taiwan.

Yet on balance, the president has been more conciliatory than competitive toward China. And with his other foreign policy goals—dominating the Western Hemisphere, pushing for regime change in Iran, and ending the war in Ukraine—vying for priority, Trump has devoted far less attention to the world’s most consequential bilateral relationship than many observers had expected when he retook office. His various retreats on China have left the United States worse off: he has undermined Washington’s leverage over Beijing while causing U.S. allies and partners to question whether they should align with a country that prefers a veneer of stability with China to coordinated efforts to address Chinese assertiveness abroad and repression at home.

Because Trump’s China policy places him at odds with much of the foreign policy bureaucracy and the vast majority of lawmakers, including those within his own party, there will likely be pent-up pressure to resume intense strategic competition with China. Candidates seeking the 2028 presidential nomination, in particular, will be tempted to pledge to take a tougher approach to Beijing, potentially including calls to curtail high-level diplomatic exchanges between U.S. and Chinese officials or adopt more expansive measures to limit China’s technological progress. But attempting to restore or make minor updates to the status quo that existed before Trump’s second term—or to go even further in an effort to project ostensible strength—would be misguided.

Most obvious, the nearly decadelong U.S. effort to slow Chinese innovation through sanctions, tariffs, and export controls is foundering. In AI, for example, Chinese companies are finding workarounds to such efforts and advancing their capabilities alongside those of their U.S. peers. Any sustainable attempt to limit technology access also requires multilateral cooperation; as Washington’s ties with many of its closest allies and partners fray, questions are mounting about whether any coalition can thwart Beijing’s technological strides and would be willing to bear the costs of trying to do so.

At the same time, China is widening its lead in sectors such as electric vehicles, advanced robots, and life sciences. Attempting to construct a technological moat around the United States is unlikely to help it regain its competitiveness. Instead, Washington will need to strike a better balance between mitigating the security risks of Chinese investment and collaboration and securing commercial benefits from Chinese knowledge and industry. The United States could gain from using the same technology transfer practices that China employed against U.S. companies starting in the late 1970s, when Washington led in many cutting-edge technologies. In battery manufacturing, for instance, U.S. firms would benefit from acquiring know-how from their Chinese competitors, and both countries would gain if their laboratories pooled capabilities to accelerate the discovery of cures to diseases such as cancer.

The United States also may not be able to pick up where it left off before Trump retook office because even its well-wishers no longer can treat his foreign policy as an aberration. He won a second term after losing in 2020, and he did so more decisively than in 2016. Although U.S. allies and partners would welcome a more multilateralist administration after Trump, they will find it difficult to overlook the possibility that aggressive unilateralism will periodically shape Washington’s foreign policy. Merely eschewing Trump’s crude bullying will not be enough to rebuild trust; his successor must avoid treating allies and partners as instruments of strategic competition.

Paradoxically, though, Trump’s mishandling of China policy has opened the political space for a new, more durable strategy to emerge. Previously, proponents of sustained high-level diplomacy between the United States and China were often dismissed as shortsighted, Chinese investment in the United States was seen as a Trojan horse, and critiques of zero-sum conceptions of bilateral relations were considered naive. But Trump’s actions have shifted the Overton window of what is possible, creating room for a wider variety of ways to work productively with China.

Both Republican and Democratic members of Congress have gradually resumed bipartisan visits to China. Even Secretary of State Marco Rubio, one of the foremost hawks during his time in the Senate, said in September that it would be “outrageous” for top U.S. and Chinese officials to avoid each other. Respected voices in Washington, including former senior Biden administration officials, are calling for some joint ventures with Chinese companies to help U.S. companies develop. And there is a growing recognition in both Congress and the White House that China’s steady accrual of power necessitates a more focused approach to competition.

There is also public support for a new China policy. Although many policymakers frame relations with Beijing as a competition to be won, most Americans regard it as a reality to be managed. In a July 2025 survey by the Chicago Council on Global Affairs, 53 percent of Americans wanted the United States to pursue “friendly cooperation and engagement with China”; it was the first time since 2019 that a majority of respondents had expressed that view. Young people are driving this shift. A January 2026 Pew poll found that only 32 percent of Republicans and ten percent of Democrats between the ages of 18 and 49 called China an enemy, compared with 55 percent and 18 percent, respectively, of those age 50 and older. A March 2026 survey by the Searchlight Institute, a center-left think tank, found that 62 percent of respondents between 18 and 34 wanted the United States to “cooperate with China and improve U.S.-China relations.”

A better China policy will begin by abandoning hollow admonitions to “win” the strategic competition with Beijing. Although China faces numerous internal challenges, including slowing productivity, an aging population, and mounting government debt, it also has powerful advantages, two of which have become undeniable in Trump’s second term: China’s dominance of supply chains that provide vital commodities to Americans and its stranglehold on rare earths that the U.S. automotive industry and defense industrial base need to function.

The next president should neither understate nor exaggerate Beijing’s trajectory; rather, as we have previously argued, Washington should consider it “an enduring yet constrained competitor.” The inescapable reality is that neither country can deliver any knockout blows against the other without doing grievous damage to itself. Both countries will remain interdependent and have to operate with awareness of their vulnerabilities to each other.

If the world’s two foremost powers are fated to compete over the terms of coexistence, they must redouble efforts to avoid a catastrophic confrontation. Washington will have to build trust with rightfully wary allies and partners if it is to shore up deterrence in Asia. It will also have to continue signaling to Beijing a willingness to take reciprocal steps to reduce the risk of incidents that could spiral out of control, such as accidental air or naval collisions. Such signaling will become ever more essential as AI rapidly advances. Protecting the U.S. homeland against AI misuse and misalignment will require Washington to coordinate with Beijing on guardrails for frontier models and reject the argument that such efforts are inimical to securing a decisive strategic advantage over China. The need to work together on AI safety is already shaping U.S.-Chinese relations: at September’s summit between Trump and Xi, the two countries agreed to establish an incident alert system, and many members of Congress have publicly supported broader bilateral efforts with China to reduce the risks of AI-enabled security crises.

Reindustrialization will also be a major project for Trump’s successor. Progress on that front will be essential if the United States is to reduce its vulnerabilities to China and put itself on firmer footing to pursue a favorable coexistence. The next Congress and potential 2028 candidates could outline realistic plans for reducing dependence on China for active pharmaceutical ingredients, rare earths, and potential future chokepoints. They could also prioritize key industries on which U.S. security depends, such as AI-powered robotics and shipbuilding, and explain how they would deploy funding and talent to those sectors. Elsewhere, with public support for the president’s tariffs sagging, his successor could narrow or place sunset provisions on—or, through executive order, rescind—protectionist policies toward China that lower productivity and increase the costs of inputs for American producers. Such efforts would be broadly politically popular at home and would better position the United States to work with allies and partners to push back on China’s unfair economic practices, including extensive subsidies, undervalued currency, and suppressed domestic demand.

Ultimately, the United States must remember that its external competitiveness will flow from its internal vigor. Leaders in Washington should accordingly embed policy positions on China in a hopeful vision: helping the United States realize its potential as a pluralistic, self-correcting democracy. Framing U.S.-Chinese relations around the United States’ capacity to steer its ties with China in productive directions is more likely to produce a resilient approach than casting Washington as a victim or reasserting a policy framework that no longer meets the moment.

Observers can and should debate specific policy positions vigorously. But another harsh swing in the United States’ approach to China would create more policy instability while also discounting the extent to which Beijing’s rise is transforming the geopolitical balance. Taking advantage of the political space that Trump has generated, however ineptly, the new Congress taking office after the 2026 midterms and presidential aspirants looking to run in 2028 will have an opportunity to present a steadier approach, articulating a clear focus on national renewal and a balanced posture toward Beijing that reflects the mood of the American public. Approaching China’s challenge with this sensibility would not only be more prescriptively sound; it would also be more politically sustainable.

How America Can Take Advantage of the Status Quo

Why the United States Should Not Overreact to China and Russia

Beijing’s Green Path to Ending Dollar Dominance

How the Search for Economic Security Will End in Geopolitical Calamity

Trump Could Salvage the Kremlin’s Objectives in Ukraine

#6008. Russia the Disruptor

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: Russia the Disruptor

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

Understanding the conflict four years on.

In a famous scene from Band of Brothers, the HBO epic about a U.S. paratrooper regiment in World War II, a Nazi general surrenders to U.S. forces. The Americans, rather than humiliating him, allow him to deliver a stirring address to his troops after they lay down their arms; the soldiers from both camps are visibly moved.

That incident may be exaggerated or apocryphal, but it echoed a widely held sentiment among the Allies in 1945: “In victory, magnanimity,” as Winston Churchill once summarized. But over four decades later, as the Berlin Wall fell and the Soviet Union collapsed, magnanimity was harder to come by: How much, if anything, was the United States willing to offer Russia in the wake of the Cold War?

In a famous scene from Band of Brothers, the HBO epic about a U.S. paratrooper regiment in World War II, a Nazi general surrenders to U.S. forces. The Americans, rather than humiliating him, allow him to deliver a stirring address to his troops after they lay down their arms; the soldiers from both camps are visibly moved.

That incident may be exaggerated or apocryphal, but it echoed a widely held sentiment among the Allies in 1945: “In victory, magnanimity,” as Winston Churchill once summarized. But over four decades later, as the Berlin Wall fell and the Soviet Union collapsed, magnanimity was harder to come by: How much, if anything, was the United States willing to offer Russia in the wake of the Cold War?

That question is at the heart of two books, Hubris: The American Origins of Russia’s War Against Ukraine by British historian Jonathan Haslam and Boiling Point: How America Brought War to Ukraine by journalist Stephen Kinzer. Both authors question to what extent Russia’s war in Ukraine is a reaction to decades of arrogance by the United States, full of swagger as the last superpower standing amid the ashes of the Cold War.

Hubris: The American Origins of Russia’s War Against Ukraine, Jonathan Haslam, Belknap Press, 368 pp., $25.95, September 2026, paperback

Both accounts, though overeager to blame Washington for Moscow’s belligerent turn, serve as a helpful reminder of the paths not taken in the 1990s and early 2000s, when, rather than bringing Russia into the liberal world order as a true partner, the United States encouraged rapid NATO expansion and democratization in Eastern and Central Europe, antagonizing the country.

If those decisions partly drove Moscow to move toward a break with the West, a third book, We Shall Outlast Them: Putin’s Global Campaign to Defeat the West by international relations scholar Hanna Notte, documents the culmination of Russia’s effort, showing how, since its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022, the country has leveraged ties with allies around the globe to evade sanctions and political pressure. Alienated from the West, Russia has turned to the rest of the world. The question now is whether the West, thrown into even greater chaos by U.S. President Donald Trump’s fractious impulses, will be able to adapt.

In February 1990, soon after the fall of the Berlin Wall, Soviet leader Mikhail Gorbachev met with Western diplomats over the question of German reunification. In a phrase that has come to haunt much of post-Soviet history, U.S. Secretary of State James Baker told Gorbachev that NATO would move “not one inch” eastward if the Soviet Union withdrew from East Germany.

The flags of NATO nations are pictured during the inauguration ceremony of NATO’s Supreme Headquarters Allied Powers Europe near Paris on July 23, 1951. AFP via Getty Images

While much ink has been spilled about the meaning of that phrase—and Baker’s authorization to issue such assurances in the first place—this anticipated a pattern that would plague the U.S.-Russia relationship for 30 years. At each stage, the expansion of NATO’s membership and mission, partly at the behest of Central and Eastern European nations understandably nervous about a resurgent Russia, undermined Moscow’s perception of its own security. Ukraine would become the ultimate battleground between the forces of democratization and NATO enlargement on the one hand, and the security interests of an increasingly autocratic Russia on the other.

In the early 1990s, NATO was at a crossroads. Its raison d’être had dissolved along with the Soviet-dominated Warsaw Pact, created as a counterweight to the alliance. Haslam convincingly argues that where Western Europeans would have accepted the emergence of a post-Cold War security alliance accommodating Moscow, Washington doubled down on the status quo, which—to paraphrase NATO’s first secretary-general—meant keeping the Russians out, the Americans in, and the Germans down.

How would NATO adapt to this unipolar moment? The answer came when the Czech Republic, Poland, and Hungary were welcomed into the club in 1999, not without significant Russian complaints. The United States ignored these grumblings, pushing for the swift admission of seven more countries in a “big bang” of expansion in 2004. NATO had just 12 members at its founding in 1949; it now has 32.

Membership was not the only thing NATO was set on expanding: It also sought a new animating impulse. Although NATO was originally framed as a defensive pact, the wars in Yugoslavia in the 1990s allowed more hawkish elements of the alliance to push for its use as an offensive force. Russia, seeing phantoms of its former self in the disintegrating Yugoslav state, vocally opposed NATO’s involvement in the Balkans, culminating in a complete break with the alliance over its unilateral bombing of Serbia in 1999.

Moscow’s inability to dissuade NATO put the Yeltsin administration in the crosshairs of nationalists on the rise at home just as the Russian economy began its free fall after the 1998 stock market crash. Both Haslam and Kinzer point to many missed opportunities in which Western powers could have bolstered Russia’s economy in its darkest hour. Instead of something akin to the Marshall Plan for Europe after World War II, Russia had to take on massive International Monetary Fund loans in exchange for painful, deeply unpopular economic reforms.

Russian navy sailors hold their passports as they line up to vote at a polling station in the Ukrainian city of Sevastopol, where the main base of Russia’s Black Sea Fleet is located, on Dec. 2, 2007. Sergei Supinsky/AFP via Getty Images

Reconciliation was still feasible in the early 2000s, when Vladimir Putin became Russia’s president. In his early years in office, Putin voiced some openness to the West. Haslam details how Putin overrode members of his security apparatus to offer significant intelligence assistance to the United States after 9/11 and even approved the opening of NATO bases in Central Asia, considered Russia’s backyard, to facilitate the U.S. war in Afghanistan. Haslam argues that Washington snubbed Putin’s generosity: “Putin’s grand gesture … was effectively wasted,” he writes. The White House read it as “a desperate bid for a powerful ally against a common foe — rather than a welcome concession from a former adversary and a potential partner.”

It’s in everyone’s interest to stop the fighting. That’s still not enough.

Like a struggling swimmer, he may take desperate measures to stay afloat.

The U.S. push for regime change in Iraq, a longtime ally of Russia—and its subsequent support for revolutions in Georgia in 2003 and Ukraine in 2004—further alienated Putin. While his country could not rival the United States economically, he had other ways to put Russia back on the map. “[D]eclining powers can be at least as disruptive as rising powers,” Haslam writes, quoting William Burns, the former U.S. ambassador to Russia. “If [Putin] can’t easily build Russia up, he can take the United States down a few pegs.” Numerous foreign theaters, from Syria to Ukraine, would give him ample opportunity to try.

Boiling Point: How America Brought War to Ukraine, Stephen Kinzer, Henry Holt and Co., 384 pp., $32.99, November 2026

Both Haslam and Kinzer reach similar conclusions: that the arrogance of several U.S. presidencies, puffed up with victory in the wake of the Cold War, left them heedless to Russia’s security concerns in Eastern Europe. Neither reckons fully with Ukrainian agency, particularly with the fact that since independence in 1991, leaders of Ukraine have, of their own accord, pushed back against Russian attempts to control the country. Nor do they discuss at any length Moscow’s proactive role in fomenting trouble in its near abroad, whether through the distribution of Russian passports in breakaway parts of Georgia or by choking off energy supplies to Ukraine.

While Haslam’s approach is meticulous and relatively balanced, Kinzer, whose books have often been critical of interventionist U.S. foreign policy, clearly has an axe to grind when it comes to Ukraine. Kinzer commits to pro-Kremlin narratives and conveniently sidesteps facts that contradict them. He sees neo-Nazis everywhere in Ukraine, takes claims about the threat to Russian language there at face value, and treats the separatists in eastern Ukraine who began fighting the central government in 2014 as an autonomous force, rather than entities that were fully funded, armed, and backed by Russia from early on.

Yet both authors, in their rush to assign blame, fail to grapple with the reality that the Soviet collapse created a security vacuum in Europe. It is to some extent inevitable that Russia would feel unease at the loss of its republics, and that any moves by former allies to bolster their own security would provoke anxiety in Moscow. It is to some extent inevitable, too, that as Russia became increasingly autocratic, it would perceive any democratization nearby, including in Ukraine, as not just a domestic affair, but one with existential stakes for the Kremlin.

Of course, the war in Ukraine was not inevitable. Putin’s growing isolation, paranoia, and authoritarianism undoubtedly made a military ending to the Russia-Ukraine divorce more likely. And the hubris of the U.S. elite in the wake of the Cold War—and the whiplash created by U.S. electoral politics—meant that warning signs blinking red in the 1990s and early 2000s went unheeded, paving the way for policy that antagonized Moscow further. Many decisions could have been made differently, but even with the best of intentions, it would have been hard to resolve the fissures that culminated in the alienation of Russia from the West.

Putin addresses Western sanctions during a meeting on economic issues at the Kremlin in Moscow on Feb. 28, 2022, following Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine. Alexey Nikolsky/Sputnik/AFP via Getty Images

In the nearly five years since Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine, Moscow has been able to pivot economically and militarily away from the West, as Notte shows in We Shall Outlast Them. With lucid and zippy writing, Notte takes us from the ports of India to the deserts of the Sahel, revealing how Russia has survived in the face of coordinated and extensive sanctions by Europe and the United States.

We Shall Outlast Them: Putin’s Global Campaign to Defeat the West, Hanna Notte, W.W. Norton & Co., 320 pp., $31.99, August 2026

Russia was still highly dependent on the Western economy as of 2022. The EU bought 50 percent of Russian oil exports and over 60 percent of its gas exports, and Western companies provided the vast majority of financing and insurance for energy shipping markets. Russia also imported one-third of its goods from the EU. Unlike the slow and uncoordinated response to the annexation of Crimea in 2014, the West moved swiftly and cohesively after the 2022 invasion, imposing price caps on Russian oil insured or shipped by Western providers and banning exports of electronics, luxury items, and dual-use goods in an effort to choke supplies.

Russia has artfully evaded these measures. In oil and gas, Russia built up a “shadow fleet” of off-the-book tankards to transport oil and evade the price cap, backed by insurers or financiers in Dubai and beyond. Russian oil exports to China and Turkey have risen, and in India alone, Russia’s share of the country’s oil imports increased from just 3 percent in 2021 to 38 percent in 2023.

For imported goods, Putin essentially established duty-free trade from third countries, allowing many European and U.S. products to enter via countries such as Turkey and Kazakhstan. Russia has pivoted towards Chinese exports and financing, decoupling its economy from the U.S. dollar (while making it significantly more dependent on the yuan). The Russian economy has certainly felt the war’s impact, with its GDP taking an initial hit before stabilizing somewhat in the years since. But it has not collapsed.

Notte also shows how Russia has navigated international diplomacy, tapping into anti-imperialist sentiment in much of the global south to position itself as the plucky outsider standing up to a hegemonic West, despite its own history of colonialism. This has paid dividends at the United Nations and beyond, where many Asian and African countries have stood with Russia in defiance of U.S. and European pressure. Where the West has sought to cut off military supplies, Russia has bolstered its alliances with North Korea and Iran, which have become major providers of munitions and drone technology. It has expanded its military footprint abroad including in the Sahel, leveraging its extensive network of mercenary groups.

A truck driver and parked trucks are reflected in a puddle outside the Mangalore Refinery and Petrochemicals Ltd. parking terminal in Mangaluru, India, on Sept. 5, 2025. Abhishek Chinnappa/Getty Images

Of course, there are limits to Russia’s influence abroad when it is so consumed by the war in Ukraine. In Syria, Russia could do little but watch as longtime ally President Bashar al-Assad was forced from power in 2024. Closer to home, in Armenia and Kazakhstan, Russia’s distraction militarily and economically forced these former satellites to hedge their bets and turn to partners elsewhere. Meanwhile, Moscow shored up its commitment to BRICS as an alternative to Western-dominated institutions such as the G7, but as Notte shows, the bloc’s diverse stakeholders have struggled to unite on global issues.

While Russia has avoided total breakdown, the devastation of the war cannot be understated: Aside from the economic impact, roughly 1 million soldiers are estimated to have been injured or killed, and hundreds of thousands have left the country. Russia’s autocracy has kicked into full gear, with even those voicing the mildest opposition to the war facing consequences.

In many ways, Russia’s willingness to disrupt has taken a toll not just on its own people but on the rest of the world. While many may welcome a system in which the West is no longer able to bend the globe to its will, the end of a unipolar world has so far heralded the rise of a new order that appears more fragmented, unstable, and violent. Putin has had plenty of help in this regard: Trump has done his fair share to undermine the previous status quo with his reckless gamble in Iran, his assaults on traditional allies in Europe, and his stunning betrayal of Ukraine amid the so-called diesel deal with Moscow.

Veterans of the Russian special forces lay flowers at a monument to Russian service members as they mark their professional day in St. Petersburg, Russia, on Oct. 24, 2025. Olga Maltseva/AFP via Getty Images

Soviet leader Nikita Khruschev once famously quipped about capitalist states, “It doesn’t depend on you whether or not we exist. … History is on our side. We will bury you!” Putin has not buried the West, but he has shown how far he will go to undermine it. Putin’s regime, and Putin himself, may or may not outlive the war; as Haslam poignantly notes in closing his book, “[W]e will all sooner or later have to come to terms with a new [Russian] regime, democracy or dictatorship.” What remains to be seen is whether the collective West, whoever that may encompass by then, will have learned anything from recent history.

#6005. DOJ set to appeal ruling in Alaska Senate race dispute over ‘Decoy Dan’

Fox News' Matt Finn reports from Anchorage, Alaska with updates on the second Dan Sullivan appearing on voter ballots. The DOJ has asked a federal judge to halt the certification of Democrat Mary Peltola if she wins. Peltola has denied being involved in the alleged 'Sullivan scheme.'

A federal judge on Sunday dismissed the Justice Department’s lawsuit seeking to delay certification of Alaska’s Senate election amid the "Decoy Dan" controversy, rejecting the Trump administration’s bid two days before the midterm elections.

The lawsuit centered on two candidates with similar names: Republican Sen. Dan S. Sullivan and challenger Daniel J. Sullivan Jr. The DOJ lawsuit sought to delay or block certification while investigating whether voter confusion may have affected the outcome.

DEM BUCKS PARTY OVER ALLEGED ALASKA VOTE-SIPHONING EFFORT: 'NEITHER PARTY SHOULD BE DOING IT'

President Donald Trump shakes hands with U.S. Senator Dan Sullivan, R-Alaska, as he invited him to speak during a campaign rally at Curtis D. Menard Memorial Sports Center in Wasilla, Alaska, on Oct. 13, 2026. (Kent Nishimura/ AFP via Getty Images)

U.S. District Judge Sharon Gleason said the government had not shown how the requested investigation could identify voters’ intent while protecting ballot secrecy. She also said the relief sought would intrude on Alaska’s responsibility to run its elections.

The DOJ said it would appeal, arguing the judge dismissed the case on procedural grounds without addressing its claim that Sullivan Jr. entered the race to confuse voters. The case now heads to the 9th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals.

The dispute has roiled the campaign in recent days. The New York Times reported that people connected to Democratic nominee Mary Peltola’s campaign helped recruit Sullivan Jr. to run as a spoiler. Peltola has denied involvement, and Sullivan Jr. has denied coordinating with her campaign.

Sen. Sullivan said Sunday he would accept the results of Tuesday’s election, even as the Trump administration pursues a criminal investigation into Sullivan Jr.’s candidacy. Sullivan accused the challenger of trying to confuse voters.

GOVERNOR PROBING 'DECOY DAN' SULLIVAN ELECTION SCANDAL WARNS SYSTEM FLAW HIDING IN PLAIN SIGHT

Alaska Gov. Mike Dunleavy speaks during a proclamation signing ceremony in the Oval Office of the White House on June 11, 2026, in Washington, D.C. (Alex Wong/Getty Images)

Alaska Gov. Mike Dunleavy, a Republican, has suggested he may not certify a Peltola victory. Sullivan declined to say whether he would support efforts to refuse certification.

The Senate race could help determine which party controls the chamber. Alaska uses ranked-choice voting, under which lower-performing candidates are eliminated in rounds if no one wins a majority in the first count.

A DOJ sign on the edifice of the building. (Kevin Carter/Getty Images)

The closely watched race could help determine which party controls the Senate.

Alaska uses ranked-choice voting, so if no candidate wins a majority in the first round, the last-place candidate is eliminated and voters’ next choices are counted.

#6003. North Korea’s Enemies Within

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

How Long Will Elites Stay Loyal to Kim Jong Un?

In 2015, the North Korean diplomat Thae Yong Ho received an unusual assignment. Kim Jong Chol, the older brother of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, was coming to London to attend an Eric Clapton concert, and Thae, who was then stationed in London, was assigned to look after him. Providing direct service to the ruling family confers honor on North Korean officials and their households, so Thae expected his two sons to be impressed.

To Thae’s surprise, his sons were outraged. Ordinary North Koreans could be expelled from university (or worse) for listening to Western music, they told him. And now the Kim family was spending thousands of dollars in a single evening to enjoy entertainment forbidden to all other North Koreans. In the eyes of his children, Thae later wrote in his memoir, Password to the Third Floor, he was a prisoner forced to take care of a prison warden. Their reaction left him ashamed.

The next year, Thae, his wife, and their sons escaped to South Korea. His defection surprised the international community. Only weeks earlier, Thae had been defending the North Korean regime with great fervor. Yet this incongruous behavior is familiar to those who understand life among North Korea’s elite: officials can profess full devotion to the Kim family right until the day they run.

The gap between public conduct and private belief is difficult to observe from the outside. For over a decade, I interviewed dozens of former diplomats, Korean Workers’ Party cadres, military officers, businesspeople, and technicians, all of whom had lived, studied, or worked in Pyongyang. These people ran the party, military, embassies, foreign-currency businesses, and technical programs on which the state depends for its survival. They received the best education, housing, food, and access to influential social networks their country could provide. Some once believed sincerely in the system; others learned early that loyalty was a role they had to pantomime in a nationwide, lifelong performance—now with Kim Jong Un as the maestro. Nearly all described an elite class under growing strain.

Since Kim Jong Un came to power in December 2011, he has tightened his grip on the country’s elites by carrying out sweeping purges of the old guard of senior party officials and military officers and executing others for minor political missteps. Many members of North Korea’s elite fear that they or their families can be killed at the drop of a hat. And their escape routes are narrowing; since Kim sealed off the borders during the COVID-19 pandemic, the number of North Korean defectors reaching South Korea has plummeted.

Those North Koreans who still manage to escape increasingly come from the elite. As of August 2024, South Korea’s National Intelligence Service reported that the number of defections from North Korea’s upper class recorded under Kim Jong Un was two and a half times the combined total observed during the reigns of Kim Il Sung (1948 to 1994) and Kim Jong Il (1994 to 2011). The absolute number remains small—in the hundreds—and it would be a mistake to interpret this trend as a harbinger of imminent regime collapse. North Korea is remarkably effective at preventing private grievances from becoming organized opposition. The regime does not need every official to believe in it. But it needs its doubters to feel isolated and alone in their skepticism and disillusionment.

North Korea has long been referred to as the “Hermit Kingdom,” but that term offers the wrong impression of the country. It is not totally isolated from the world. It is a nuclear-armed state with diplomatic relations with over 160 countries, and it has the world’s fourth-largest standing army and highly skilled cyber-operators. The vast majority of the population may be fed a robust diet of state propaganda, but most elites are exposed to life outside North Korea. They have a reasonable sense of how the world works and their country’s place in it. And yet they have to live with intense cognitive dissonance, at once understanding the real world and having to create, enforce, and trumpet the state’s official narratives to their countrymen and foreigners.

Many high-ranking North Koreans are finding it harder to sustain this balancing act. Access to privilege can purchase compliance—and terror can enforce it—but the state will not give elites what they increasingly want: guaranteed security for them and their families and a viable future for their children. Kim can allay some frustrations by doling out luxury apartments, promotions, and access to extravagant lifestyles. He cannot address the deeper sources of discontent without relaxing the very controls that protect his family’s rule from challenges to its power. The regime remains capable of suppressing any organized resistance in the country, but the growing disaffection of the elite threatens to weaken the state from within. Kim presides over a system increasingly marked by doubt, suspicion, and pervasive anxiety. When the chips are down and North Korea faces a true crisis, its most powerful leaders may not know who they can trust and where they can turn.

Most of the more than 34,000 North Koreans now living in South Korea came from the poor northern provinces along the Chinese border. Many of them fled to the south to escape famine and economic hardship. They had little access to the regime’s inner workings, even if they had tremendous experience of its cruelty and depredations. In contrast, elite escapees come from a wholly different world. They are products of Pyongyang, a capital of roughly three million that is closed to most of the country’s 25 million citizens. They grew up with relatively reliable electricity and running water, and attended schools designed to educate the next generation of regime operators. They benefited from the new construction of luxury residential districts, extravagant resort towns such as Samjiyon and Wonsan-Kalma, and the country’s sustained economic growth in the face of heavy international sanctions. North Korea’s elite serve in a range of occupations beyond just the party cadre ranks, the military, and the security services; they can be artists, software engineers, restaurant managers, and much else. The regime groomed them from birth to serve the party and the Kim family.

Consider the family of Ri Jong Ho and his son Hyunseung Lee. Ri spent three decades in Office 39, which earns and manages foreign currency for the leadership. Kim Jong Il appointed him to senior trade and economic posts, and in 2002 the state awarded him a Hero of Labor medal and a Kim Il Sung watch, prestigious honors and a recognition of his loyalty and patriotism. Ri’s son attended some of the country’s finest educational institutions, including Kumsong Academy and Pyongyang Foreign Language Institute, which prepare the children of senior cadres to enter high positions in the party. Hyunseung and his sister were also among the rare few North Koreans who were permitted to study abroad in China. By his mid-20s, Hyunseung was already a party member, working in China, and was trusted to chair a branch of the Kim Il Sung Youth League while helping facilitate millions of dollars in trade between Chinese companies and North Korea. This family faithfully served their country while living at the highest echelons of their society and enjoying the best creature comforts. But their privileged positions did not make them safe.

The shocking 2013 execution of Jang Song Thaek, Kim Jong Un’s uncle, unnerved Ri. The slain Jang had been married to Kim Il Sung’s daughter and had served all three Kims. If the regime could eliminate such a decorated official—and a member of the ruling family—and seek to erase him from the country’s history and memory, then no one was untouchable. Ri’s family escaped to South Korea from their posts in China in 2014, and Hyunseung and his sister later earned master’s degrees from Columbia University. Despite the many privileges their family received from the regime, they fled because their proximity to power made them feel deeply vulnerable.

North Koreans live under institutions designed to make independent organization impossible. Party committees operate inside every school, embassy, farm, military unit, office, and factory. Security bureaus obsessively police information flows and political conduct, while neighborhood units monitor every household. Every citizen, starting at a young age, belongs to a state-directed organization. For elites, closeness to power invites suffocating scrutiny; it places them more deeply within the grasp of the state rather than freeing them from the overbearing system.

The Organization and Guidance Department, the party’s most powerful agency, oversees appointments, inspections, and discipline everywhere—in Pyongyang as well as rural villages. No escapee is known to have come from the OGD’s central ranks, an indication of the control imposed on the country’s most influential officials. Diplomats and workers sent abroad must surrender their passports and leave relatives in North Korea as collateral to deter defection. When they come home, they must account for what they saw, heard, and did abroad, enumerate every single political sin they committed, and then undergo extensive ideological deprogramming and reprogramming.

Kim Se Yun (a pseudonym), a former senior party cadre who traveled overseas, described the monthlong central review she underwent every time she returned to Pyongyang. Officials have to “cleanse” themselves of what they have encountered and report even small details, including whether they had gone out drinking with colleagues after dinner. A late night out could require a written piece of self-flagellation to account for a lapse unbecoming of “a devoted worker for the state.” The review was meant to minimize the effects of foreign exposure: a person could see how others lived but, upon returning, had to renounce what she had seen. Kim Se Yun described the constant surveillance when abroad and at home as miserable and utterly asphyxiating.

An elite position brings goods, status, and influence denied to ordinary citizens, but it also places a person’s career, relatives, and life within reach of the leader who can destroy them at any point for any reason. As one common saying in Pyongyang has it, “The closer you are to the sun, the more likely you will burn.”

Nothing impressed this lesson upon the elite class more forcefully than the fall of Jang in 2013. His sudden arrest and execution sent lasting fear through Pyongyang. Every elite I spoke with understood the warning implicit in his killing.

Jang had already undergone “revolutionization” three times. This distinctly North Korean punishment, used especially to discipline and defenestrate high officials, strips a person of office and sends him to perform physical labor and political reeducation, often in the countryside. It humiliates an elite official in front of the people he once commanded, even if it does not foreclose the possibility of his future rehabilitation. Jang returned each time, only to be executed in the end by the third leader he served. Heo Kang Il, an elite defector who managed a North Korean restaurant in China and in 2016 brought 12 of his staff with him to South Korea, knew people subjected to revolutionization and was subjected to it once himself. He told me that many never recovered even after being rehabilitated. Some developed psychological illnesses or died within a few years from physical ailments.

When Kim Jong Un became the country’s leader in December 2011, he was in his late 20s (his exact birth year is unknown). He aggressively imposed his writ by carrying out purges of party senior cadres and military officials who had served under his two predecessors. Outside observers described his vicious consolidation of power as an attempt to compensate for his youth, his limited political experience, and his complete lack of a military background. When the COVID-19 pandemic hit, Kim Jong Un reinforced the country’s already hard borders to eliminate any unauthorized cross-border movement and passed draconian laws that further criminalized consumption of any unauthorized foreign media, all with the aim of “repurifying” the country’s socialist Korean culture. Although the pandemic has passed, the information environment is more controlled than ever before and the borders remain sealed.

In North Korea, loyalty is evaluated and documented from childhood. A person’s conduct at school; performance in political organizations; and songbun status (a hereditary classification of loyalty to the regime that breaks up North Koreans into three broad groups: “core,” “wavering,” and “hostile”), determine admission to universities, party membership, and promotion. Adults attend regular self-criticism sessions. At home, portraits of the Kims must hang spotless at the highest point of the most prominent wall, with nothing placed above them. One woman I interviewed recalled habitation inspectors making regular visits in white gloves to check the portraits for dust. She cleaned the portraits meticulously, not because she revered the men in them but because a speck of dust could endanger her family.

Even a weekly public self-criticism session, which requires a person to publicly share a political transgression they have made and then criticize a colleague, often becomes a coordinated performance. Many elite defectors told me that the day before a self-criticism session, colleagues would warn one another about what they planned to criticize at the next day’s session. The advance notice protected friendships while allowing everyone to satisfy the requirements of the political cleansing ritual. A person might confess to lacking sufficient revolutionary zeal during the previous week and vehemently rebuke a friend for a similar offense. The trick was to disclose transgressions that were sufficiently blameworthy but not serious enough to get oneself or a colleague in too much trouble.

Another former elite described the public mourning after Kim Jong Il’s death in 2011. She felt relief at the leader’s death, and no sorrow, but knew that she had to appear to cry, so she dredged up her saddest memories—those of her grandmother’s death and of a cousin who was forcibly repatriated after a failed defection—to force herself to weep. When tears still would not come, she bowed her head, dramatically shook her shoulders, and avoided eye contact with anyone in the hysterically wailing crowd. She believed that many people around her were giving the same performance.

Scholars refer to this gap as preference falsification: people publicly misrepresent their true beliefs because honesty is dangerous. In Pyongyang, preference falsification is an essential part of ordinary daily life. Elites constantly calibrate their speech and compartmentalize their innermost private thoughts. Some still believe in the system or feel genuine national pride. Others resent Kim while valuing the status his regime gives them. Belief and cynicism can exist in the same office and within the same person. This is how a senior diplomat can lecture British bureaucrats about North Korea’s victimhood one day and then flee North Korea with his entire family a few weeks later.

When elites do leave, their departures remind others who harbor private doubts that they are not alone. This is one reason Pyongyang treats prominent defections as security emergencies. When Heo (the restaurant manager) and 12 workers left a North Korean restaurant in China in 2016, the state recalled all other workers in China for interrogation. In his memoir, Thae analogized this group defection to stones falling from a castle wall: small losses can eventually lead to the collapse of the entire castle.

Nobody lightly gives up their lofty place in the pecking order of North Korean society. Kim Jong Un has not neglected his ruling class. His government has built gleaming new housing in Pyongyang for scientists, party cadres, and other favored groups, and it distributes foreign cars, watches, jewelry, food, and apartments. An elite position also provides something less tangible: a strong sense of purpose and relevance. A bureaucrat, as powerless as he may be, matters in North Korean society. People appreciate his official title, defer to his family, and recognize the heft of his social and political connections.

The prospect of losing that status can make the thought of escape unpalatable even for people who detest the regime. Over dinner at a Seoul barbecue restaurant buzzing with customers eating sizzling meat and drinking somaek, a mixture of beer and soju, a former official I call Mr. Kim recalled an incident elsewhere in which he had asked a table of loud college students to quiet down. Decades younger than him, they barely looked at him—they “didn’t even put their drinks down.” One student told him to eat somewhere else. As he recalled the humiliation, Mr. Kim removed his glasses and rubbed his eyes. In North Korea, he told me, a restaurant would have gone silent when he entered. He would have walked with his shoulders straight and head held high, knowing that his family name and job carried weight. In South Korea, where he worked a warehouse job, he was “just a mere North Korean defector with a suspicious accent.”

Mr. Kim’s experience of losing status and purpose after defection helps explain why the regime’s mixture of privilege and coercion still works. Elites know they have a lot to risk and lose, so for most aggrieved elites, the rational option is to remain in the system, perform loyalty for the rest of their lives, and extract all the advantages their positions still offer.

Yet this bargain has an expiration date. After retirement, senior officers and cadres do not receive pensions. The government takes back state-provided cars, homes, and appliances. After serving the state for decades, retired generals and senior cadres are often reduced to working as guards or market vendors. Younger elites watch this happen and accumulate money as much as possible, knowing that the state that demands their whole lives will not care for them in their old age.

Younger and middle-aged elites are also less inclined to put up with the careers and sacrifices the state has historically presented as honorable. Many interviewees told me that elite parents no longer want their sons to spend over a decade conscripted in the army or build careers entirely inside the party. They prefer business, technical work, study abroad, and amassing private wealth. They want access to the outside world and would welcome greater economic liberalization at home, even if they do not expect full democracy or the reunification of the peninsula in the near future. Above all, they want their children to have lives less constrained than their own.

Money has altered the old order in what Kim Jong Un calls a “socialist paradise.” During one interview, an escapee told me that he knew who would succeed Kim Jong Un to become North Korea’s fourth ruler. I expected him to name Kim Jong Un’s daughter or sister. “Money,” he said. He was being cheeky, but only partly. He pulled out his phone and read the biblical warning that no one can serve two masters, God and money; in his analogy, Kim occupied the place of God. North Koreans jostle for food, better education, and influence, and search for ways to make money outside state-sanctioned activities by paying bribes and using black markets. Younger elites increasingly judge a good marriage, job, and future career path in terms of how it can offer them access to cash and useful business connections. A title remains useful, often because it can be converted into income. The collapse of the state’s public distribution system and devastating famine years of the 1990s drove the grassroot marketization of the country, with the development of state-regulated marketplaces, some so large that they’ve hosted over 10,000 vendors. Presently, in this de jure socialist country that officially condemns capitalism, markets and the imperative of earning hard cash shape people’s daily lives.

This change appears in ordinary choices. One former businessman carried a two-dollar bill in his wallet as a good-luck charm to attract prosperity and wealth. Other interviewees remembered a time when families seeking husbands for their daughters preferred sons of party cadres; today, a man with access to foreign currency or a profitable business can be much more desirable. Political rank still opens doors, but money can keep them open.

Kim has accommodated some of these economic changes; once prohibited, markets are now regulated and taxed by the state. Although foreign media and the Internet remain strictly prohibited, the state allows North Koreans to access a highly controlled intranet and use smartphones with local software and applications. It grants apartments to selected families and permits officials to enrich themselves by engaging in business ventures, provided they continue to perform their jobs exquisitely and contribute mandatory “loyalty funds” to the state.

What Kim cannot offer is dependable and lasting safety. Everything remains subordinate to the leader’s needs and whims. An official who follows the law may still be punished for crossing a better-connected family, and a successful cadre may be swept away because his patron was purged.

Growing elite grievances do not mean an uprising is near. A disaffected elite has five options: remain silent and maintain the status quo; flee the country; build parallel networks of money and influence; protest; or push for incremental reform. The last three are extraordinarily dangerous in North Korea. The regime will extinguish dissent, bids for reform, and any networks that strive to operate or even seem to operate beyond the state’s control. For most, the choice then narrows to either carrying one’s grievances to the grave or mustering the courage and resolve to defect.

Most people opt to stay. Through imposing guilt by association and collective punishments, the North Korean state has spent decades attenuating the relationships through which private anger might be converted into collective action. Its surveillance system encourages colleagues, neighbors, and even relatives to watch and report one another. Unapproved associations and even impromptu sports matches at schools that bring together more than a few people are forbidden unless they are specially approved, and the state tightly controls information about purges and acts of protest to prevent the spread of dissension. In some border cities, such as Sinuiju, the party has recently banned any unapproved gatherings of three or more people.

When I discussed the obstacles to collective action with Lee Chul Eun, a former high-ranking official in the Ministry of State Security who defected in 2016 by swimming six kilometers to South Korea, he described the state’s security apparatus as a spiderweb of security agents, surveillance offices, military commands, and party bodies. A person would have to be “absolutely insane” to challenge such a system, he told me. Another former official summarized the social knowledge produced by that web: “We [elites] know everything but just don’t express it.” Elites can recognize propaganda, corruption, and private anger around them while not knowing whom they can fully trust to confide in.

The regime has other sources of durability. Trade with China, weapons sales, cybertheft from foreign banks and companies, and sending North Korean laborers abroad all help fund the regime’s operations, including its nuclear weapons program, security services, national network of political prison camps, and luxuries necessary for managing elites. The regime has survived famine, international sanctions, and leadership successions. Discontent has been building for years without ever transforming into any form of sustained, organized opposition.

This helps explain another surprising finding from my interviews and research. Many elites understand that the official story is false. They know that colleagues complain in private, seek bribes, watch foreign media, and concoct safe ways for their children to remain and work abroad. What they cannot safely determine is who would follow and stand with them if they tried to protest and push for reform. First movers are the most vulnerable to state repression, while everyone else can deny sharing the same views. North Korea’s political system depends on atomization and making doubters feel isolated from one another. The regime understands that the most plausible organized threat to its hold on the state would be Pyongyang powered, not people powered; a challenge or rebellion is more likely to start among elite insiders than as a mass revolt from below. But for now, elites don’t trust one another and lack the shared vision required to move against the regime.

Defection is the one option an individual can choose without first building a coalition. Elite escapees take with them expertise and signal to peers that leaving is possible. Yet China continues to forcibly repatriate North Koreans who cross its border. Officials posted abroad wishing to defect must dexterously evade state security officials stationed alongside them and come to peace with the fact that loved ones back home will surely be punished.

In August 2025, a North Korean interpreter traveling with a delegation from Pyongyang’s Academy of Social Sciences entered the South Korean embassy in Ulaanbaatar in Mongolia and asked to defect. A year later, the request is still unresolved and the interpreter remains in limbo in the embassy. Mongolia, which maintains relations with Pyongyang, has been slow to cooperate. North Korea objected, and Seoul, currently under a left-leaning administration that seeks warmer relations with the North, will not even confirm that the case existed. For officials considering escape, the lesson is stark: even reaching the embassy of an ostensibly democratic country may not deliver them from the tyranny of home.

Several elite defectors I spoke with had reached the United States and applied for asylum. Their claims have languished for years or been rejected; one claimant has waited more than nine years. They cycle through hearings and background checks with no resolution to their cases. A former North Korean official could certainly pose a counterintelligence risk. But keeping them in this purgatory tells others that the United States may be happy to take their valuable intelligence while still not offering them a future. The vagaries of American asylum policy are not responsible for the persistence of the Kim family regime, but Washington’s reluctance to welcome these defectors could suggest to wavering elites in Pyongyang that it might just be safer to stay put.

Kim Jong Un can continue to manage elite disaffection by rotating, purging, rewarding, and surveilling the very people who keep his regime running on a daily basis. But this approach provides only symptomatic relief to the overarching and insoluble crisis produced by his totalitarianism. To gain the confidence of his elites, he would have to stop using purges and executions, end the collective punishment of families for the transgressions of an individual, close political prison camps, permit freer flows of information, and allow for autonomous market activity without the fear of unpredictable shutdowns and confiscations. Each concession, however, reduces the fear and isolation of elites. To Kim’s detriment, that would only embolden elites to turn against him.

He will likely not relax his grip on the ranks of the powerful. But in so doing, he will weaken his own regime. The costs of preference falsification and performative loyalty will accumulate among the highest echelons of the regime’s operators. That will become most dangerous during a succession struggle or severe economic shock. In ordinary times, each official can continue assuming that all his colleagues will strive to maintain the status quo. But a crisis—such as a military coup, another volcanic eruption of Mount Paektu, or an economic meltdown—can change those assumptions swiftly.

In a moment of upheaval and uncertainty, already disaffected officials may conclude that obedient compliance will no longer protect their positions or their families. Officials who appear loyal today might withhold cooperation from a startled regime, engage in a power struggle, or defect en masse. If enough regime operators make these calculations, then Kim will find that the people he depends on to carry out his orders are prioritizing their own survival over that of his regime. And he could lose his hold on the country.

My interviews with erstwhile members of North Korea’s elite do not point to imminent regime collapse. Rather, they suggest a more haunting vulnerability for the Kim family: the loyalty of the elites, which is critical for regime survival, is becoming more performative, less genuine, and motivated by self-preservation, not a wellspring of ideological conviction. The regime’s habitual recourse to maximalist punishments and mass purges will likely drive more elites to defect and maybe, more strikingly, muster unprecedented expressions of collective action. Kim will face his last days when a large enough body of North Korean elites conclude that remaining loyal is no longer their safest and most rational choice.

JIEUN BAEK is the author of Privileged but Powerless: How North Korean Elite Grievances Reveal the Regime’s Greatest Weakness, from which this essay is adapted, and North Korea’s Hidden Revolution: How the Information Underground Is Transforming a Closed Society.

#6001. The World’s Biggest Crypto Heist

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

World Brief: The World’s Biggest Crypto Heist

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at an alleged North Korean cyberattack on Binance, Israel’s parliamentary election results, and mixed NATO messaging on Russia’s threat to Europe.

Hackers targeted the world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange late Thursday, stealing more than $6 billion in under 30 minutes. Experts are calling it the biggest crypto heist in history, and initial investigations suggest that North Korea is to blame.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at an alleged North Korean cyberattack on Binance, Israel’s parliamentary election results, and mixed NATO messaging on Russia’s threat to Europe.

Hackers targeted the world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange late Thursday, stealing more than $6 billion in under 30 minutes. Experts are calling it the biggest crypto heist in history, and initial investigations suggest that North Korea is to blame.

According to U.S.-based blockchain research firm TRM Labs, an elite North Korean hacking unit called TraderTraitor targeted $6.36 billion in Binance digital coins spread across 10 blockchains. The hackers were unable to access consumer accounts, instead only affecting Binance corporate holdings. Around $3 billion in assets have already been located or frozen, but TRM Labs estimates that the hackers had moved roughly $2.4 billion in crypto coins “beyond any issuer’s reach.”

“Your assets are safe,” Binance co-CEO Richard Teng said on Friday in a statement intended to ease customers’ concerns. However, analysts argue that the heist—despite its limited impact on consumers—has exposed the risks of trading crypto as well as the growing prowess of North Korean hacking efforts.

Cryptocurrency is a relatively new phenomenon in the world of economics—and one that is still largely unregulated. In June 2024, then-presidential candidate Donald Trump vowed to make the United States the “crypto capital of the planet” if elected for a second term, and since then, he has focused on reducing aggressive oversight of the lucrative industry. He also launched his own cryptocurrency platform, World Liberty Financial, which is run by his two sons, as well his own meme coin cryptocurrency.

Some countries, such as Russia, have turned to cryptocurrency to help evade sanctions and procure sensitive dual-use goods. Crypto companies have also faced a slew of cyberattacks. In December 2022, South Korea’s spy agency accused North Korean hackers of stealing roughly $1.1 billion in cryptocurrency and other virtual assets over the course of five years. Such hackers came under scrutiny again in February 2025, when the FBI said that TraderTraitor stole $1.5 billion from Bybit, one of Binance’s top rivals. And last month, attackers took around $350 million from cryptocurrency exchange Bitget.

A Binance spokesperson told The Associated Press on Friday that the company is “working closely with our law enforcement partners to confirm the identity of the criminals and bring them to justice.” However, TRM Labs said that its analysts had “high confidence” in the company’s assessment that the North Korean hacking group is responsible.

“North Korean hackers are among the world’s most aggressive and highly skilled cyber actors,” former South Korean Vice Defense Minister Shin Beom-chul told the Financial Times on Friday. Last year alone, Pyongyang’s hackers stole around $2 billion in cryptocurrency, accounting for roughly 60 percent of total crypto heists in the world, according to estimates from blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis.

Bitcoin’s value plummeted more than 5 percent overnight Thursday into Friday in response to the Binance heist, and other cryptocurrency exchanges dropped as much as 9 percent.

Israel’s coalition talks. Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and his allies have lost their parliamentary majority, final election results confirmed on Friday. Although Netanyahu’s Likud bloc still has the most seats in the Knesset, a historic showing by unaligned Arab parties means that lawmakers likely have several weeks of intense coalition-building negotiations ahead of them before a new government can be formed. In the interim, Netanyahu remains in charge.

According to the final results, Netanyahu’s Likud-led bloc won 53 seats, main opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot’s Yashar party-led alliance won 48 seats, and Arab-led groups won a record 19 seats. If Eisenkot wants to dethrone Netanyahu, then he will likely need to ally with the Arab parties to secure a majority. However, Eisenkot may be loath to do so, as one of his key partners, right-wing lawmaker Avigdor Lieberman, has ruled out joining a coalition with the predominately Arab Joint List or Raam parties.

Netanyahu has capitalized on such hesitance, accusing Qatar of funneling money into Israel to support the Arab vote. Israel’s elections committee said the “allegations of systematic violations of electoral integrity are baseless,” and the Qatari government dismissed Netanyahu’s accusations as “ridiculous and dangerous.” Yet Israeli intelligence agency Shin Bet has promised to investigate the claims.

Pentagon messaging on Moscow. The Trump administration has urged several NATO members in recent weeks to water down their rhetoric regarding the threat that Russia poses to Europe, Reuters reported on Friday. Sources said that senior U.S. defense officials, including Defense Department policy chief Elbridge Colby, have urged Estonia, Latvia, and Lithuania to dial down their warnings, arguing that they are not helpful for ongoing peace talks. Russian hybrid warfare, including drone incursions, has hit all three NATO members in the past year.

U.S. negotiators are reportedly considering new incentives to end the Russia-Ukraine war, including possible sanctions relief for and business deals with Moscow. Last week, U.S. special envoy Steve Witkoff held separate talks with Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky and Russian President Vladimir Putin; during talks with Putin, Witkoff called the Russian leader a “humanitarian” and announced that negotiations would soon begin on the technical level.

The report about the Pentagon’s urgings also came on the same day as a G-20 foreign ministers’ meeting in Atlanta that Russian Foreign Minister Sergey Lavrov attended in person. Deep rifts among the attendees prompted U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio to cancel the group’s traditional family photo, as many participants balked at standing beside Lavrov. Several European leaders have accused the Trump administration of being too soft on Moscow.

Global port strike. Dockworkers in 22 countries went on strike on Friday to protest the use of ports for arms exports and, specifically, for the delivery of weapons to active war zones. More than 25 unions across 113 ports—from Southern Europe to North Africa and India—backed the protests, which are in support of the so-called Istanbul Declaration, a document signed in May by dockworker unions from more than 35 European ports that demands an end to arms shipments from their sites.

“From planes to tanks, guns to bullets, explosives to ammunition, uniforms to boots, our ports have become replenishing and forwarding bases for war and ⁠genocide,” ​unions said at a press conference held to announce the strike. The Global Sumud Flotilla, a pro-Palestinian maritime activist group that has repeatedly tried to break Israel’s blockade of Gaza to deliver humanitarian supplies, also vowed to support ​Friday’s movement.

It is unclear how disruptive the 24-hour demonstration might be. Organizers said that the strike could impact more than 40 percent of Mediterranean cargo and around $2 billion worth of freight. However, shipping experts suspect that global trade will not be significantly hurt. Still, FP energy reporter Keith Johnson told World Brief that the strike “is more sand in the gears of a global economy already strained by U.S. President Donald Trump’s trade wars and real wars, both of which have sent freight rates to nosebleed levels this autumn.”

How did right-wing presidential candidate Flávio Bolsonaro react to incumbent President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva’s runoff election victory on Sunday?

A. He graciously conceded defeat. B. He called for his followers to protest his loss in Brasília. C. He fled to Chile. D. He said he would request an annulment of the election.

For a small sum of $56 million, you too can be the owner of a famous Impressionist painting. Claude Monet’s Iris became the most expensive artwork sold at auction in France last Thursday. The massive canvas, depicting some of Monet’s favorite flowers in his Giverny garden, is part of a 20-piece series produced near the end of the artist’s life. It took the title of most expensive work sold at a French auction from sculptor Amedeo Modigliani’s Head ⁠of ​a Caryatid, which sold for around $48 million in 2010.

D. He said he would request an annulment of the election.

Lula became the first Brazilian presidential candidate in history to come from behind to win the election’s second round, FP’s Catherine Osborn reports. Bolsonaro, the subject of several criminal probes, subsequently fled to Miami, where he called himself “the leader of Brazil.”
