#5982. The Limits of China’s Self-Sufficiency

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

JOEL WUTHNOW is a Senior Research Fellow at the Institute for National Strategic Studies at the National Defense University and a co-author of China’s Quest for Military Supremacy. The views expressed here are his own.

Why the Country’s Food Insecurity Could Prevent a War Over Taiwan

When assessing the probability of a conflict in the Taiwan Strait, analysts tend to focus on a few obvious risks that China would face—namely, military defeat, economic slowdown, and restricted oil and technology imports. All of these are important, but an equally significant vulnerability is often left off the list: China’s reliance on soybean imports.

Beijing recognizes this liability. At the 20th Party Congress, in 2022, Chinese leader Xi Jinping identified “ensuring the security of food, energy resources, and critical industrial supply chains” as issues essential to national security and social stability. Food security, conspicuously listed first, is an especially sensitive issue for China’s 1.4 billion inhabitants: roughly 15 percent of the country’s population today was alive during the 1959–61 famine, which resulted in an estimated 30 million deaths.

As with technology and oil, Beijing has tried to limit its dependence on soybeans by building up stockpiles and diversifying its sources. But because soybeans are a fragile foodstuff, there is only so much China can do. Beijing can support a war effort, therefore, for only as long as the Chinese public could put the People’s Liberation Army’s success above its nutritional needs. And the key for deterring Chinese leaders from starting a conflict is to convince them that a domestic crisis would arrive sooner than any battlefield victory.

Disruption to critical imports is one of the most powerful deterrents to China’s use of force, and it’s a threat that the Chinese government has spent decades working to counter. The specter of a foreign blockade in the Strait of Malacca, through which most of Chinese oil imports pass, has worried Chinese leaders since at least 2003, when Chinese leader Hu Jintao warned about the risk of major powers’ interference creating a “Malacca dilemma” for China’s energy security. Beijing, however, has stockpiled massive reserves of oil, which it could complement in a crisis by increasing imports of Russian oil and, as Moscow has done, building up a sanctions-evading shadow fleet to transport oil from the Middle East.

Access to foreign technology is a newer but just as important concern, and China is similarly stockpiling critical components, such as chips. It is also having success in developing local alternatives. Beijing has already pulled ahead in some sectors, such as solar and battery manufacturing, or it is working to close the gap in others, as in semiconductors.

But Beijing has fewer options when it comes to self-sufficiency in food. Domestic production of staple grains—such as wheat, rice, and corn—accounts for more than 90 percent of consumption in these categories, but China remains dependent on foreign producers for other important foodstuffs, including soybeans, beef, and barley. Between 2000 and 2020, the share of China’s food supply provided by imports grew from six percent to 34 percent, and analysts predict it to increase to 41 percent by 2030. Part of this growth comes from the country’s economic development; as people become wealthier, their diets diversify, and they are no longer satisfied eating only grains. Between 1962 and 2024, meat as a percentage of the typical Chinese diet increased by a factor of 18.

This is where the central role of soybeans comes in. The legume provides a complex array of proteins in animal feed that other food sources and synthetic amino acids cannot replicate. Without it, livestock herds in China would shrink, thereby threatening domestic meat and dairy supplies. Soybean oil is also the most used cooking oil in China, making up nearly half the market. Alternatives, such as palm and rapeseed oils, are also mostly imported and face the same risks of maritime disruption, offering little relief.

China has invested a great deal in securing and, when possible, diversifying its access to foreign food sources. In 2013, a Chinese company bought Smithfield Foods, the United States’ largest pork producer. And state-owned enterprises such as COFCO International, a branch of China’s largest food and agriculture company, are buying farms and building logistical infrastructure in major agriculture-producing countries, such as Argentina and Brazil, which allows them to buy directly from farmers. Such direct investments provide a buffer against price volatility, giving Beijing more control over the volume and cost of supplies at their source. China has also steadily increased its own domestic meat production, which accounts for more than 90 percent of China’s demand. But such efforts do little to mitigate the effects of disruptions in maritime shipping—especially of soybeans, which is a supply chain that is fragile even in peacetime.

The central rub is that China has no choice but to import its soybeans. There is not enough arable land in China to both expand soybean cultivation and sustain self-reliance in staple grains. (Soybeans are also a much less efficient crop than corn or wheat in China’s arid north, making this tradeoff the economical choice.) But the countries that grow and export soybeans are almost all in the Western Hemisphere, which presents even more logistical challenges. When the trade war between the United States and China broke out in 2018, for instance, Beijing limited its U.S. soybean imports from 40 percent to about 20 percent of its supply, making up the difference with increased imports from Brazil. But Brazil’s poor infrastructure and frequent natural disasters make its harvest more susceptible to failure than the U.S. harvest. In 2024, one Brazilian state lost a quarter to a third of its soybean harvest because of flooding.

U.S. and Brazilian ports are also about as far from China as they could be. Brazil’s Santos port, for example, is more than 10,000 miles from Guangzhou, which takes 45 days to ship. Because of soybeans’ sensitivity to humidity, this distance means that ships often arrive in China with rotten cargo. For similar reasons, the legume is also difficult to stockpile. Unlike rice and wheat reserves, which Beijing claims could last for years, soybean reserves are smaller and must be replaced more frequently. Chinese stockpiles might be able to last only a few months if its imports were cut off. Although China has been experimenting with bioengineered supplements in an effort to reduce the amount of soybeans in animal feed, rising food demand is offsetting any gains. In other words, its dependence on soybean imports is only continuing to grow.

The outbreak of war between China and the United States would pose existential threats to this supply chain. In the most extreme scenario, the U.S. military could block shipments from the Western Hemisphere to China, which would take away most of the country’s soybean imports. China has no naval bases or forward forces in Latin America that could counter these efforts.

But China’s food supply chains would suffer from disruption even without a U.S. embargo or blockade. Any war in the Pacific would bring with it massive risk premiums for maritime shipment insurance that might cause shipping firms to balk. Such an effect has already been on display for shipping in the Red Sea, where Houthi missile threats have caused insurance costs to skyrocket since 2024. A war in the Pacific could be far more intense and costly because it would involve major navies, and ships could not route around the danger zone, making the long-haul journey from Brazil financially ruinous for many companies.

Beijing could, in theory, offer sovereign guarantees to offset firms’ insurance premiums. Even then, the firms transporting soybeans, most of which are owned by U.S. and allied country parties, might cease transporting products to China to avoid abetting a hostile regime, to steer clear of dangers that would put their employees at risk, and to head off possible future exposure to sanctions for doing business with the country.

Chinese analysts believe that China could circumvent some of these risks by using its own commercial fleet. Although this fleet has impressive capacity during peacetime—the Chinese shipping giant COSCO owns 468 dry bulk carriers—in wartime it would be stretched thin, facing demands to help move construction materials, industrial supplies, and foodstuffs for the People’s Liberation Army and to transport critical military materials such as coal and iron ore. Even available ships would likely lack personnel with the training needed to keep soybeans fresh during the long journey. And if ships tried to avoid the most dangerous sea-lanes, trips could take even longer, surpassing the 40 days that soybeans can be safely stored. Cargo might arrive rotten, if it arrived at all.

A war in the Pacific would not trigger an immediate food crisis in China. In the shorter term, the government could manage reduced imports by releasing supplies of soybeans, frozen pork, and other staples from state reserves. It could also reduce the minimum soybean inclusion rates for animal feed, which would slow livestock growth and cut meat output but which would be workable for a time. A few months of a managed crunch, however, is a best-case scenario. More likely is that panic buying and shipping disruptions would drain reserves faster than planned, turning a manageable shortfall into rising prices and visible shortages within months.

There is reason to believe, for instance, that Chinese stockpiles might not provide the short-term security they purport to. Over the last decade, local government warehouses meant to store emergency food supplies have been found empty or filled with rotten supplies, largely because of corruption. This not only calls into question how much extra food China truly has but also undermines public confidence in the government. In the case of a conflict, citizens conscious of the long-term disruption to come would begin panic buying and hoarding supplies. Whatever reserves do exist would eventually run out, with members of the elite likely being prioritized.

If the conflict dragged on, the situation would become increasingly dire. Agricultural producers, especially small farms, would be forced to cull their herds. Poultry, which requires especially high soybean content in its feed, would be the first casualty, which would then threaten the egg supply. Pork would be next. Cooking oils would grow scarce and expensive, making it difficult for restaurants and households to function. Eventually, public patience would wear thin: hoarding, protests over prices and shortages, and local officials diverting supplies could put mounting pressure on Beijing to end the war.

Unlike China’s vulnerabilities in energy or technology, the fragilities of the food system are endemic. If a conflict were to break out in the Pacific, the United States would not need to institute a blockade against China’s agricultural supplies or gather an international coalition to sanction China’s soybean imports. Soaring insurance costs and ship owners’ reluctance to sail into a war zone would be enough to squeeze the flow, and the Chinese government would have little recourse to stop it.

If Chinese leaders believed that they could achieve victory quickly and with few shots fired, they might take the bet. Deterrence, then, will require convincing them that they cannot. The United States, Taiwan, and other governments should invest in drones, rebuild munitions stockpiles, and ramp up messaging to China that a long, drawn-out conflict is likely. As long as China’s leaders believe that a war would be indecisive in its early stages, the threat of domestic crisis would be a powerful reason not to start one.

How the Search for Economic Security Will End in Geopolitical Calamity

Henry Farrell, Abraham Newman, and Yeling Tan

Demographic Decline, Political Paralysis, and the Fate of Great Powers

What Happens When Oceans Are Up for Grabs

The Delusions of Summitry and the Limits of Personalist Diplomacy

The Arab Monarchies Must Help Washington and Tehran Save Face

#5981. Poll flashes warning signs for Republicans in 7 battleground Senate races

Kansas Reflector political reporter Anna Kaminski and SCETV public affairs reporter Gavin Jackson join 'Fox News Live' to break down the latest polling data and early voting trends in the tight Kansas and South Carolina Senate races, discussing how undecided voters could decide Senate control in the midterms.

Republicans are trailing Democrats in seven key Senate battlegrounds, including longtime GOP strongholds Texas and Kansas, according to a poll released Sunday, just days before the midterm elections.

The New York Times/Siena poll found Democratic candidates leading in five states currently represented by Republican senators — Alaska, Kansas, Maine, Ohio and Texas — while the race in Iowa is virtually tied. In Michigan, the only Democratic-held Senate seat surveyed, the party's candidate holds a double-digit lead.

Don Levy, executive director of the Siena Research Institute, said the results suggest Democrats could capture control of the Senate, though he cautioned that several races remain within the poll's margin of error.

SENATE CONTROL IS A COIN FLIP — AND DEMOCRATS’ PATH RUNS THROUGH TRUMP COUNTRY

A voter fills out their ballot at a polling location inside Wayne Community College Eastern Campus on the final day of early voting for the midterm election in Detroit, Michigan, on Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026. (Emily Elconin/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

"While a blue wave and Democratic control of the Senate is, according to our polls, a distinct possibility, with so many races within the poll's total margin of error, a Senate majority for either party remains possible," Levy said.

The findings are particularly striking in Texas, which has not elected a Democrat to the Senate since 1988, and Kansas, where Democrats have not won a Senate race since 1932.

In Texas, Democratic state lawmaker James Talarico leads Republican Attorney General Ken Paxton 50% to 43% among likely voters. In Kansas, Democrat Adam Hamilton leads Republican Sen. Roger Marshall 49% to 44%.

Democratic Senate candidate James Talarico, left, speaks during a press conference at the Hyatt Centric Congress Avenue on July 22, 2026, in Austin, Texas. Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton waves at the conclusion of a campaign event at Matt's Rancho Martinez restaurant on August 4, 2026, in Allen, Texas. (Brandon Bell/Getty Images; Brandon Bell/Getty Images)

The poll also found former Democratic Sen. Sherrod Brown leading Republican Sen. Jon Husted in Ohio, 49% to 44%, while Democrat Troy Jackson leads Republican Sen. Susan Collins in Maine, 51% to 45%.

In Alaska, former Democratic Rep. Mary Peltola leads Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan 48% to 46%. Iowa is even closer, with Democrat Josh Turek edging Republican Rep. Ashley Hinson 48% to 47%.

In Michigan, Democrat Abdul El-Sayed holds the largest lead of the seven Senate races surveyed, leading Republican Mike Rogers 53% to 42%.

Republican Michigan Senate candidate Mike Rogers, left, and Democratic rival Abdul El-Sayed. (Nic Antaya/Getty Images; Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images)

The poll also found signs of broader dissatisfaction with President Donald Trump and Republicans' handling of the economy.

Just 38% of likely voters across the seven states approve of Trump's job performance, according to the Times, despite Trump carrying five of those states by double-digit margins in the 2024 presidential election.

President Donald Trump arrives to speak during a campaign rally at Rock Hill Sports and Event Center on Thursday, Oct. 29, 2026, in Rock Hill, S.C. (Scott Kinser/AP)

Democrats hold a nine-point advantage over Republicans on which party voters trust more to address the cost of living, with Democratic Senate candidates leading on the issue in all seven states.

The poll also found Democratic candidates leading among independent voters in every state surveyed.

VETERAN DEM OPERATIVE SAYS SUBPAR GOP CANDIDATES POISED TO HAND OVER SENATE MAJORITY

Democratic candidates improved their standing in six of the seven states compared with Times/Siena polls conducted in September. Alaska was the lone exception.

Still, the poll offered some encouraging signs for Republicans.

Voters who made their decisions within the past month favored Republican candidates by nine points, and several races remain within the margin of error.

The Times also noted that polling underestimated Collins in 2020, when she trailed in every survey before winning re-election comfortably.

Democrats must defend their existing Senate seats and flip at least four Republican-held seats to capture control of the chamber.

The surveys of likely voters were conducted from Oct. 23 through Nov. 1, with state-level margins of sampling error ranging from plus or minus 3.4 percentage points in Texas to 5 percentage points in Alaska.

#5977. Steve Hilton rallies at 'symbol of 16 years of Democrat failure' in closing days of California race

California gubernatorial candidate Steve Hilton says the Pacific Palisades symbolizes Democratic failures to prevent wildfires, respond to disasters and rebuild communities.

Republican California gubernatorial candidate Steve Hilton rallied Sunday in Pacific Palisades, a Los Angeles neighborhood devastated by the January 2025 wildfires, blaming Democratic leadership for the destruction and slow recovery just days before voters will choose the state's next governor.

"I’m so sorry. And it’s so sad about what happened because it could and should have been avoided," Hilton told supporters and residents. "This place is a symbol of 16 years of Democrat failure."

The Palisades Fire burned more than 23,000 acres and destroyed roughly 6,800 structures, many of them homes. Fueled by powerful winds and dry conditions, the blaze displaced tens of thousands of residents and devastated large sections of the community, leaving a massive rebuilding effort in its wake.

JURY DEADLOCKS IN FEDERAL TRIAL OF MAN ACCUSED OF STARTING DEADLY PALISADES FIRE IN LOS ANGELES

California Republican gubernatorial candidate Steve Hilton holds a press briefing on homelessness during a campaign event on Friday, Oct. 30, 2026, in Sacramento, Calif. (AP Photo/Jose Luis Villegas)

Hilton argued that the disaster and its aftermath reflected broader failures of California's Democratic leadership, from wildfire prevention and emergency response to rebuilding efforts.

"These are not random, disconnected events," Hilton said. "These are what has gone wrong in the state of California over 16 years of one-party rule."

Democrats have held California's governorship for the past 16 years. Hilton faces Democrat Xavier Becerra, a former state attorney general and Health and Human Services secretary under President Joe Biden, in Tuesday's election to succeed term-limited Gov. Gavin Newsom.

A split image of California Republican gubernatorial candidate Steve Hilton and Democratic gubernatorial candidate Xavier Becerra. (Getty)

Hilton, a former Fox News commentator who previously advised British Prime Minister David Cameron, has made criticism of the state's Democratic leadership a central theme of his campaign.

STEVE HILTON LAUNCHES AD MOCKING XAVIER BECERRA'S '36 YEARS' AS A CAREER POLITICIAN IN CALIFORNIA GOV RACE

At the rally, he accused state and local officials of failing to adequately prepare for the fires and help residents rebuild, blaming what he called a combination of "incompetence, ideology and corruption."

He also criticized environmental and land-management policies, arguing that inadequate brush clearance contributed to the destruction.

"The ideology that for years and years and years not just failed to clear the brush that could have prevented these fires here and in Altadena ... but deliberately chose not to do that," Hilton said.

Flames from the Palisades Fire burn a building on Sunset Boulevard amid a powerful windstorm on January 8, 2025, in the Pacific Palisades neighborhood of Los Angeles, California. (Apu Gomes/Getty Images)

Hilton pledged to appoint a recovery CEO to oversee rebuilding efforts if elected, arguing that residents had waited too long for their communities to recover.

"I spent the time, I’ve listened, and I can tell you right now, when I’m governor, I will appoint a recovery CEO to finally get things done, to finally get what you deserve," Hilton said.

He closed by urging voters to reject the idea that California must remain under Democratic control.

"This is not a blue state. It is not a red state. It’s the Golden State," Hilton said. "And we are going to fight with everything we’ve got."

#5974. Haley Stevens' endorsement stunt backfires with El-Sayed setting single-day fundraising record

Democratic Rep. Haley Stevens endorses Republican Mike Rogers over fellow Democrat Abdul El-Sayed in the Michigan Senate race, triggering a major party backlash. Top Democrats, including Chuck Schumer and Nancy Pelosi, publicly condemn the endorsement. Former communications director for Speaker McCarthy Mark Bednar joins 'Fox & Friends First' to analyze the political fallout.

Democratic Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed’s campaign says it raised more than $2.4 million in a single day, setting a fundraising record in the final stretch of the Michigan race.

The Democratic candidate's campaign said Sunday’s haul came from more than 65,000 donors, who gave an average of $36.74. Nearly 29,600 people contributed to the campaign for the first time.

The fundraising surge came on the heels of Rep. Haley Stevens, D-Mich., endorsing Republican Senate candidate Mike Rogers on Sunday after months of turmoil following her primary loss to the more progressive candidate.

DEMOCRAT HALEY STEVENS ENDORSES GOP SENATE NOMINEE MIKE ROGERS

Rep. Haley Stevens’ endorsement of Republican Mike Rogers on Sunday drew swift backlash from Democrats, who accused her of undermining the party’s Senate nominee.

"As Michiganders head to the polls, the momentum behind Abdul’s campaign has never been stronger," campaign spokesperson Cole Wozniak said in a statement following the final-hour cash haul.

Stevens, who was considered the more moderate establishment candidate in her primary against El-Sayed earlier this year, said she remains "a very proud Democrat" who loves Michigan.

She insisted, however, in the video message posted to social media over the weekend that she could not support El-Sayed, citing his backing from radical left-wing political streamer Hasan Piker.

"Hey Michigan, as you may have seen, our Democratic nominee for U.S. Senate, Abdul said that I was no longer welcome in his campaign and that I should stay away and he's right. And that's why I'm endorsing Mike Rogers for the United States Senate today," Stevens said in a video posted on X.

The endorsement of the Trump-backed Republican candidate drew swift criticism from Democrats.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer called it "a despicable betrayal" and said his caucus in the upper chamber remains united behind El-Sayed.

Michigan Democratic Party Chair Curtis Hertel also condemned Stevens’ decision, saying she had "abandoned everything she claims to care about."

Democratic Senate nominee in Michigan Abdul El-Sayed takes questions from reporters after a campaign rally on Oct. 24, 2026 in Detroit, Michigan. (Paul Steinhauser/Fox News Digital)

El-Sayed brushed off the endorsement Sunday, telling reporters he does not think about Stevens or Rogers and was focused on voters and policy.

He also has attempted in recent months to distance himself from Piker amid the streamer's rising national recognition and increased scrutiny on his controversial statements — including antisemitic sentiments and claiming that "America deserved 9/11."

#5966. California is still without a functioning community solar program

California, the state with the most installed solar capacity in the country, has spent years trying, but still doesn’t have a community solar program. That means residential solar and storage systems are often still only accessible by the affluent, and low-income residents and renters continue to experience the country’s second-highest electricity rates without any self-generated relief.

A community solar project in Colorado, developed by Pivot Energy.

Despite the California Legislature mandating that the state create a community solar program in 2022, nothing yet has been established. Solar and community advocates have been battling with the California Public Utilities Commission (CPUC) and investor-owned utilities to establish a working community solar market, but talks have reached a stalemate. Outgoing Gov. Gavin Newsom vetoed the latest community solar legislation in September, and the state continues to lose out on this new solar market development.

In 2022, Newsom signed AB 2316 into law, which aimed to create a community renewable energy program that would support both solar and storage projects. The bill required large utilities serving more than 100,000 customers to create and implement programs that “enable ratepayers to participate directly in offsite electrical generation facilities that use eligible renewable energy resources.”

In 2024, the CPUC rejected a proposal from solar advocacy groups that would support community solar in the state, instead putting more decision-making power into local utility hands. State legislators then attempted in 2025 to pass a new bill (AB 1260) designed to strengthen and expand the initial framework for California’s community solar-plus-storage program, guaranteeing affordability to low-income customers, among other stipulations. AB 1260 was never fully adopted.

Instead, community and solar advocates threw their support behind AB 1813, the Community Renewable Energy Program Act. Groups like Californians for Local, Affordable Solar and Storage (CLASS) said AB 1813 was a workable community solar and storage program that could overcome the CPUC’s “built-to-fail” program outline.

California Gov. Gavin Newsom Photo credit: Gage Skidmore

The California Legislature passed AB 1813, which directed regulators to value community solar and storage using the CPUC’s own Avoided Cost Calculator, an existing tool the state has declined to apply, and required paired battery storage so projects deliver power when the grid needs it most. Ultimately, Newsom vetoed the bill in September, and community solar development in California is still stalled.

“With Gov. Newsom’s veto of AB 1813, California continues to fall further behind other states who are aggressively moving forward with successful community solar programs. Unfortunately, the voices of community leaders, environmental justice organizations, ratepayer advocates, labor, community choice aggregators, the building industry and so many others were denied a solution they have long fought for,” said Derek Chernow, executive director of CLASS. “Study after study demonstrates the benefits of community solar-plus-storage to households, businesses, our environment and the grid. We are committed to working with the next administration to make community solar and storage part of the solution to California’s growing affordability crisis.”

Newsom, whose gubernatorial term ends in January 2027, stated in his veto that AB 1813 “would effectively require an administratively set price for the generation of power that exceeds the value that generation provides to the grid. This would result in significant upward pressure on consumer energy bills without delivering reliable, high-performance capacity to the grid at the times we need it. Fundamentally, this bill would shift costs and work counter to the state’s energy affordability and reliability goals. For these reasons, I cannot sign this bill.”

Without an approved process for bringing community renewable energy efforts online, project developers will focus on opportunities outside the state.

A Renewable Properties solar + storage project in California. Credit: Renewable Properties

Aaron Halimi, founder and CEO of Renewable Properties, a community solar developer headquartered in San Francisco, said Newsom’s veto relied on outdated cost assumptions and would hurt local rate payers looking for utility bill relief.

“Twenty-two other states have workable community solar programs that are attracting investment, creating local economic benefits and expanding consumer access to clean energy,” he said. “As a result of this veto, Renewable Properties will invest more of our resources into those 22 community solar states instead of our home state of California.”

There’s not much else advocates can do until the next legislative session begins in December. Then a new governor would have until September 2027 to sign any community solar legislation into law. Chernow said CLASS will continue putting pressure on legislators to build a successful community solar market in California.

Kelly Pickerel was a long-time reporter with Solar Power World before leaving her post as editor in chief in 2026. You may find her on LinkedIn.

#5959. Distributed Energy Infrastructure completes New York solar project despite site challenges

Distributed Energy Infrastructure (DEI), an EPC firm for solar and battery storage, has completed the 3.37-MWDC Sapphire Solar project in Jefferson County, New York.

The Sapphire Solar project. Credit: Distributed Energy Infrastructure

Shallow bedrock and a high water table presented site challenges that required the team to adapt its design mid-construction. During excavation, DEI encountered shale bedrock only 16 to 20 in. below grade across several areas of the site, accompanied by groundwater continuously seeping into open trenches. The conditions impacted installation and equipment pad areas, making the original underground DC conduit design impractical to implement.

DEI’s engineering, construction, procurement and contractor teams collaborated closely to quickly develop a revised design that converted a portion of the DC underground scope to an above-ground CAB system. Underground trenching was retained at row-to-row crossings and other locations where it remained appropriate. The revised design also preserved site access for the owner’s long-term operations and maintenance needs.

The full redesign, material procurement and field implementation were completed within the timeframe originally planned for the underground trenching scope.

“When we encounter unexpected challenges, our goal is to address them quickly and adapt our approach to what will work best for the site,” said Rusfid Haroon, senior project manager at DEI. “Even hitting shallow bedrock and water table across the site, we brought together the experience of each team working on the site to re-evaluate, re-design and implement a solution that kept the project moving while ensuring long-term reliability.”

News item from Distributed Energy Infrastructure

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5958. Judge tosses Trump's attempt to block Alaska Senate results if GOP incumbent loses

President Trump listens as Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan of Alaska, who is running for reelection, speaks at an Oct. 13 campaign rally in Wasilla, Alaska. Alex Brandon/AP hide caption

A federal judge has thrown out the Trump administration's highly unusual attempt to block the certification of results from a key U.S. Senate race in Alaska if the Republican incumbent loses.

In a ruling released Sunday, U.S. District Judge Sharon Gleason found the federal government does not have the ability to bring this lawsuit, which cited the preamble of the National Voter Registration Act.

It asked the judge to stop state officials from certifying election results to allow for an investigation into whether voters were confused by having two similarly named Senate candidates appear on Alaska's ranked-choice ballot.

But Judge Gleason concluded that such a court order would likely violate Alaskan voters' right to a secret ballot.

"The absence of any explanation from the United States as to how its requested relief could redress its alleged injury while simultaneously maintaining the secrecy of the ballot is telling," wrote Gleason, a nominee of former President Barack Obama.

The Justice Department is planning to appeal, spokesperson Kiersten Pels told NPR in a statement. "Alaskan voters deserve a fair election, not to be misled in an attempt to rig outcomes," Pels added.

Voting rights groups that stepped in to help defend Alaska against the lawsuit cheered the ruling.

"DOJ's attempt to throw out Alaska's election results before a single ballot was counted was lawless from the start, and the court saw it for exactly what it was. It was a reckless waste of taxpayer money aimed at silencing hundreds of thousands of Alaska voters," Sophia Lin Lakin, director of the American Civil Liberties Union's Voting Rights Project, said in a statement.

President Trump said he ordered this lawsuit to be filed because out of the four Senate candidates on Alaska's ballot, two have similar names — Sen. Dan Sullivan, the Republican incumbent, and Daniel Sullivan Jr., who is not affiliated with a political party.

Alaska's Republican Party supported an earlier effort in state court that tried to keep Sullivan Jr., the non-incumbent, off the ballot. But in June, the Alaska Supreme Court ruled Sullivan Jr. has the right to run as a candidate.

Still, Republican allegations of Democrats propping up Sullivan Jr. to spoil the incumbent Sullivan's chances of winning reelection have long hung over this closely watched Senate contest.

A New York Times report about Democratic campaign operatives' efforts to recruit Sullivan Jr. — which NPR has not independently confirmed — drew new attention to those allegations. Alaska's Republican Gov. Mike Dunleavy has ordered an investigation and indicated the results of the race may not be certified by the state depending on how that investigation turns out.

"If we find out there's wrongdoing, and if that means that the certification then is stopped or changed, then that's what happens," Dunleavy said during an Oct. 30 interview with NewsNation.

At an Oct. 30 press conference in Anchorage, U.S. Attorney Michael Heyman said there is an "ongoing" criminal probe by the Justice Department.

The Democratic candidate, former Rep. Mary Peltola, has denied any involvement.

In an interview Sunday with CNN's State of the Union, incumbent Sullivan said that he would accept the election results, win or lose.

This controversy hasn't stopped Alaskans from voting. Over 86,700 voters in the state have cast their ballots during early voting, according to the latest figures from Alaska's Division of Elections. And more Alaskans are expected to exercise their rights as voting continues through Tuesday's Election Day.

Additional reporting by NPR's Eric McDaniel. Edited by Benjamin Swasey

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

#5954. How to think about the midterms — and the political moment the country is in

President Donald Trump spoke to members of the media during a Halloween party at his Mar-a-Lago club on October 31, 2026 in Palm Beach, Florida. Andrew Harnik/Getty Images North America hide caption

📲 Get the latest on the midterms & more in the NPR App

President Trump was elected in 2024, in large measure, with a mandate to bring down prices. But that hasn't happened. Instead, Trump is facing record-low approval ratings, record-high intensity of opposition, and voters are blaming him for prices that have continued to rise since he took office this second time around.

Most presidents get more blame and credit than they deserve for the economy. But the war with Iran and Trump's tariffs were choices that have directly led to increased prices on goods and energy.

The economy is increasingly bifurcated with the wealthy doing well, in large part, because of increasing stock portfolios dominated by AI companies, while regular Americans are turning against AI data centers, struggling because of inflation that is outpacing wages, a middling labor market and interest rates that are creeping higher. Those rates are making it harder to buy a home or a car, making millions of people feel like they're treading water.

The latest NPR/PBS News/Marist poll showed a plurality — 44% — said their finances have mostly stayed the same and another 38% saying their economic situations have worsened. Only 15% said things have actually gotten better for them from a money standpoint.

Add to that the disapproval of the administration's hard-line immigration enforcement tactics, cuts to domestic spending on programs like Medicaid and other health care programs, and the slashing of regulations and the government workforce, and it's angering swing voters and firing up Democrats.

As a result, the president's party is likely to pay the price. Midterms are usually difficult for the president's party to begin with.

Only twice since World War II has the president's party gained seats in the House in a midterm election. On average, the president's party loses 26 seats in midterm years, and it's worse when a president's approval rating is below 50%, as Trump's has been. When that's the case, the presidents' parties have lost an average of 35 seats. When they're above 50%, they still lose seats but only 15 on average, 20 seats better than when a president is unpopular.

The fact that presidents' parties lose seats in midterms is something Trump himself has acknowledged multiple times at campaign events.

Meanwhile, Democrats have had political success in this Trump term. They have consistently overperformed in special elections, which are indicators of midterm prowess, and they cleaned up in the 2025 off-year elections. Since then, the political landscape has only gotten worse for Republicans because of the unpopular Iran war that has led to skyrocketing gas prices.

All of that points to Democratic gains this cycle. They are favored to win control of the House, and they are in striking distance of winning the Senate, something that had been seen as a longshot at the beginning of this cycle.

While the landscape has undoubtedly moved more in Democrats' direction over the last several months, the extent of the likely potential gains is not clear.

A big reason for that is structural. When looking at the Cook Political Report's ratings, there are about 30 fewer competitive districts — ones rated as lean or toss up — this year than in 2018 when Democrats won a net gain of more than 40 seats in the House.

Mid-decade redistricting has also helped to insulate Republicans this cycle, even if marginally. After all the redistricting fights across the country, with Trump explicitly pushing GOP-led state legislatures to get more Republican seats, an NPR analysis shows Republicans netted an estimated 8-seat advantage out of redistricting.

Depending on how Latinos in South Texas, in particular, vote those gains might be lower.

While Republicans face significant headwinds and the GOP is not viewers favorably, the Democratic Party is also not viewed positively. Democrats have had trouble wrangling a base of voters who should be Democrats but are restive and unsatisfied by the party.

That said, the latest NPR poll showed that self-identified "democratic socialists" were very engaged in these midterms and hotly disapprove of the job Trump is doing.

As the old saying goes, the enemy of my enemy is my friend. This group of voters may vote in big numbers in this election as a check on Trump — 44% of poll respondents said their vote was a vote against Trump while 21% said it was a vote for him. But there's a likely clash of ideals and approach coming on the left over the next several months, as the Democratic presidential primaries.

And make no mistake, the 2028 election begins almost immediately after these midterms. It's an open election — the first one post-Trump, post-Obama-Biden, post-Clintons and Bushes. Candidates who want to make a splash and need a national boost tend to announce earlier rather than later. Think: January, February.

It's important to remember nothing is a done deal until the results are in. A lot of this is going to come down to Republican turnout.

That's why Trump has been deployed to red states to try and push and plead with his base to get out and vote. Without a big GOP turnout in these places, a one- or two-seat pick up for Democrats in the Senate can quickly turn into six or seven.

Be ready for surprises. But if Democrats don't win the House, it would be the shocker of all shockers and represent a polling error like we haven't seen this century.

While most states and districts will be called by the time people go to bed on election night, some will not. Those lingering races may be in key places, and given the margins, particularly in the Senate, it's possible party control won't be known for days, if not weeks.

Trump is already laying the groundwork for claims of rigged elections. That's no surprise. He's done that after every election that hasn't gone his way previously in one way or another. After the 2020 election, his false claims of fraud led to the Jan. 6 siege at the Capitol.

The latest NPR poll found most people across parties overwhelmingly believe their states will run fair and accurate elections and that their preferred candidate should accept the outcome, no matter what it is.

But a quarter of Republicans said their candidate likely shouldn't accept the results. That leaves room for potential chaos and misinformation if elections are close and not decided for weeks.

Trump has been a clear drag on the GOP this cycle. You don't have to believe polls to know it's the case. Look at what Republican candidates are doing. Their mentions in ads of Trump during the primaries were sky high.

Republicans will admit privately that Trump is a double-edged sword — he's needed with the base, but toxic with swing voters.

And that's been the story of Trump for the past decade — beloved by an ardent slice of the country and loathed by an even bigger one.

But this year, with the economic strain on people and the U.S.'s continued involvement in the kind of ongoing war he promised to keep the country out of, polls have found consistently over a long period of time this year that his support with many of those who crossed over to vote for him in 2024 has cratered.

That hardly sets up Trump and his party for success Tuesday and beyond — despite what will be claims of nefariousness that will hold little weight. But that doesn't mean either party will have massive majorities, and compromise will be necessary to get big things done.

#5948. EIA: Renewables could overtake natural gas output in 2027

Renewable energy is still outpacing fossil fuels as the leading new sources of electricity on the U.S. grid. Electrical generation from renewable energy sources has increased by 10%, or more than 43 GW, in the first eight months of 2026, according to an analysis of Energy Information Administration (EIA) data by the SUN DAY Campaign.

Renewables are expected to account for more than 40% of the United States’ total electrical output over the next year. At the start of September, renewable energy accounted for 34.5% of utility-scale generation in the country.

Utility-scale and small-scale solar are the two fastest growing renewable energy sources being tied to the grid. Compared to the first eight months of 2025, utility-scale solar projects increased by 20%, and smaller systems increased by 12% in 2026. Between September 2025 and August 2026 utility-scale solar increased by 27.1 GW and small-scale solar 6.5-GW. Battery energy storage systems’ capacity increased by 17.6 GW.

New natural gas and nuclear power only grew by 2.6% an 1.3%. Coal’s electrical output decreased by more than 8%. If new renewable energy proliferation continues at its expected pace, EIA is predicting that it could overtake natural gas output by summer 2027.

“Each new issue of EIA’s ‘Electric Power Monthly’ reinforces the theme that the growth of solar, wind and battery storage has become unstoppable,” said Ken Bossong, executive director of the SUN DAY Campaign. “Meanwhile, the share of the nation’s electrical generation and generating capacity provided by fossil fuels and nuclear continues to shrink.”

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5946. Connecticut Sen. Chris Murphy is hospitalized with a stroke

Sen. Chris Murphy, D-Conn., appears at a news conference at the U.S. Capitol in 2025. Murphy's office says he was hospitalized this weekend with a stroke. Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images hide caption

Sen. Chris Murphy, D-Conn., has been hospitalized following a stroke, according to his office.

In a statement on Sunday, Murphy's chief of staff, Allison Herwitt, said he is expected to make a full recovery.

"Yesterday afternoon, Senator Murphy started experiencing numbness in his arm while at his son's hockey game," Herwitt wrote. "He was taken to a local hospital, where it was discovered he had experienced a stroke. He was transferred to Johns Hopkins for further monitoring and is responding well to treatment."

Murphy, 53, is a prominent figure in the party and has been a prolific campaign surrogate for candidates through the midterm cycle. Viewed as a possible 2028 presidential contender, Murphy has carved out a position as a foreign policy leader and champion of aggressive executive branch oversight.

He recently returned from a multistop tour in the Middle East, after which he warned that President Trump would dramatically escalate his war on Iran after the midterm election.

The lawmaker is the latest in a string of lawmakers to experience a health crisis this year. Sen. Mitch McConnell, R-Ky., missed months of Senate work during a lengthy hospital stay and rehabilitation process. Rep. Tom Kean, R-N.J., was missing from the Capitol for months for what he later announced was treatment for depression.

In 2022, three prominent Democratic lawmakers — Ben Ray Lujan of New Mexico, Chris Van Hollen of Maryland, and then-Senate candidate John Fetterman — were all hospitalized for strokes.

#5942. Haley Stevens endorses GOP candidate in Michigan Senate race, spurning Abdul El-Sayed

Michigan Republican Senate candidate Mike Rogers, right, and Rep. Haley Stevens, D-Mich., attend a service at Perfecting Church, Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026, in Detroit. Jose Juarez/AP hide caption

DETROIT — Democrats in Michigan and Washington are furious after Rep. Haley Stevens revoked her endorsement of former primary rival Abdul El-Sayed to back his Republican opponent in the race for U.S. Senate, Mike Rogers.

The dramatic reversal less than 48 hours before Election Day stunned Stevens allies and friends in Michigan and on Capitol Hill, with several saying publicly she was reassuring them up until the eleventh hour that she would continue to support El-Sayed, even as the rift between the two Democrats spilled back into public view in the final days of the race.

Despite efforts by Democrats to bring Stevens and El-Sayed together in a show of unity before Election Day, around 11 a.m. on Sunday, Stevens arrived at the Perfecting Church in Detroit to sit in the pews not with her party's nominee for Senate, but with his Republican opponent.

Soon after, she announced she was endorsing Rogers, who she once slammed as a rubber stamp for President Trump.

"Abdul made very clear that I was not welcome in his campaign, that he didn't need me there, that he didn't want me there," Stevens told reporters standing alongside Rogers. "I did endorse the Democratic nominee, and he chose to close this campaign by saying I wasn't welcome."

The August primary divided Michigan Democrats, exposing fractures over Israel and the direction of the party broadly. El-Sayed defeated Stevens by about 1 percent of the vote.

Some voters who backed Stevens have remained hesitant about El-Sayed. But most prominent Democrats rallied around him after the primary and did so again on Sunday. Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., called Stevens' actions on Sunday a "despicable betrayal."

Retaining Michigan's open Senate seat is seen by Democrats as critical to retaking the chamber. Polls have suggested the race could be narrowly decided.

During the primary, El-Sayed and Stevens frequently clashed over rhetoric and positions related to Israel. El-Sayed is a vocal critic. Stevens a staunch defender. El-Sayed regularly scorched Stevens for the millions of dollars in spending on her behalf by the pro-Israel lobbying group AIPAC in his calls to curb the influence of special interest money in politics.

Stevens, and some Jewish voters, also raised concerns about statements El-Sayed and some of his supporters, like left-wing streamer Hasan Piker who campaigned with El-Sayed, have made.

In August, after Piker implied Jews' support for Israel puts them at risk of personal violence, El-Sayed did not disavow him, but told reporters Piker did not speak for the campaign.

And after a March attack on a Detroit-area synagogue preschool by a man who had family killed by Israeli strikes in Lebanon, El-Sayed said violence is a cycle and that "hurt people, hurt people." He later apologized, saying the timing was inappropriate.

In recent days, Stevens had started to ramp up criticism of El-Sayed, saying he was not doing enough to reassure uncertain Jewish and Black voters who supported her during the primary.

On Wednesday, Stevens' fiance, Detroit-area lawyer Jason Turkish, said he planned to vote for Rogers. In a TV appearance, Stevens said she was still supporting the Democratic nominee.

It was around this time that Rep. Debbie Dingell, the dean of Michigan's Democratic House delegation, was trying to organize a joint appearance between El-Sayed and Stevens. The idea was for the two to attend a Sunday Souls to the Polls event at a church in Detroit alongside Rep. Jim Clyburn, D-S.C., a prominent Black Democrat.

But on Friday, Stevens set a condition for her participation — that El-Sayed reject a digital ad supporting him that blamed U.S. aid to Israel for high grocery prices and apologize for rhetoric she considered to be inflammatory and offensive. The ad was linked to Cenk Uygur, of the left-wing outlet Young Turks, not El-Sayed's campaign.

Michigan Democratic Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed speaks with reporters before a Souls to the Polls campaign event at Northend Church of God in Christ, Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026, in Detroit. Julia Demaree Nikhinson/AP hide caption

Tensions continued to ratchet up, with El-Sayed releasing a statement of his own saying Stevens, "seems intent on relitigating a primary that ended three months ago." Dingell says she told Stevens not to attend the event because the turmoil had become a distraction.

El-Sayed's campaign said he had nothing to do with the ad, and on Saturday, told reporters he did not agree with its content. Asked by reporters if he wanted Stevens to come to the church with him, El-Sayed said, "I would rather not distract from the key issues of the campaign."

Through Saturday night, Stevens was telling Democrats including Dingell and Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., that she would not rescind her endorsement of El-Sayed. Still many Democrats were not sure whether Stevens would come Sunday morning. In the end, she did not and appeared instead with Rogers, at another church roughly six miles away.

"I'm not trying to harm anyone," Stevens told reporters. "I've worked really hard for Democrats. I'm not turning my back on Democrats. It is one vote. When you see repeated hate, it's not acceptable. I still love the party."

Rogers said Stevens "showed courage and bravery in putting Michigan first."

The original event went on without her, as El-Sayed joined Clyburn and other prominent Black politicians and faith leaders to rally voters on the last day of early in-person voting. El-Sayed told reporters outside that he does not think about Stevens.

"None of this matters," he said. "You know what matters. You can go, like, 15 minutes that way, or 15 minutes that way, or 15 minutes that way. And there are families who are preparing to send their kids to schools tomorrow that look like they did 50 years ago."

Elected Michigan Democrats' rallied around El-Sayed. The Michigan Democratic Party Chair, Curtis Hertel, wrote on X that Stevens was no longer a member nor would she be in the future.

Stevens' chief of staff for eight years, Justin German, wrote in a statement that he and many of Stevens' congressional staff resigned Sunday morning, saying they are "shocked and heartbroken" by Stevens' decision to endorse Rogers.

"I have severe concerns about AES' rhetoric redounding dangerously on Jews," Michigan State Rep. Noah Arbit wrote on X, referring to El-Sayed. "I have shared those concerns with him & publicly. But the sane answer for anyone who claims to care about democracy, rule of law, minorities cannot be to embrace Rogers' disgusting, dangerous Islamophobia."

Rogers and other Republicans, including Trump, have focused their closing argument painting El-Sayed as dangerous and un-American, accusing him without evidence of ties to the Muslim Brotherhood, Hamas and Hezbollah, which El-Sayed has forcefully denied.

At rallies, El-Sayed says Rogers is using his faith and his name to distract from soaring prices. El-Sayed would be the first Muslim American elected to the U.S. Senate.

In a CNN interview Sunday night, Stevens struggled to explain why she reversed after telling colleagues she would not.

"I was shocked when I saw what happened on Sunday," Dingell told NPR in an interview on Monday. "I'm worried for her. I was very sad to watch her interview last night."

There are Democrats who did not vote for El-Sayed in the primary and struggled with whether to vote for him this fall. At a farmer's market and Halloween festival in Oakland County, retiree Jan Jacobs, a member of the temple that experienced the truck attack in March, says she voted for Democrats across the board, but left the Senate race blank.

"I understand we want to have Democrats represented in the Senate, I'm just not sure if this is the right person for me," she said on Saturday.

Michelle Boddy, who wants a Democratic Congress to check Trump, said while she wasn't initially sold on El-Sayed, she got there. "I know how Obama feels about democracy and the fact that he endorsed him gave me a sense of yes, that's the person to vote for."

El-Sayed has been making the case that he can win by harnessing anger at establishment politicians to sway young voters, disengaged voters and former Trump voters.

At an early vote location in Macomb County, Jason Kerr said he voted for Trump in 2024. Now he is voting for El-Sayed. He is frustrated with how Trump has handled his second term.

"I'm just surprised by some of the decisions that have been made," Kerr said. "Making statements that inflation is not that bad. That gets difficult for me, when messaging doesn't match what's actually going on."

Will this latest drama with Stevens sway significant numbers of undecided voters? El-Sayed says it will not. "I'm a gut politician, I think it's had a reverse impact," Dingell told NPR.

But this was not the closing discussion his campaign was hoping for.

The turmoil, though, has re-exposed fault lines within the party that have been deepening all year — not just over Israel, but about the party's identity going forward.

Even if Democrats reclaim one or both chambers of Congress on Tuesday, that won't resolve these questions. So this debate will likely continue especially as Democrats prepare to choose a presidential nominee in 2028. One of the earliest primary states to weigh in will be Michigan.

#5937. Do you know signs of a stroke? BE FAST are the words to remember

Sen. Chris Murphy, D-Conn., speaks to reporters in March 2026, in Washington. Murphy suffered a stroke on Saturday and is expected to recover. Allison Robbert/AP hide caption

Sen. Chris Murphy, D-Conn., suffered a stroke on Saturday. His prognosis is excellent, according to his office. His case illustrates the importance of knowing the warning signs of a stroke.

The American Stroke Association says following the six letters in the acronym BE FAST — can help save stroke victims and prevent permanent brain damage.

Murphy, 53, had the "A" for "arm weakness" — he felt numbness in his arm while he was at his son's hockey game, according to a statement from his staff. And they said he acted quickly to get care — that's the "T" for "time."

Being seen by medical professionals as soon as possible — be fast! — gives people the best shot at survival and full recovery.

Here are all six of the stroke-spotting signs in the BE FAST acronym, according to the American Stroke Association:

Dizzyness, trouble walking or a sudden loss of balance are all signs that a stroke may be occurring.

Is the person having trouble with their vision? Blurred vision, double vision or loss of sight in one eye are important symptoms.

Ask the person to smile. Is their smile uneven? Numbness or drooping on one side of the face is a symptom.

Numbness in one arm, as Murphy had, is a symptom. Weakness is too. Ask the person to raise both arms. Does one arm drift down?

Trouble talking is another common symptom. Can the person repeat a simple sentence? Is their speech slurred or hard to understand?

If you or someone you are with is experiencing these symptoms, call 911 as soon as you can. Getting care quickly is very important for survival and to minimize long-term effects.

A stroke happens when blood flow to or within the brain is interrupted. It can happen when an artery is blocked or when a blood vessel in the brain bursts. Brain tissue that is deprived of oxygen can be permanently damaged, resulting in long-term disability or death.

Other important symptoms of a stroke are a severe headache, confusion and numbness on one side of the body.

Stroke is the #4 leading cause of death in the U.S., killing more than 162,000 people in 2023, according to the American Heart Association.

#5931. SolSmart celebrates 10 years of helping local governments go solar

SolSmart, a national program that helps local governments remove barriers to solar energy adoption, today marks its tenth anniversary. Since 2016, SolSmart has helped over 600 local governments and regional organizations cut red tape, increase energy choice for residents and strengthen community energy resilience. SolSmart is a free technical assistance program supported by the U.S. Department of Energy and co-led by the Interstate Renewable Energy Council (IREC) and the International City/County Management Association (ICMA).

SolSmart offers free expert technical assistance to help local governments adopt nationally recognized best practices to advance solar energy adoption. With electricity costs rising rapidly and a sixth of U.S. households behind on their utility bills, SolSmart helps local governments reduce energy costs for residents. Solar projects can help meet rising electricity demands at lower costs than other energy sources, but only if local processes like permitting and inspection allow them to come online efficiently and affordably. SolSmart enables this by helping communities streamline policies while maintaining safety and reliability.

Participating communities can earn graded designations by taking specific steps to reduce barriers to solar adoption, such as improving permitting and inspection practices and expanding information for residents. Designation provides national recognition of communities’ work to expand energy options for residents and businesses, while providing measurable benefits. Independent research, published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, found that SolSmart designation is associated with an 18–19% increase in solar capacity per month compared to non-designated communities.

“The city of Athens is proud to be one of the first communities in the country to pursue SolSmart designation,” said Steve Patterson mayor of Athens, Ohio. “When we first achieved Bronze designation in October 2017, we knew this was only the beginning of our residential sustainable energy story. Since then, we received SolSmart Gold designation in 2023 and, in 2025, became the first SolSmart Platinum community in Ohio, the 4th City in the country, and have added over 2 megawatts of solar capacity to our municipal facilities, with much more planned for the future. We continue to see a steady increase in solar installations in the City and know this trend will continue thanks to our SolSmart efforts.

More than 600 local governments across 44 states have received designations through the SolSmart program, in places such as Fort Worth, Texas, Iowa City, Iowa, and Olympia, Washington.

“Local governments are at the heart of clean energy adoption around the country. Policies on topics like permitting and inspection of solar installations can make or break residents’ access to solar,” said Brandy O’Quinn, program director of local initiatives at IREC. “When local governments take steps to remove unintended barriers to solar adoption, the results are significant.”

The SolSmart team is hosting a digital event on October 29 at 2 p.m. ET to share information about its 2026 SolSmart Accelerator.

“The SolSmart program provides free hands-on support to help hardworking local government staff align their local rules with national best practices that cut red tape while maintaining strong safety standards,” said Debra Perry, Clean Energy Program Director at ICMA. “As communities grapple with rising energy costs, increasing solar access can be a valuable way to support residents’ energy choices and advance affordability. We look forward to supporting many more communities, and invite interested local governments to access our free support.”

News item from the Interstate Renewable Energy Council

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5928. What Vance's midterm push reveals about his potential 2028 bid

US Vice President JD Vance listens to US President Donald Trump as he hosts Stephen Hubbard in the Oval Office of the White House in Washington, DC, on October 30, 2026. ALEX WROBLEWSKI/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

At a Thursday speech in Weatherford, Texas, Vice President JD Vance seemed to relish the opportunity to snipe at Democratic Senate candidate James Talarico.

"Now James Talarico has also said that he really has to grapple with his masculinity in addition to his whiteness," Vance said. "And you know, James, the good news is, there ain't a whole lot to grapple with," he finished to cheers from the crowd.

Vance gave the speech the same afternoon that President Trump spoke in Rock Hill, South Carolina, as part of his pre-midterm blitz of rallies. And while Vance's delivery was different from the president's – less borscht-belt comedian, more practiced politician – it contained plenty of MAGA DNA, from insulting a candidate's masculinity to promising a return of American greatness. It all gives a sense of what a Vance presidential pitch might look like once 2028 campaigns begin in earnest next year.

Vance paints a rosy picture of the America of his youth, in his speeches – in North Carolina this week, he invoked the "sense of pride and unity and fellowship" Americans had after September 11, 2001.

He added to that message in multiple speeches remembering his childhood football teams being sponsored by local small businesses.

"I distinctly remember looking in my old jerseys and seeing the names of the businesses that had sponsored those teams and realizing that most of the teams I played for, those businesses no longer existed," he said in Howell, Michigan. "They were casualties of politicians in a faraway place who didn't give a damn what happened to Michigan or Ohio, and it was us that paid the consequences."

It's a story about a Norman Rockwell vision of America corrupted by Washington elites. The story Vance tells is that his brand of populist politics can bring that back.

But it's not just about economics; Vance leans hard into the culture wars fought during the Trump years. In his stories about peewee football, Vance talks about racial harmony well before the advent of Black Lives Matter: "the children of working-class Black families and the children of working-class white families would play football together, would cheer with one another, would never worry about the color of their skin or who had more privilege," he told the crowd in North Carolina.

It amounts to a more traditional, more eloquent packaging of Trump's anti-woke MAGA message. However, Vance also is experimenting with crassness in his remarks, dropping the occasional swear word or telling the Texas crowd he was "sweating my balls off" at the podium.

The challenge for Vance right now is in promoting GOP candidates while the Republican president he serves is unpopular.

That challenge is particularly acute where the president's policies have directly caused economic woes. Vance doesn't tend to bring up the Iran War up in his stump speeches – a war that sent gas and diesel prices spiraling upward – but he does cast the gas prices caused by the Iran war as an unfortunate, recurring result of geopolitics:

"Remember in 2022 when Russia invaded Ukraine and gas prices skyrocketed? They skyrocketed, by the way, far higher than they are today," he told a Montana crowd. "They skyrocketed in 1979. They skyrocketed in 2001. They skyrocketed in 2007. They skyrocket every time some tin pot dictator decides to throw a tantrum."

Likewise, Vance has acknowledged the high price of beef, casting it as a problem of consolidation among meatpackers, while sidestepping that Trump's own policies have angered beef producers.

Interspersed with his campaign speeches this year have been appearances as the head of President Trump's anti-fraud task force, which has a goal of rooting out malfeasance in social safety net programs.

The task force provides Vance with both resume material and an opportunity to talk about his personal story.

"I worked as a cashier at a grocery store for a couple of years, this was my very first job, Dhillon Foods, probably 20 miles from where we're standing today," he told a crowd in Cincinnati last week. "I also had a lot of members of my family benefit from these food benefit programs provided by federal tax dollars."

Vance was first propelled onto the political scene as a result of his memoir about growing up in poor, rural southern Ohio.

His biography gives him some political tools that President Trump does not have, like the ability to talk personally about military service and economic difficulties.

However, crowds loved Trump not because of his biographical relatability but because of his bombast and unfiltered anger. The challenge for any would-be 2028 Republican candidate is figuring out how to follow Trump's singular, decade-plus-long performance, and what – if anything – can replace that in the hearts of GOP voters.

#5922. The Pedego Element 2 eBike — CleanTechnica Tested

Pedego was one of the earliest companies to see the promise of ebikes and to build them at scale. It sales model was based around developing retail stores hosting both sales and rental options for customers. It was a great option, as it let prospective buyers see Pedego’s various ebikes in person, rent a bike for a day, and even pick it up in person.

When ebikes moved into the mass market, Pedego was slow to adapt, and as a result, its $4,000 ebikes felt expensive and uncompetitive. Its retail stores were compelling and it felt like the company had a solid foothold in the market, so we were excited when Pedego reached out about the more affordable $2,195 Pedego Element 2.

Disclaimer: Pedego provided the Element 2 to the author for the purposes of this review.

We were excited to put the model through its paces to see how the company had improved its bikes for the masses and to see if they had cut any corners to achieve the lower pricing that they’re now able to offer. Assembly for the Pedego Element 2 was straightforward. It needed pedals, the handlebars to be mounted to the stem, and a few other odds and ends. But all told, it was very easy and straightforward to get it from the box to being street ready.

Hitting the streets, we found the low-step frame to be extremely comfortable, with an attractive colorway in its satin finish paint. Ours came in a sand color that’s extremely attractive and looks modern with the black accents on the bike.

The Element 2 has an integrated rack to which Pedego fitted a set of panniers for us that make it just the ultimate cargo bike right out of the box. We loaded this up with some groceries on a neighborhood run to pick up some food for dinner and found it to be more than capable of both getting down the hill loaded up with groceries and, obviously back to the house.

Taking it on a run down to the beach, the Pedego is a breeze and feels very confident, especially with its oversized knobby tires. Their larger volume gives it a bit of extra cushion and complements the front suspension fork, making for a very comfortable and confident ride whether you’re on the road or on a short bit of dirt.

On that note, don’t let the knobby tires and suspension fork fool you into thinking this is an all-terrain machine. While it can handle the occasional stint on the dirt, it was never designed to excel in off-road adventures.

When it came time to tackle the hills, the Pedego’s torque sensor and high torque motor make it more than capable of pushing even my oversized frame up some of the most difficult hills you’re likely to find on a road bike. On the way back down, we tested the front and rear hydraulic disc brakes and found them to be extremely responsive even under the most aggressive braking.

On the lower end of the spectrum, the torque sensor makes it very comfortable to ride and lets you dial in exactly the amount of assist you want, no matter what you’re carrying or what terrain you’re riding on.

The bike features an integrated color display that makes it easy to see what’s going on, whether you’re looking for your pedal assist mode or your current speed. It also features a real-time chart that displays just how much power the motor is putting out in real time so that you can see just how much help the motor is giving you as you’re moving along.

It comes with integrated front and rear lights, which provide plenty of confidence and light if the sun goes down before you’re finished with your adventures or if you start your adventures before the sun comes up. They’re also fantastic integrated options just to add a bit more visibility to the bike while riding around town.

Pedego is offering this bike in a number of colorways that make it flexible enough to be used for just about any gender or age. The low-step frame also makes it a great option for a wide range of rider heights and sizes — anywhere from teens up through taller adults won’t have much of an issue feeling comfortable on this bike with just a few adjustments to the seat and angle of the handlebars.

The Pedego Element 2 is a fantastic platform that really ushers in a new chapter for Pedego to dominate the world of electric bikes in a much more competitive scheme than they saw when they first entered or even created the e-bike market all those years ago.

It features tons of great accessories, like the integrated rack to which you can add just about anything, that make it a great base platform for whatever adventures you have in store for your day, week, or life.

At just $2,195, it’s a great value from a brand that has been proven, meaning they know what components to put on it, offer fantastic support, and even have a few retail shops around the country that you can stop in at.

As with most e-bikes, you can also just take it to your local bike shop for a tune-up, as most are comfortable working on e-bikes and most of the components you’ll need service on aren’t brand specific. It’ll just be simple things like adjusting the chain, tuning up the brakes, etc., which is common to any bike.

For more information about the Pedego Element 2 or to buy one for yourself, head to its official online home.

Live intentionally, make conscious decisions, love more, act responsibly, play. The more you know, the less you need. As an activist investor, Kyle owns long term stock holdings in Tesla and Rivian.

#5921. When it comes to election pressure, this year's Georgia or Michigan could be anywhere

A sign directs residents to a polling place in Dearborn, Mich., on Aug. 4. Scott Olson/Getty Images hide caption

There's one big difference, Tina Barton says, between running an election in a battleground state and a non-battleground.

It's what happens when there are mistakes.

"If a human error happens, it's easily explained in the press and it's not a story," Barton says. "You have a human error in a battleground state and all of a sudden it can become a massive conspiracy."

In 2020, she oversaw elections as city clerk of Rochester Hills, Mich. On election night, her office mistakenly sent an unofficial absentee vote file twice because the first one didn't initially appear to send.

Such mistakes happen every election cycle in a national process run by tens of thousands of human beings. A machine isn't plugged in. A poll worker sleeps through their alarm clock and doesn't open a precinct on time. Someone gets handed the wrong ballot.

In Barton's case, the issue didn't impact actual votes, and it was corrected in the tally the following day.

The chairwoman of the Republican National Committee accused her office of improperly giving votes to Democrats, and Barton received a rush of death threats, including a voicemail saying she deserved a "throat to the knife" because she had "frauded out America of a real election."

More than half a decade later, for better or worse, election officials in swing states like Michigan, Georgia and Arizona are more used to that level of pressure and scrutiny.

In this year's midterms, however, because the races that will decide control of the U.S. House and Senate are spread around the country, local officials in places like Iowa, Alaska, New York and Kansas may find themselves in the national spotlight for the first time.

Chris McGinn is executive director of the Texas Association of County Election Officials. In an interview with NPR earlier this year, he noted that Texas has been a reliable Republican state for the better part of 50 years, which has meant fewer competitive elections and less pressure on voting officials in federal election cycles.

A huge part of McGinn's job has been preparing clerks for the possibility that could change this year, considering the U.S. Senate race there could decide the balance of power in that chamber. The Republican nominee for that seat sought to help President Trump overturn his 2020 election loss.

"We're still in a state of not thinking it can happen to us — 'That happens in other places,'" McGinn said. "One of the things that I jokingly but realistically preach to our members is we don't want to be Florida in 2000."

On a whole, the threat environment for voting officials seems calmer than it did in the period following 2020.

Wendy Link — the supervisor of elections for Palm Beach County, Fla., a county that includes part of a toss-up congressional district this year — says her office still regularly gets "love letters," as she calls them, but she's not as worried for own safety as she has been at different points in the job.

"We get emails saying, you know, 'We're going to send our army to arrest you, you'll be spending your time in Guantanamo Bay,'" Link told NPR. "So do I love reading some of the emails? No. Do I think some of them are clearly intended to intimidate me? Yes. But am I reaching out and asking for a security detail? No."

But Barton, the former Michigan clerk, worries it could all escalate again if the midterms are close. She's now vice president of the Committee for Safe and Secure Elections, which helps local officials game-plan and prepare for different worst case scenarios.

"It can happen whether you're a red state or a blue state. Controversy can find you," Barton said. "All it takes is some ballot equipment going down, a problem with ballots jamming, a website going down."

Researchers who study election misinformation say most false narratives usually emerge from real events like those Barton mentioned, which are then seized on by influencers or politicians to falsely imply they're happening intentionally or at a wider scale than they actually are.

This year CSSE doubled the number of trainings the organization led when compared to 2024 — a sign of new concerns around the Trump administration's actions in elections but also an expanded understanding of what it means to be in a battleground state.

When Barton talks to officials in competitive districts, her message is simple.

"I've told them all, you better be on your game," Barton said.
