#5918. The Need for Maintaining Renewable Energy Directive Stability in the Post-2030 Energy Framework

NGOs, civil society and industry call on the European Commission to refrain from reopening the RED legislative framework during the negotiation of the post-2030 energy architecture.

Ahead of the Energy Council of 29 October, a group of NGOs, civil society actors and companies are calling on the European Commission and Member States to commit to the stability of the Renewable Energy Directive (RED) and refrain from reopening this legislative framework during the negotiation of the post-2030 energy architecture.

The RED is a European success story. It remarkably drove the deployment of renewables in the last decades. Not reopening the RED is the most rigorous and timely pathway to ensure regulatory stability and not amending the rules is the best simplification exercise.

The Renewable Energy Directive has been revised four times in as many years. While each revision addressed legitimate policy developments, the cumulative effect is that the new provisions included since 2023 are still yet to be implemented in most EU Member States.

This regulatory instability undermines the investment certainty essential to meeting Europe’s climate and energy security objectives. Investment in renewable energy and associated infrastructure depends on predictable regulatory frameworks. Financial institutions require clarity on targets, permitting procedures, grid and network access rules, auction mechanisms to underwrite long-term deployment and effective power purchase and heat purchase agreement frameworks.

What Europe needs is rigorous implementation of these rules across all Member States, not legislative revision. To accelerate this, the Commission should:

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5917. 6 things to watch for on election night

People walk by a sign for voting on November 1, 2026, in Detroit, Michigan. Michigan has become a focal point in the upcoming midterm elections, with a close race between Republican Senate candidate, former U.S. Rep. Mike Rogers (R-MI), and Democratic candidate Abdul El-Sayed that could decide who controls the chamber. Spencer Platt/Getty Images hide caption

It can be hard to know what to focus on in midterm elections. There are so many races, from elections for governor and senator to the House and state legislatures. For a better idea of how the night might go to see which party might control Congress, here are six things to watch:

There are two states, Indiana and Kentucky, that will see results in the 6 p.m. ET hour. And then there's Florida, which is split between the Eastern and Central time zones. But most of the state is on Eastern time. So lots of House race results will be coming in during the 7 p.m. ET hour, even if statewide contests like the Senate and governors races won't be called at the earliest until 8 p.m. ET.

Some races to watch for early indicators of what might come later in the evening — Indiana's 5th Congressional District, Kentucky's 6th and Florida's 22nd and 27th. Indiana's 5th and Kentucky's 6th are seats held by Republicans that are more competitive now than at the beginning of the cycle. If these are close or tipping to Democrats, it could be a sign of low Republican turnout and a bigger Democratic wave.

Florida's 22nd is held by a Democrat but redrawn to tip in Republicans' favor. Trump won Florida's 27th by double-digits so this heavily Latino district could be an indicator of how this key subgroup votes.

Independents are a key swing group, but they are also a lower-propensity voting group in midterms.

It used to be that Republicans had to win a bigger share of independents before the Trump era. Mitt Romney, for example, won 55% of independents in 2012 but lost the presidential election. But there's been a political realignment in this country with blue-collar white voters trending toward Trump, fewer people identifying as Democrats and better-educated white voters shifting toward Democrats.

So in the decade since Trump has been on the political scene, Republicans have done well when they win at least 46% of independents. When they haven't, they've lost.

The Michigan Senate race is important for control of the Senate. If Republicans flip it, the magic number for Democrats goes from four to five, meaning Democrats would have to win five seats held by Republicans to win control of the Senate.

For Abdul El-Sayed (D) to win in Michigan, a key is going to be Black voters in Wayne County. If he runs up the score in and around Detroit, he has a path to victory, but if he doesn't, it becomes that much harder — and it's been an area of vulnerability for him.

During the Trump era, Democrats have needed 68% or more in Wayne County to win Michigan unless, like Sen. Elissa Slotkin in 2024, they can overperform in the suburbs.

Wayne County Democratic margins2024 Slotkin 63% Win (11% share)2024 Harris 62% Loss2020 Biden 68% Win (12%)2020 Peters 68% Win2018 Stabenow 69% Win (15%)2016 Clinton 66% Loss (13%)

Voters 18 to 29 see a drastic drop off in turnout between presidential and midterm elections. During presidential races since 1992, 43% of registered young voters cast ballots. But in midterms, that falls to 21%, just 1 in 5 who show up at the polls.

But they are crucial to Democratic success. In the last 20 years, when Democrats won this group by more than 20 points, they have done well, winning presidencies or making gains in midterms. The only exception was 2022, but arguably, the strong margin Democrats won with young voters that year, helped them stem what could have been a much larger Republican tide. That year, Democrats lost nine House seats, but gained a Senate seat.

Democratic margin with voters 18-29, election result2024 +11, Trump wins presidency2022 +28, Republicans win House, Democrats gain Senate seat2020 +25, Biden wins presidency2018 +35, Democrats win House2016 +19, Trump wins presidency2014 +11, Republicans expand House majority, win Senate2012 +23, Obama wins re-election2010 +17, Republicans win House2008 +34, Obama wins presidency2006 +22, Democrats win House

Given past polling errors in Texas that underestimated the Republican vote, there's a healthy skepticism that Democrat James Talarico's margin of error lead in polling averages is enough to win.

There was lots of enthusiasm in 2018 for Beto O'Rourke's (D) 2018 bid against an unpopular Republican senator in Ted Cruz, but Cruz still won, although by less than 3 percentage points. No Democrat has won statewide in Texas since 1994, and no Democrat has been elected to the Senate from Texas in 38 years.

For it to actually happen in this state Trump won by 14 points in 2024, two groups are key – Republicans and Latinos. Ken Paxton (R) is unpopular. Republican Gov. Greg Abbot is also facing a tight race, but he's thought to be in better shape than Paxton. In 2018, Cruz won about 400,000 fewer votes than Abbott, but Cruz still won by more than 200,000 votes. So watch if the difference between Abbott and Paxton is more than 400,000 for Talarico.

Voters cast their ballots in Anchorage, Alaska on November 1, 2026. Christian Moorman/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

The second part of Talarico's path to victory runs through South Texas. Statewide, O'Rourke won 64% of Latinos in 2018, and they were 26% of the electorate, according to exit polls. If Talarico is north of both of those, that's how he wins the seat. Talarico would need to improve on O'Rourke's totals in these Latino-heavy counties: Cameron 63%, Hidalgo 69%, Webb 71%, Nueces 51%. As a bonus, watch the fast-growing and heavily populated Dallas suburbs – Collin 47%, Denton 46%, Tarrant 51%.

Because of the political realignment that's taken place in the Trump era, watch how people identify in exit polls.

The percentage of those saying they were Democrats hit a low in 2024 at 31%. That trend is happening as the base of the Democratic Party has become more liberal and more disaffected with their own leadership. This year, though, Democrats were among the highest in enthusiasm levels, according to the latest NPR poll, but they are likely going to have to be far higher than 31% as a share of the electorate to do well.

Democrats as a share of the electorate2024 31%2022 33% 2020 37%2018 37%2016 37%2014 35%2012 38%2010 39%2008 39%

That's quite possible given the opposition to Trump in this election is sky high – 53% of likely voters in the latest NPR poll said they strongly disapproved of the job he's doing. That is very likely to be a unifying force for the left in this election. As the saying goes, the enemy of my enemy is my friend.

Along these lines, it's going to be important to watch races like the Michigan Senate race and the contest for Michigan's 7th Congressional District, both of which have very progressive Democratic candidates trying to win in purple places. Democratic unity – and the party's ideological future – is going to be a key storyline of the 2028 presidential primary and could define the American left for years to come.

#5913. China Steps Back From Promoting Second-Life EV Batteries

China spent much of the 2010s electrifying urban bus fleets while most countries were still testing whether battery-electric buses could replace diesel. By the end of 2025, it had 551,400 new-energy urban buses, representing 87.5% of the fleet. That early success has brought China to a stage other countries will encounter later, when large numbers of batteries reach the end of their automotive service and owners must decide what to do with them.

In 2021, Chinese policy encouraged companies to give retired EV batteries a second life in stationary energy storage, backup power and charging infrastructure. The idea was attractive. A battery that had lost enough capacity to become less useful in a vehicle could still store substantial electricity, potentially avoiding the manufacture of another battery.

China has now changed its regulatory approach. New rules effective in April 2026 repealed the earlier cascade-utilization framework. A July 30 announcement from the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology withdrew special recognition for cascade-utilization companies and directed industry associations to discontinue promotional activities associated with the category.

Some reporting has described this as China banning second-life batteries. That misrepresents the change. Retired vehicle batteries can still be used in suitable applications when the resulting products meet the applicable standards. Reuse in electric bicycles and other restricted applications is prohibited. The ministry’s stated concern is the safety of inadequately tested, reassembled batteries with varying remaining capacity and inconsistent cell condition.

China’s experience deserves attention because battery retirement is becoming an industrial activity rather than a small collection of pilot projects. Electric buses have been operating for years, battery manufacturers have expanded production, and companies have invested heavily in recovering materials from retired packs. The assumptions that looked sensible when batteries were expensive and early electric fleets were young are now being tested against practical experience.

There is also an economic question beyond the regulatory change. New lithium iron phosphate batteries have become much cheaper, while old packs differ in age, condition, design and operating history. Testing, repackaging, controls and warranties can consume the savings from reusing cells. A second-life installation has to compete with new products designed for the same application.

That does not eliminate useful reuse opportunities. Operators with known battery histories and suitable stationary applications may have advantages that companies buying mixed retired packs do not. Product quality and economics will determine how much of the anticipated second-life market develops.

#5912. Two economists studied California's billionaire tax. They reached wildly different conclusions

An attendee holds a sign before the start of a rally led by US Senator Bernie Sanders, Independent of Vermont, during the campaign kickoff for the California Billionaire Tax Act at The Wiltern in Los Angeles, February 18, 2026. Sanders is seeking the support of California voters for a November's ballot initiative of an emergency tax on billionaires to help the state's healthcare system. (Photo by Patrick T. Fallon / AFP via Getty Images) PATRICK T. FALLON/AFP via Getty Images/AFP hide caption

In 1978, California voters approved Proposition 13, a historic tax revolt that became an opening salvo in the Reagan revolution and its national movement to slash taxes. Now, Californians are voting on Proposition 40, which could prove to be just as revolutionary — but in the opposite direction: it would create America's first-ever wealth tax targeted at billionaires.

As Election Day approaches, economists have been duking it out over this proposed wealth tax. It could provide a sort of nerdy preview of a coming national fight over taxing the superrich. Will this wealth tax help rein in runaway inequality and fill the state's coffers — or will it convince billionaires to fire up their private jets and set up residence in mansions outside the state? Some megabillionaires have already fled California since the tax was proposed.

On one side of this debate is economist Gabriel Zucman, a leading advocate for taxing the superrich who splits his time between UC Berkeley and the Paris School of Economics. Zucman helped design the national wealth tax proposals of Senators Elizabeth Warren and Bernie Sanders when they were running for president (which I covered back then in the Planet Money newsletter and podcast). He is the author of a new book with a blunt title: We Need To Tax Billionaires. Naturally, he supports California's wealth tax, and he co-authored an influential analysis estimating it could raise about $100 billion in revenue.

"If California says, 'We can tax billionaires. We can tax the wealthiest, most powerful people in the world,' it will be the beginning of not only a broader U.S.-wide movement, ultimately paving the way for a federal wealth tax — but I think it will be the beginning of an international movement," Zucman tells Planet Money.

On the other side of the debate is Stanford economist and Hoover Institution senior fellow Joshua Rauh. His analysis, co-authored with Benjamin Jaros, comes to a wildly different conclusion: the tax could lead to an exodus of billionaires out of California, damaging the state's economy and blowing a hole in the income tax revenue the state already collects from billionaires.

" It would not raise $100 billion for the state," Rauh argues. Instead, he estimates, this tax could actually end up draining California's state coffers. " We calculate that Proposition 40 would cost the state of California $39 billion."

It's a classic Berkeley-Stanford rivalry, but the stakes are much higher than a football game. California voters are deciding whether to pursue a historic wealth tax experiment, which could influence tax policy around the nation, even the world. Indeed, the superrich, fearing that this wealth tax could spark a widespread movement, have poured a staggering $333 million into a group trying to defeat this proposition, according to a recent report in The New York Times.

The wealth tax Prop 40 proposes is pretty straightforward: a one-time, 5% tax on the net worth of billionaires, with some assets exempted. It would apply retroactively to any billionaire who was a resident of California on January 1, 2026. The money would be earmarked mostly for the health care of low-income Californians, to help offset federal cuts to Medicaid.

A wealth tax is sort of like a property tax, except it extends beyond real estate and taxes a whole range of assets: stocks, superyachts, Basquiat paintings, dinosaur skeletons, Mercedes G Wagons, samurai swords, basically everything a rich person owns. Of course, figuring out what all a billionaire's stuff is worth — especially assets that rarely change hands — can get complicated. Do you value a Picasso or a yacht by the price originally paid, what it might sell for today, by how much they've insured it for? And so on. That said, most of the wealth held by California billionaires is in publicly traded stocks, whose value is easy to determine.

Zucman, together with Jasper Boll and Emmanuel Saez, released an analysis of the California billionaire class and this proposed wealth tax.

"California billionaires' wealth has reached $2.3 trillion by mid-2026, as large as all the annual income reported on income tax returns of all Californians," they wrote. They found that California billionaire wealth had exploded by 144% between 2023 and 2025, thanks largely to the AI boom. "Over the longer run, the real wealth of California's billionaire class — the top 0.001% richest households — has been multiplied by 25 from 1982 to 2026, while average real family income in California has only doubled."

Zucman says a crucial problem with the existing tax system in California, America, and beyond is that the superrich have been seeing their wealth snowball bigger and bigger while paying relatively little in income taxes. Billionaires can structure their finances to generate little or no taxable income, making the income tax rate "totally irrelevant," he argues. Zucman believes a wealth tax will help address this, thereby helping to fight rising inequality, counteract the political power of "the oligarchs," and raise much-needed funds for everyone else.

Zucman, Boll, and Saez estimated that the state wealth tax proposed by Prop 40 could raise "about $100 billion, with comparatively minor impacts on income tax revenue." They arrive at that figure by taking 5% of their $2.3 trillion estimate of billionaire wealth, then reducing the projected revenue by 10% to account for avoidance, evasion, and the possibility that some billionaires ended their California residency before January 1, 2026.

Rauh argues that this 10% reduction to account for billionaires avoiding the tax is too optimistic.

As we've previously reported in the Planet Money newsletter, wealth taxes have been tried before around the world and they have had a dismal track record. Even Zucman, a wealth tax champion, writes in his new book that past wealth taxes "by and large, have been abject failures."

Zucman and his coterie of other (typically French) economists have spent a lot of time studying why past wealth taxes failed and have sought to design new ones that address those failures.

One crucial problem was that wealth taxes tended to have loopholes that allowed the rich to shelter their assets, making these taxes a leaky bucket. For example, before France scrapped its wealth tax back in 2018, it exempted qualifying business assets, including shares in companies their owners ran. That, Zucman says, was a big loophole that drastically reduced the amount of revenue it raised.

Another crucial problem — which is at the center of the debate on this California wealth tax proposal — is that some superrich people move to avoid the tax. France became a cautionary tale, with critics often pointing to estimates that tens of thousands of millionaires left while its wealth tax was in place. However, how much of that exodus was caused by the wealth tax remains disputed.

Zucman acknowledges that the flight of rich people has been an issue with past wealth taxes, but he also argues that this problem has been overblown. He, for example, pointed to a recent working paper about Norway, which has had a wealth tax since 1892. It found that recent increases to this existing wealth tax, alongside other tax increases on the rich, drove at most 100 rich people to leave. But, it found, those departures didn't do much to dent the resulting gains in wealth-tax revenue. That said, Norway is a nation, not a state, and moving to another country is a bigger deal than, say, moving from California to Nevada.

To avoid the failures of past wealth taxes, Prop 40's designers have tried to plug potential leaks. Unlike France's old wealth tax, it would tax business holdings, including shares in companies that billionaires run. And by making it a one-time, retroactive tax tied to where billionaires lived on January 1, 2026, they hope to prevent billionaires from escaping the bill by simply moving away.

However, whether these provisions will actually work is at the heart of the debate.

Rauh recently released a competing analysis with a provocative conclusion: instead of raising $100 billion, as Zucman and the measure's supporters claim, this wealth tax could ultimately cost the state billions.

First, Rauh argues, at least seven famous billionaires had already left California before the January 1, 2026, residency cutoff, putting their fortunes beyond the tax's reach. Those billionaires are Larry Page, Sergey Brin, Peter Thiel, Don Hankey, Steven Spielberg, Travis Kalanick, and David Sacks.

Those departures alone, Rauh says, reduced the potential revenue from this wealth tax by "around $30 billion."

Zucman, however, disputes that those fortunes are beyond California's reach. He says the legal experts he trusts believe it's unlikely that any of these billionaires successfully ended their California tax residency before the cutoff. Ending your residency, he argues, takes more than a last-minute move across state lines. Prop 40 was designed with this in mind. It was announced in late October 2025, giving billionaires only about two months to establish residency elsewhere before January 1, 2026. Talk about a New Year's Day hangover.

Take Google co-founder Sergey Brin. Sure, he's moved just across the border to the Nevada side of Lake Tahoe — and meanwhile spent more than a hundred million dollars trying to defeat Prop 40. But, Zucman says, he still frequently commutes to work in Silicon Valley. A quick change of address, he argues, doesn't necessarily mean you've left California behind for tax purposes. Rauh counters that Brin, backed by what we can only assume are the best accountants and lawyers, has likely succeeded in establishing Nevada residency. He stands by his assumption that Brin and the others are beyond the tax's reach.

Like much of the debate over how much this tax will ultimately raise, this dispute has as much to do with the law as it does economics.

Which leads us to the second reason Rauh believes this proposed wealth tax will raise much less money than its supporters hope: the courts. Billionaires, armed with the best lawyers money can buy, will mount a slew of constitutional challenges to this tax. And given this wealth tax's novelty — and the Supreme Court's conservative majority — there's a real risk that at least part of Prop 40 could be struck down.

For example, Prop 40's controversial retroactive provision. It would tax some billionaires based on where they used to live. Rauh argues that could violate the Constitution's due process protections, although the Supreme Court has upheld other retroactive taxes in the past. The tax also reaches beyond California's borders, targeting wealth held around the world, which could create legal issues. Rauh also raises legal concerns about how the tax would value privately held companies. He says that venture capital deals can put a price on a company that overstates what it's actually worth. Taxing an owner's share of that inflated value, he argues, could create constitutional problems.

The architects of Prop 40 designed the law with legal challenges in mind, and it has all sorts of fallback provisions meant to keep the tax working if the courts strike down any part of it. But how much revenue would survive those legal battles? This is another big disagreement between Rauh and Zucman.

Rauh and Jaros estimate a big range of possible outcomes. But their central estimate, which has generated plenty of headlines, cuts the remaining projected revenue in half to account for legal uncertainty. That is a strong assumption — not a certainty about how the courts will rule — and Zucman disputes it.

After accounting for billionaire departures and the anticipated legal challenges, Rauh and Jaros estimate the wealth tax would bring in only "around $30 billion."

But that's not the end of their gloomy estimate. Because Rauh and Jaros expect even more billionaires to flee the state if Prop 40 passes, taking their existing income tax payments with them.

Zucman questions that prediction: if moving won't necessarily get billionaires out of paying this retroactive, one-time tax, why would they leave? They're already on the hook, and this isn't an annual tax.

Rauh's answer: because they will rationally fear that this won't be the last wealth tax that California passes. The sense that California is becoming a hostile environment for the superrich could drive departures even if another wealth tax never materializes, he argues. After all, Zucman and many other supporters of Prop 40 see this one-time tax as only the beginning. Zucman advocates for an annual wealth tax in his book, and he told us, yeah, he doesn't really want this to be a one-time tax.

The way Rauh sees it, Prop 40 trades a stream of income-tax revenue in the future for a more immediate payout of wealth-tax revenue today. His calculation assumes those income tax losses continue indefinitely. All of which is how he and Jaros arrive at their shocking conclusion: "On net, the state loses $39 billion in expectation because of this tax," Rauh argues.

Zucman objects that their calculation factors in future income tax losses from billionaires fleeing feared wealth taxes — but none of the revenue those hypothetical wealth taxes would bring in.

"That's absurdly one-sided," Zucman says. "You can't just count the loss and not the gain."

Zucman also argues that the income tax losses from departing billionaires would be smaller than Rauh predicts. Using public financial disclosures, his team estimates that some of the richest billionaires — including Larry Page and Sergey Brin — pay relatively little in income taxes.

Rauh defends their calculation, arguing it follows standard procedures for scoring tax proposals. "It would be absurd to count revenue from not yet proposed taxes," he says. He also argues that the public financial disclosures Zucman and his team focus on give an incomplete picture of how much billionaires pay in income taxes.

How much income tax revenue California could lose depends not just on what billionaires currently pay, but also on how many leave if Prop 40 passes. And the state has a powerful magnet that could keep them there: its thriving tech ecosystem.

For years, conservatives have been painting California as a progressive basket case, predicting that Democratic mismanagement would plunge its economy down the tubes and send rich people fleeing. Yet the state has become the epicenter of the AI boom. In 2025, Bay Area companies attracted three-quarters of all U.S. AI funding — and more than half the worldwide total — according to the Bay Area Council Economic Institute.

Economists refer to this industrial clustering as "agglomeration." Companies, workers, and investors in the same industry benefit from being near one another. It's like a gigantic magnet for economic activity. Zucman suggests these agglomeration effects are so strong that billionaires will still want to live and work in California even if there is a wealth tax.

Zucman points to Mark Zuckerberg as an example. Rather than simply leaving California, Zuckerberg reportedly struck a deal with Meta's board that would let him pay the tax without sacrificing control of his company. Essentially, the deal would let him sell nonvoting shares to cover his potential wealth tax bill while holding on to enough voting shares to keep control of the company. In return, he agreed to stay in California for eight years, subject to conditions, including Meta remaining headquartered there.

Jensen Huang, the CEO of Nvidia, told Bloomberg in January that he was "perfectly fine" with paying the wealth tax envisioned by Prop 40. Under it, he would be on the hook for something like $8 billion. "I've got to tell you, I have not even thought about it once," Huang said. "We chose to live in Silicon Valley, and whatever taxes they would like to apply, so be it. I'm perfectly fine with it."

Rauh, however, doubts that California's tech ecosystem has enough gravity to keep billionaires there if the tax passes. "Agglomeration effects are fragile," he argues. A wealth tax could saddle startup founders with big tax bills based on their companies' valuations, even before they've cashed out. Prop 40 includes provisions that would allow qualifying taxpayers with illiquid wealth to defer payments, but Rauh argues those protections are inadequate.

" You have to understand that the entire startup ecosystem of California is threatened by taxes like this," Rauh argues. He predicts that "investors will pull out of California if the wealth tax is passed."

Zucman hears echoes of an earlier fight in all these warnings about taxing wealth: the fight over taxing income back in the early 20th century. Back then, he says, critics argued that an income tax would drive people away and hurt the economy.

He points to Wisconsin's adoption of a progressive income tax in 1911 as a crucial turning point. Two years later, America adopted the federal income tax we know today. Zucman hopes California can play a similar role for taxing wealth.

"I have little doubt that it will work very well for the state of California," he says. "And then very quickly you'll see other states doing the same."

Ultimately, Zucman hopes this movement will lead to a federal wealth tax. He acknowledges that billionaires are less likely to move to avoid a national wealth tax than a state one.

Rauh also expects less flight under a national wealth tax. But he argues that such a tax would still discourage investment and threaten the U.S. economy.

California voters are now being deluged with ads and glossy mailers in what has proven to be one of the most expensive ballot fights in the state's history. If wealth tax supporters have their way, the rest of America could face a similar choice in the near future. For now, it's up to Californians to decide whether this wealth tax is a golden opportunity or a costly mistake.

#5910. First Solar to inaugurate South Carolina solar panel finishing facility

First Solar will today inaugurate the first phase of its new manufacturing facility in Gaffney, South Carolina. The $330 million facility onshores final production processes for the company’s Series 6 Plus modules.

The Gaffney plant currently employs approximately 500 people, and First Solar expects that number to rise to 700 by mid-2027 when the facility’s second phase is completed.

The Gaffney plant joins First Solar’s three fully vertically integrated manufacturing facilities in Ohio, and one each in Alabama and Louisiana, along with R&D centers in Ohio and California. First Solar will have a 3.7-GW annual manufacturing capacity in South Carolina, bringing the company’s total U.S. manufacturing capacity to 17.7 GW annually.

The First Solar Series 6 Plus module features the company’s thin-film cadmium-telluride base to reach 480 W at 19% efficiency. The panel has aluminum under-mount frames and dual junction boxes. First Solar is working on a bifacial version of the panel, which would allow light absorption on both sides.

#5907. Boston Dynamics Workers Protest Use Of Robot Dogs By ICE

Boston Dynamics makes those cute little robot dogs that amaze and amuse us in videos like this one. How could anyone possibly object to something so endearing?

When it was revealed recently that four of its robot dogs have been acquired by US Immigration and Customs Enforcement for $1.3 million, many workers at Boston Dynamics were aghast. The four machines have been specially modified to include a robotic arm, thermal camera, and sensors for explosive materials.

They were not sold directly by Boston Dynamics but rather by a Connecticut company known as RADeCO, which markets the modified robots under the trade name Cerebus. In Greek mythology, Cerebus is the three headed dog that guards the gates of hell. How charming. While the contract from ICE is with RADeCO, Boston Dynamics is directly involved with technical support.

Boston Dynamics is 100 percent owned by Hyundai, by the way. So now a South Korean company that was humiliated by ICE agents a year ago when hundreds of its employees and sub-contractors were arrested, placed in handcuffs, and frog marched onto buses as undesirable aliens, is supplying ICE with advanced robots so it can better pursue its campaign of terror all across America. Greed conquers all, apparently.

In a letter to top executives reviewed  by the Boston Globe, hundreds of Boston Dynamics employees said they are “uncomfortable” with the deal, and warned it could hurt the company’s reputation and workforce. “We cannot be proud of working at BD when that work is being used by a government agency that brutalizes civilians.”

In the letter, they expressed concerns that they will be seen  by their family, friends, and community as “working directly with ICE. “Many of us have gotten messages from friends and family, asking us in disbelief and horror if this news is true, and how we can live with ourselves if it is.” The letter says the risks for Boston Dynamics are “simply too great to continue with this deal. We fear we will lose teammates over this decision, and worry about the risk it creates to achieve our shared company goals.”

In response, the company issued a statement saying the purpose of the robots is to keep people safe, and that the Department of Homeland Security must follow the company’s terms and conditions, which prohibit weaponization of the robots or using them for harm or intimidation.  A spokesperson for the Department of Homeland Security told The Globe the robot “provides law enforcement officers with an additional tool to safely assess potentially dangerous situations before placing personnel or the public at risk.”

Anyone who thinks ICE will adhere to any guidelines is a fool. Ask the heirs of Alex Pretti and Rene Good if they think ICE agents can be trusted to act appropriately. Just this week, Pretti’s parents spoke publicly about their son’s execution by ICE in January. At a press conference, they said the federal government had not provided them with meaningful information about his death. “At every turn, they have ignored us, misled us, and shielded Alex’s killers,” said Michael Pretti. “We are a family who love this country. We trust in the institutions of this country. We believe in the rule of law — and that people should be held accountable through that law.”

Or consider the electric shock gloves ICE will use to torture detainees. The gloves carry the brand name G.L.O.V.E., which stands for Generated Low Output Voltage Emitter, and are manufactured by Compliant Technologies, a company owned by military veterans and based in Lexington, Kentucky.

According to The Guardian, during experiments involving “volunteers” at Leavenworth military prison, the people subjected to the gloves felt intense pain, stopped fighting immediately, and feared additional exposures, an army study found. The authors of that study told the Associated Press, “After experiencing the effects of the GLOVE … participants stated just seeing the GLOVE was enough to make them want to comply.”

The electric shock from the gloves does not cause the body to freeze like a Taser. Instead, it “works by overloading the peripheral nerve endings” of the skin to cause pain, the study found. Participants who were wet or had oil on their skin described even more powerful shocks, with one reporting discomfort for 30 minutes afterward.

Just what the doctor ordered, ICE decided. After reading the report, it ordered 6000 pairs at a cost of $16.7 million. And while the company has a detailed list of do’s and don’ts, what is the likelihood that ICE thugs will abide by them, if they know about them at all?

There is another component of the dog robot story. Students of history know that the Nazis used dogs that had been trained to be especially aggressive to terrorize people detained in their concentration camps. The dogs were trained initially at Buchenwald and later at Auschwitz. Surely those in charge of things at DHS and ICE are aware of the historical context between dogs and detainees?

Older Americans will vividly recall how police in southern US states used dogs to intimidate civil rights protesters. Boston Robotics can issue all the press releases it wants, but the connection between dogs and interference with freedom of expression cannot be ignored. Overall, it is not a good look for the company.

A spokesperson for the company pooh poohed the letter, noting that half of its 300 signatories did not identify themselves by name and that 300 was just a fraction of the company’s 1,400 workers. Amanda McMaster, who once served as interim CEO of Boston Dynamics was unmoved by the workers’ concerns. She said the company is “standing behind the decision to move forward with this and other potential sales to government customers.”

She cited the company’s anti-weaponization guidelines, and vowed to make sure the dog robot is not misused. “We believe this decision is in line with our purpose of ethically advancing intelligent machines in service of humanity,” she wrote, asking workers to “accept this decision, even if you do not agree with it.” Some readers may recall when Google’s motto was “Don’t be evil,” That was before it became one of the prime enablers of government surveillance in the US.

The deal has drawn criticism from advocacy groups in Massachusetts. Barbara Katzenberg and other members of the De-Ice Action Network have staged protests outside of Boston Dynamics’ headquarters. In agreeing to let ICE have the modified robots, Katzenberg said Boston Dynamics is “hoping and believing that ICE will stand by their terms and conditions and ethical principles. We believe that’s naive.”

It may be beyond naive. It may be a danger to anyone who encounters one of these robots. We recognize that the specialized sensors could be used to sniff out explosives, which is a potential benefit, but the possibilities for abuse and misuse are enormous. Readers are free to decide for themselves whether the Cerebus robots are a wise investment for US taxpayers.

People arrive at a polling location during early voting in the midterm election, in Atlanta, Georgia, on October 13, 2026. Photo by Erik S. Lesser/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

At this stage in the midterms, all that's left is the voting and the waiting. On election night, I'll be in Studio 31 at NPR headquarters with browser tabs full of spreadsheets, watching the results roll in and sharing context with NPR listeners.

One of those spreadsheets lists all the congressional districts that have been redrawn since 2024 as part of a mid-decade redistricting push by President Trump. Facing down the historical precedent that the president's party usually loses seats in the midterms, the president pressured Republican-led states to create more sure-thing GOP districts.

But headed into voting, the results in two-thirds of those districts are very much up in the air. In some cases, Democrats are favored. These are places Trump would have won by 10 points just two years ago.

Last month, I saw that the president had a campaign rally scheduled in Greenville, N.C. But national Republicans essentially stopped running ads for Michael Whatley — Trump's handpicked nominee — and diverted resources to states with more competitive races. So, why was the president holding another rally? I reached out to a few sources in the state.

What I learned is that the real reason for the president's trip was almost certainly to help the Republican candidate in eastern North Carolina's first congressional district. It is now one of the most hotly contested and expensive House races in the country, despite new district lines that should have made it an easy Republican pick-up.

Two days of driving through this very large district, one Trump rally, two bags of gummy candy and a lot of coffee later, I was back in the office digging into my spreadsheet of congressional districts. NC-01 isn't an isolated case. A majority of districts redrawn to be a firewall for a Republican majority have turned out to be far more competitive than expected.

So, Tuesday night (and beyond), I'll be filling in the wins and losses to see how this gambit turned out. In the meantime, read more from me about how Trump is spending his final days on the campaign trail. And, you can listen to everything you need to know about the midterms on Up First: The Sunday Story.

#5903. Jetson Launches Fixed Price Heat Pump Water Heater with Installation Included

This Heat Pump Water Heater Week, Jetson dropped some big news. They are bringing a heat pump water heater (HPWH) to market using the same model of vertical integration that they use with heat pump installations, where they procure all equipment as well as install and monitor the HPWHs, all for a fixed price.

CEO Stephen Lake talked with the Advanced Water Heating Initiative about the heat pump water heater that the company is launching into the market and the company’s unique way of owning every step of the installation process, which they believe drives down cost and improves customer experiences.

Jetson is also joining 17 other HPWH manufacturers in what is becoming a full, efficient water heating marketplace.

Jetson launched its heat pump model for heating and cooling homes just two years ago. They got started in Vancouver BC and Denver, and since have moved into New York, California, Tennessee and Maryland.

Their model is to own the entire process of heat pump installation, from quoting to procurement, to installation, to servicing and repairs. They also analyze data from their equipment to make sure systems are running optimally. Their goal is to tackle the two biggest hurdles to electrification and decarbonization — cost and consumer experience.

From heat pumps for space heating, the company is now moving into the heat pump water heater space. They said they’ve heard from homeowners who wanted their water heater replaced in addition to their furnace. And Jetson is bringing the same philosophy of owning the whole installation process, which could shake things up in the industry.

Heat pump water heaters are at an inflection point. Their sales are still only around 2 to 3% of water heater sales nationally, but they’ve grown consistently since coming to market 15 years ago and there are now many product options and manufacturers to choose from. Plus, an upcoming DOE water heating standard will phase out inefficient, electric water heaters and give a boost to this technology starting in 2029. Sales could rise into the millions by the end of the decade.

But, like most technologies on the early part of their adoption curve, heat pump water heaters can carry an upfront cost that is 2 to 3 times more than other water heaters. Many areas have rebates to help with these costs, and many consumers are DIYing their installations to save money, but the high price barrier remains.

Jetson sees an opportunity here, they are promising to install a 50 gallon heat pump water heater for a fixed price of $4,495 all in, and that’s before rebates. This includes any electrical work that might be required to upgrade a house to accept a 240V HPWH that needs a 30A breaker. The company feels like owning every part of the process allows them to trim costs that otherwise get added as the equipment moves from manufacturer to distributor to contractor to homeowner.

Their HPWH is pretty standard for the industry these days — it has top and side connections (which makes installation easier), is ducting enabled, and has leak detection. They are also including a mixing valve with every installation, which means the tank will store water at 140 degrees and will provide extra hot water.

Other differentiators for the company are their ability to monitor and maintain the unit. They claim to monitor all of their installed equipment to make sure it is working as well and as efficiently as possible. For HPWHs, this could be important to ensure the water heaters are delivering the efficiency and utility bill savings promised.

Jetson also offers a “Jetson Care” package, where for around $10/month the HPWH will receive any maintenance it needs. This piece of mind could be important. If the compressor was to fail in year three, the HPWH would be replaced at no cost. We’ve seen other extended warranties emerge in the industry, but this is the first I know of offered by a manufacturer.

All in all, it is exciting that Jetson is getting into the HPWH market. Fixed price installations that are cost competitive, done quickly, and maintained over time could greatly improve customer experience of HPWHs and increase adoption.

Check out our interview with Jetson as part of HPWH Week.

#5900. Tesla’s Full Self Driving Has Now Dramatically Reduced the Number of Reasons to Intervene or Complain

7 years ago, I paid Tesla $6000 for a permanent subscription to “Full Self Driving” for my new Model 3. At the time, FSD would navigate and drive automatically on limited access highways (mostly Interstates), but that was about it.

I’m currently driving a 2026 base Model Y with FSD V14. Now, I can get into my car in my garage or a big parking lot or a Supercharger, and without touching anything, say “Hey Grok,” then verbally say “Navigate me to [nearly any business, my home, or any address].” Then, FSD will drive me automatically out of my garage or the parking lot or the Supercharger to the location requested and park my car in a parking lot or on the street (but not yet in my garage). I sit back and “supervise,” but I seldom must intervene or complain about its actions as it drives.

What are recent improvements with FSD that I have observed? FSD now reacts properly to more and more edge cases.

There is no question in my mind from personal experience: FSD is a critical safety feature that will save and has saved the lives of Tesla drivers and their passengers who have it activated (as well as other vehicles, pedestrians, bikes, etc.). However, Tesla is letting its own customers die if they haven’t paid the $99/month to activate FSD. This is Tesla choosing an income stream over the safety of its customers.

However: That still leaves at least 15% of Tesla customers that can and will be injured or killed because they don’t have a safety feature that Tesla has withheld from them. Note: even though the uptake rate for FSD is supposed to be 75%, lately, this is only for new customers. If a customer decides they no longer want or can’t afford the $99/month fee, they won’t have it any longer. It seems to me that Tesla could make the “Automatic Collision Evasion” feature available to all of their customers. I think this is a software option that could be implemented for customers without having to give them automatic driving functionality. Also, Tesla could make “FSD Engaged” the default and require the driver to turn it off if desired, rather than the other way around, which we have now.

Warning! In my opinion, Tesla customers that don’t opt in for the $99/month FSD subscription are missing a very important safety feature of their Tesla.

It appears to me that Tesla’s FSD no longer needs to be supervised on highways. Can’t Tesla get this limited Level 5 automation approved? Being able to sleep, read, text, etc. while driving on long trips would be one of the most important advantages of L5 automation.

This will get you a total of 3 months free Full Self Driving. It will be $99/month after that. For a Cybertruck, you will get $1000 off your purchase price. At this moment, Tesla is offering a year of free Supercharging with the purchase of a new Model 3 Premium or Performance.

#5897. Louisiana DEQ Issues Rushed, Controversial Air Permit to Proposed Hyundai Steel Facility

Donaldsonville, Louisiana — Yesterday, the Louisiana Department of Environmental Quality (LDEQ) posted the final, approved air permit for Hyundai’s proposed steel plant. Hyundai promised a state-of-the-art, clean facility, but the proposed plan falls short of those promises and would still bring thousands of tons of pollution to Cancer Alley, a region already overburdened with dirty air and water.

Throughout 2026, Sierra Club, working with experts and local groups, submitted a series of comments to LDEQ in an effort to bring to light the errors, miscalculations, and omissions in the permit application. Hyundai Steel made some changes to its permit application as a result of those comments, updating plans to electrify some components of the facility, which will lower levels of pollution.

Still, the final air permit issued by LDEQ yesterday fails to require Hyundai to significantly reduce pollution by electrifying additional components or blending in green hydrogen, nor does it require best-available technology for controlling toxic dust. Astoundingly, LDEQ found that this massive new greenfield facility would have “no adverse impacts” and therefore failed to include an environmental justice analysis, even though Ascension Parish already experiences toxic pollution at levels exceeding 96% of the rest of the country. (Hyundai Steel initially included, but then completely removed, the environmental justice analysis from the permit application.) LDEQ also did not do any independent analysis of alternative sites to determine if there is a location that would be less harmful to surrounding communities.

In response, Sierra Club Industrial Transformation Campaign Director Matthew Miles Goodrich issued the following statement:

“Hyundai Steel says it wants to be a ‘catalyst for the future steel industry and hydrogen ecosystem,’ but its air permit tells a different story. The company made some improvements to its facility in response to public pressure, but it needs to do more to reduce dangerous pollution through electrification and make a firm commitment to green hydrogen. The South Korean government is mobilizing three-quarters of a trillion dollars to develop and incentivize clean industry, like green steel made with hydrogen, in its own country. That begs the question: why isn’t Hyundai—which is so well-positioned to produce and use steel—bringing the cleanest technologies to one of the most polluted communities in America? We will continue to evaluate the final permit and are exploring our legal options and next steps.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5895. Mach debuts autonomous mowing platform for solar project sites

Autonomous technology developer Mach is launching a platform for adapting commercial lawn mowers for autonomous deployment on solar project sites. This technology factory integrates with major commercial mowers.

“Utility-scale solar sites present one of the most demanding operational environments in off-road autonomy: narrow clearances, dense panel shadows, GPS-degraded zones and zero tolerance for infrastructure strikes,” said Colin Hurd, CEO of Mach. “By delivering our full-stack autonomy as a Tier-1 partner to commercial equipment OEMs, we provide solar asset owners with the rugged, dealer-supported machinery they already trust, supercharged with driverless capability. This ecosystem approach enables solar developers to scale vegetation management across gigawatts while giving equipment manufacturers an immediate, turnkey path to lead the autonomous solar market.”

Using Mach’s proprietary RTK-GNSS automation infrastructure, mowers are designed to work automatically in conditions with dusty, tall grass and sun glare while avoiding structural and electrical elements on solar arrays. These mowers can be deployed on multiple sites and managed by a single operator.

Maintenance is still available through the mower’s original manufacturer, since Mach’s platform integrates with existing machines. Mach will showcase its autonomous solar mowing platform at RE+ 26, from Nov. 16 to 19 at the Las Vegas Convention Center at Booth W16676

#5894. After Years-Long Community Objection, Dairyland Power Pulls the Plug on NTEC Gas Plant

SUPERIOR, Wisconsin — Today, Dairyland Power Coop announced the cancellation of plans for NTEC, which was proposed to be built on the shores of Lake Superior, and asked the Wisconsin Public Service Commission (PSC) to withdraw its permits.

The Nemadji Trail Energy Center (NTEC), a 625 MW natural gas plant, has faced years of public pushback from local residents, Dairyland Power customers, members of the Fond du Lac and Red Cliff Bands of Lake Superior Chippewa, and environmental groups.

“We applaud Dairyland Power Coop and South Shore Energy for formally cancelling the NTEC gas plant and asking for their permits to be withdrawn, after the clock ran out on their PSC permits,” said Elizabeth Ward, Wisconsin Chapter Director. “NTEC has faced years of public opposition and was outdated as soon as it was proposed. This is a victory for the Great Lakes, our climate, and the city of Superior, whose city leaders have relentlessly worked to protect residents from the air pollution caused by a dirty, expensive gas plant. We thank them for their tireless leadership and attribute this success to them.”

“NTEC was never a good idea,” said Evan Mulholland, Healthy Communities Program Director at MCEA. “NTEC would have been bad for the climate, too expensive, unnecessary for grid reliability, and harmful to public health, and it was proposed in the wrong location. MCEA is grateful for all the organizers and partners who helped with the campaign against NTEC over the past 10 years.”

“This victory is the culmination of more than seven years of relentless work to defeat a project that would have brought tremendous harm to communities in Superior and beyond,” said Clean Wisconsin Director of Legal Advocacy Brett Korte. “Clean Wisconsin first intervened at the Public Service Commission to stop the plant in 2019 and the fight continued in court and city council chambers, before agencies, and in public conversation. We are grateful to all the partners including tribal advocates, other environmental organizations, and community members who never stopped fighting and believing that against all odds, this gas plant would be defeated.”

#5893. The Truth About Renewable Electricity: Your Questions, Answered

Reliable, affordable electricity is essential for many of the products and services that underpin the modern economy. But the sources of that electricity—and their respective merits and risks—are increasingly the subject of debate and polarization.

A flood of narratives and counternarratives is rising in the media and public conversation about the reliability and affordability of renewables, as well as their impacts on the economy, jobs, and communities. These mixed messages can make it hard to get the accurate information people need as they face important decisions about how they live, spend, or vote.

Here, we answer some common questions about renewable electricity, using the latest evidence, data, and research.

Renewable electricity is power generated from naturally replenishing energy sources, including solar, wind, hydropower, geothermal, and biomass.

Solar and wind are considered “variable,” in that their output depends on weather, time of day, and season. Hydropower, geothermal, and biomass are considered “firm” in that their output is reliably available on demand (although no power plant, whether it uses fossil fuels or renewables, is always available). Experience and modeling show that electricity grids can be reliable, affordable, and clean (low-carbon) by combining high volumes of variable and firm renewables with energy storage and other low-carbon generating sources.

Renewables accounted for around 34% of global electricity generation in 2025, with 15% from hydropower, 8% from wind, and 7% from solar. Wind and solar have grown significantly since 2015, when their shares in global electricity generation were only 3.4% and 1%, respectively.

Still, having been in use for more than a century, fossil fuels remain the dominant electricity generation source globally. They account for nearly 60% of the world’s electricity, primarily from coal (35%) and natural gas (22%).

Renewable electricity is not just growing—it’s skyrocketing. Solar and wind deployment in particular are expanding faster than any other electricity sources.

The growth trajectory is most evident in new capacity additions. In 2025, over 85% of new global electricity generation capacity came from renewables. The total global renewable electricity generation capacity has grown nearly 7-fold in the past 25 years, from 764 gigawatts (GW) in 2000 to 5,149 GW by 2025. Growth in 2025 set a record, with renewable capacity growing by 15.5%, adding an additional 629 GW within a single year.

Beyond installed capacity, it is important to understand how much electricity is actually generated. Electricity generated from solar and wind has increased more than 11-fold and more than 3-fold, respectively, between 2015 and 2025. In comparison, electricity generation from all fossil fuels increased only 14% during the same period.

Notably, 2025 marked the first year in over a century that electricity generated from renewable sources exceeded that from coal, the largest fossil fuel source. This signals a structural shift in the global electricity mix.

Solar has been the fastest-growing electricity source over the past eight years, roughly doubling every three years. In 2025, it posted a record increase of 600 terrawatt-hours (TWh), bringing total solar electricity generation to nearly 2,700 TWh. Together, solar and wind met 90% of global electricity demand growth in 2025, with solar accounting for 75% of that increase. Meanwhile, total electricity generation from fossil fuels declined slightly for the first time in 2025, by 0.2%.

Battery storage capacity has also grown exponentially. New capacity additions in 2025 were more than 150 times higher than they were a decade ago. Batteries allow solar and wind to deliver more value to grids, since they can store and provide energy for times when the wind isn’t blowing and sun isn’t shining.

Solar and wind are likely to continue growing fast and are expected to account for almost all new renewable capacity additions through 2030, according to the International Energy Agency (IEA). IEA estimates that solar and wind will account for 30% of global generation in 2030, nearly double their current share.

The three main drivers are cost decreases, enabling government policies and corporate purchasing.

The cost of electricity from some renewable sources has fallen dramatically over the past decade. One way of assessing energy costs is to look at the levelized cost of electricity (LCOE), which, roughly speaking, is the average cost of electricity from a power plant over its lifetime. According to the International Renewable Energy Agency (IRENA), the LCOE for utility-scale solar power and onshore wind dropped 90% and 70%, respectively between 2010 and 2024.

This dramatic decline in solar and wind costs has been reinforced by rapid improvements and falling costs in battery storage technologies. Battery storage is a key integration strategy to bring the variable output of solar and wind into the electric grid while maintaining reliability (more on this later). BloombergNEF’s data shows that the global LCOE for four-hour battery storage—meaning the cost of a battery that can hold and discharge four hours of electricity—is $78 per megawatt-hour (MWh), having declined 90% from 2012 to 2025.

Altogether, the combination of maturing technologies, larger project scales and reduced operating expenses has made renewable electricity a low-cost option for many regions of the world, while offering greater price stability compared to fossil fuel generation since solar and wind have no fuel costs.

Government policies are another driver of growth in renewable electricity. IEA reports that more than 130 governments have policies that encourage the deployment of solar, wind, and other renewables. These range from subsidies and other financial incentives, to purchasing targets (such as Renewable Portfolio Standards) to carbon pricing policies that raise the cost of fossil generation.

These policies help to overcome lock-in by fossil fuels, many of which have benefited from decades of supportive policies. The International Monetary Fund estimated that in 2024, governments provided over $700 billion in explicit subsidies for fossil fuels, or 0.6% of global GDP. While many subsidies are directly for consumers for things like transportation or cooking fuels, governments also subsidize the coal, oil and gas industries directly. For example, the U.S. government has recognized 16 tax measures benefiting upstream fossil fuels, with the largest exceeding $1 billion annually for oil and gas. These have been in the U.S. tax code for over 35 years, with one coal tax benefit dating back to 1918.

Corporations are increasingly motivated by the cost-saving benefits of renewable electricity, as well as their own voluntary commitments to decarbonize. Hundreds of firms have pledged and achieved the goal of using 100% renewable electricity through alliances such as RE100.  Large buyers such as Google, Meta, Amazon, and Microsoft are driving roughly half of corporate purchases of renewables, accounting for about 27 GW of new solar and wind capacity in 2025.

Cost decreases, supportive policies, and corporate interest are likely to spur strong growth in renewable generation for years to come. IEA projects that renewable electricity will grow by about 1,000 TWh annually through 2030, or 8% per year.

Solar and wind generation are variable, driven by daily and seasonal weather patterns. However, power systems have successfully integrated large volumes of solar and wind generation into reliable, affordable grids. Grids can incorporate an increasing share of variable generation through “integration strategies” such as:

Electricity prices in any specific country reflect a wide range of factors, including existing investments in generation, transmission, and distribution; the cost and availability of fuels; and a wide array of policies (regulatory, tax, market design, etc.). The affordability of electricity has become a central issue across the globe, especially as energy demand grows for data centers, transport, cooling and other uses.

Renewable electricity can help keep power bills affordable because solar and wind, often paired with storage, are the lowest-cost sources of new supply in many regions of the world. They have much lower operating costs than fossil fuel alternatives because they have no fuel cost and can be deployed quickly. This cost advantage is one of the key drivers behind IEA projections that solar and wind generation will grow faster than conventional generation from now to 2030.

Renewable electricity sources have far lower GHG emissions over their entire lifecycle than fossil fuel power plants. While fossil generators produce most of their climate impact during fuel combustion — and contribute additional emissions through raw material extraction, fuel processing, power plant construction, operation and decommissioning — renewables avoid the vast majority of these carbon‑intensive steps. As a result, sources like wind power carry only a small fraction of the lifecycle carbon footprint associated with coal or natural gas, making them much cleaner options for electricity generation overall.

According to a 2021 assessment by the National Laboratory of the Rockies, the median amount of greenhouse gases emitted per kilowatt-hour of electricity generated by wind is 13 grams (g CO2e/kWh) and solar PV is 43 g CO2e/kWh. Meanwhile, coal is about 1,001 g CO2e/kWh and natural gas is 486 g CO2/kWh. The lifecycle emissions of wind and solar are almost entirely attributable to emissions associated with their raw materials and manufacturing, which will likely decline further as industrial processes and transportation decarbonize.

There are lots of ways renewable electricity offers advantages over fossil fuels beyond just costs and climate impact, including:

The current energy crisis spurred by the war on Iran underscores the risks of relying on globally traded and unevenly distributed fossil resources. Fossil fuels are heavily concentrated in a few countries, whereas every nation on Earth has sunlight and wind, and many have hydro and geothermal resources. Expanding renewable electricity deployment can therefore strengthen national energy security and decrease exposure to global fossil fuel price volatility.

Some countries are already realizing this benefit. For example, following the Russian invasion of Ukraine in early 2022, the European Union recognized the security risk of its reliance on Russian fossil fuels. Russia supplied 45% of the E.U.’s natural gas, 27% of its crude oil, and 50% of its coal before the Ukraine war. In response, the E.U. launched its REPowerEU plan to rapidly reduce dependence on Russian fossil fuels by accelerating its renewable energy deployment, energy efficiency and energy supply diversification.

Conventional thermal power plants such as coal, natural gas and nuclear facilities require large volumes of water for cooling. Studies from the U.S. Energy Information Administration estimate that coal-fired power plants in the U.S. need to withdraw more than 21,000 gallons of water to generate 1 MWh of electricity, while natural gas-fired generation needs 2,793 gallons per MWh.

Meanwhile, solar panels and wind turbines require little to no water for cooling and maintenance. For example, the Solar Energy Industries Association estimated that solar panels use approximately 20 gallons per MWh for cleaning. Using solar instead of fossil fuels can therefore significantly reduce water use in regions experiencing high levels of water stress.

Research published in 2023 in the British Medical Journal showed that an estimated 5.13 million excess deaths per year globally are attributable to ambient air pollution from fossil fuel use. Renewable electricity can help reduce air pollutants such as particulate matter (PM2.5), sulfur dioxide (SO2) and nitrogen oxides (NOx) to improve public health.

At a time when electricity demand is skyrocketing from sources such as data centers, cooling and manufacturing, solar and wind can often be deployed faster than fossil fuel power plants. Among all the electricity generation options, solar stands out as the fastest to deploy, often within two years. Even under favorable permitting conditions, non-solar options generally take 3-15 years to get online.

Meeting new data center-driven electricity demand is a complex and rapidly evolving topic. IEA analysis projects that this demand is likely to be met by more fossil generation than renewables through 2030 in the two largest markets (United States and China), though at the global level, renewables are expected to take the lead. Large users of energy are also turning to nuclear power, pursuing existing and new operations, as well as restarting decommissioned plants.

This regional increase in fossil-based power is being driven by data center developers’ and hyperscalers’ unique demands. A big one is “speed-to-power”: getting generation online fast, since data centers can be built far faster than new generation can connect to the grid. While a solar or wind farm can actually be built faster than a natural gas plant, in practice, variable renewables can be harder to deploy quickly through the grid, due to poor policy environments, locational constraints, permitting delays, and extensive grid study and building timelines.

Even large-scale gas plants are coming online slower than data center demand — a problem compounded by a natural gas turbine shortage — so building on-site is often the fastest option. Here, fossil generation has an advantage: Though solar has more siting flexibility than wind, it still needs large land areas that major data center hubs often lack. The siting flexibility and lower land needs of fossil-based generation often make it the preferred choice for “behind-the-meter” or co-located generation.

The second pressure data centers face is reliability. These gigawatt-scale loads demand highly reliable, dispatchable power around the clock. The grid itself, drawing on many balanced sources of generation, is typically the most reliable option. But where grid capacity is unavailable or can’t keep up yet, developers are turning to fossil generation to fill the gap. While on-site solar and wind paired with storage can technically serve the same load at a similar reliability, meeting those demands would require significantly more land than is typically available near many data center sites. Even when grid connection is possible, fossil power, including diesel generators, still typically serves as backup power.

Making renewables and storage the default for data centers will require faster grid modernization, along with solutions tailored to data centers’ unique characteristics. Critically, much of the disadvantage faced by renewables is policy-driven rather than inherent in the technology itself. Streamlining permitting and interconnection processes would let solar and wind deploy far faster, making them a more competitive option for meeting new data center demand.

Jobs in electricity generation from solar and wind are already outpacing many other generation sources, particularly fossil fuels. In 2024, around 14 million people worked in the electricity generation sector globally, with solar and wind generation accounting for 48% of the entire electricity workforce. Fossil fuel-based jobs comprised only 26%. Solar energy is the largest electricity subsector for creating new jobs. According to the IEA, solar PV created nearly 4 times more jobs than power generation from oil and gas worldwide in 2024. Jobs in coal power generation declined in the same year.

If we zoom in on the United States, the same trends apply. According to the 2025 U.S. Energy & Employment Report from the U.S. Department of Energy, solar and wind comprised 54% of all jobs in the electricity generation sector in 2024; fossil fuels accounted for 21%. Solar not only leads in employment but is also projected to grow faster than other sectors — at approximately 9.8%, vs. 6.8% for natural gas and 4.1% for coal.

Locally, however, the transition from fossil fuels to renewable energy can be a source of worry for employees and local businesses. In the U.S., jobs in oil and gas tend to pay higher than those in renewables, although both are higher-than-average wages. These are legitimate concerns that require proactive, thoughtful engagement to create economic opportunities in impacted communities. Some governments and businesses are using workforce strategies to ease the transition from fossil fuels to clean energy, including training and reskilling for fossil fuel workers. In Grimsby, England, for example, an offshore wind boom has revived its economy, which had suffered from a decline in the fishing and oil and gas sectors. Spain’s national Just Transition Plan is retraining workers from the coal industry for new jobs in the wind sector.

The impact on birds from wind energy is extremely small compared to other causes, such as buildings and vehicles. According to the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service, onshore wind turbines are responsible for 0.007% of human-caused bird deaths annually.

The impact is also low compared to other sources of electricity. For example, one study on the U.S. and Europe showed that fossil-fueled power stations pose a much greater threat to birds than wind and nuclear technologies, due to associated air and water pollution. Another study from the University of Geneva in Switzerland concluded that the onset of shale oil and gas production reduces bird population counts by approximately 15% in the U.S., while wind turbines do not have any measurable impact on bird counts.

There are also several effective ways for wind farms to reduce the risk to birds. For example, new technologies allow blades to turn more slowly, while wind farms can prioritize siting away from bird habitats and migratory pathways.

Modernizing electric grids, building energy storage capacity, and simplifying permitting are challenges many countries face as they try to scale up clean electricity.

Regardless of the source of electricity, grids need to expand quickly to increase reliability. Driving higher performance by modernizing and optimizing existing grid infrastructure is as important as building more. Investment in electricity grids is growing worldwide, but even more is needed, especially for high-voltage transmission. More reliable grids can support integrating higher volumes of renewable electricity.

When it comes to where that infrastructure should be placed, siting and permitting need to improve to speed the transition to the new energy economy while safeguarding local communities and nature. Streamlined administrative processes, such as setting adequate timelines for approvals and improving coordination within and between agencies, are essential. So are community benefits agreements, which can address stakeholder concerns and ensure clean energy projects produce local benefits such as jobs and infrastructure improvements.

Adequate supply of critical minerals is also a challenge for expanding clean electricity supplies. While necessary to meet surging energy demand, mining and processing these minerals can produce emissions, pollute the environment and bring risks to nearby communities, such as water contamination from mine tailings. Mining and processing are also concentrated in a small handful of countries, which can lead to geopolitical risk. Reusing critical minerals discarded technology can help minimize demand, while responsible mining can reduce risks related to environmental, social, supply chain and other concerns.

As governments scale up clean electricity technologies, they should work with businesses and industries as implementation partners and align markets to help those businesses compete in global markets. Public and private large buyers can play a major role in scaling electric vehicle fleets, increasing home and building efficiency, and boosting low-carbon industrial products, while helping to expand clean electricity to power these end-uses. Large buyers, as well as groups of smaller buyers pooling their demand together, can drive widespread adoption of clean electricity and low-carbon products through their procurement decisions.

Research shows that keeping some of the worst climate change impacts at bay requires global greenhouse gas emissions to drop to “net-zero” by mid-century. At the global level, credible modeling by organizations including IEA, IRENA and BloombergNEF show that reaching net-zero emissions by 2050 is feasible and affordable by.

The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) reached a similar conclusion.

In a world facing numerous geopolitical risks, the security and affordability of energy supplies are top-of-mind for countries, businesses and households. An economy dependent on fossil fuels is a structural economic vulnerability, leaving countries exposed to price and supply shocks whenever the next conflict hits. An economy built on home-grown clean energy protects against future shocks, provides economic resilience and ensures lower bills for consumers. Renewable electricity puts countries in control: it provides energy price stability, independence and long-term security.

WRI is a global research organization that spans more than 50 countries, with offices in Brazil, China, Europe, India, Indonesia, and the United States. Our more than 450 experts and staff work closely with leaders to turn big ideas into action to sustain our natural resources—the foundation of economic opportunity and human well-being. #5891. CPP Wind Engineering develops wind testing certification for utility-scale solar

CPP Wind Engineering, a global wind engineering consultancy for solar, is launching a wind certification program for utility-scale solar projects, which the organization believes is the first of its kind in the industry. The new program aims to improve the performance and durability of utility-scale solar over a range of wind conditions.

The program offers two services. Site Certification confirms that each project has properly assessed climate and site conditions, implemented an effective stow strategy, suitably positioned anemometers and used a qualified tracker or fixed-tilt system. System Qualification is a one-time review of a tracker or fixed-tilt product line’s wind engineering design data and methodology.

CPP’s new program is built from the organization’s 45 years of experience in wind engineering, including wind tunnel testing of solar projects, wind stow and siting studies and forensic investigation of wind damage to solar projects. Tornado risk falls outside the certification program and is assessed separately using a probabilistic risk approach.

“Wind governs how a solar power plant is built, yet there has been no consistent way to show that the wind loads behind a design were derived and applied correctly,” said David Banks, president of CPP Wind Engineering. “Most of the wind damage we investigate occurs at wind speeds sites see every year, and most of it can be engineered out. This program gives owners, manufacturers and their advisors an independent basis for confirming the reliability of wind engineering. Tornadoes, on the other hand, can’t be engineered out. We must predict the probability of loss for those.”

For project owners and developers, CPP’s Site Certification provides comparisions of mounting design options and a documented record of wind engineering diligence for financing, insurance and project sales. For manufacturers, CPP’s System Qualification evaluates the wind engineering basis of a tracker or fixed-tilt product line, providing a foundation for determining its suitability for site-specific wind conditions. For independent engineers, CPP serves as a partner, contributing wind engineering expertise to the due diligence they lead.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.
