#5890. Trump Rolling Back Methane Pollution Safeguards

The Trump administration has already delayed implementation of the critical standards for year+

Washington, DC — Last night during a rally in Texas, Donald Trump announced that the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency would roll back safeguards finalized in 2024 to limit methane pollution from the oil and gas sector. This dangerous reversal—revealed earlier this month by Administrator Lee Zeldin—would allow bad-actor corporations to release more climate-disrupting compounds and other pollutants into our atmosphere, jeopardizing the health and wellbeing of our families and communities.

In 2024, the Biden Administration issued landmark methane standards for the oil and gas sector that significantly reduce these and other pollutants from both new and existing equipment. These safeguards are based on low-cost, commonsense practices and technologies that have been available to oil and gas operators for years or even decades. For instance, the standards require operators to capture or use gas at oil wells rather than flare it; to conduct regular inspections to identify and fix leaking equipment at well sites and compressor stations; and to use zero-emitting electric pumps and process controllers rather than gas-powered devices.

Soon after taking office in 2025, the Trump EPA suspended the implementation of key provisions from the Biden-era standards, delaying their enforcement by up to 18 months despite no widespread evidence of compliance difficulties. Now, the EPA is preparing a proposal that would permanently eliminate some of the rule’s most important safeguards, including its ban on routine flaring at oil wells and its program to leverage third-party monitoring data to identify and stop particularly large methane releases known as super-emitter events.

The proposal would also shield low-producing wells from leak monitoring requirements, despite consistent evidence showing that such wells do not pollute less than higher-producing wells. Nearly one in ten people in the U.S. live in a county with over a thousand low-producing wells. These sources represent nearly half of the sector’s methane emissions and are primarily owned by large, well-resourced and highly profitable corporations.

In reaction, Sierra Club released the following statements:

Cyrus Reed, Legislative and Conservation Director of Sierra Club’s Lone Star Chapter, said: “No state produces more oil and gas than Texas, and no state has more emissions of methane and associated gases than Texas, and yet the state has no state-level rules to protect us from its dangers. This proposed rollback is an affront to the thousands of Texans who have depended on and called on the EPA to protect us, and yet this proposal is designed to let so-called low-producing oil and gas wells off the hook, even though nearly half of all methane emissions come from these low-producing oil and gas wells.”

Sarah Corcoran, Deputy Director of the Sierra Club Pennsylvania Chapter, said: “Policies that reduce methane waste are commonsense and cost effective. The Trump Administration is not doing anyone any favors by reducing these regulations, not the industry, not the environment and not the American people who will feel the health impacts of these decisions for years to come. Companies have repeatedly made promises to reduce methane pollution – but voluntary measures are often empty promises. We need strict, enforceable regulations in order to hold companies accountable.”

Antoinette Reyes, Sierra Club Rio Grande Chapter Organizer, said: “New Mexico cannot afford a federal retreat on methane. The 2024 EPA safeguards were designed to do basic, practical things: find leaks, stop routine flaring, catch super-emitter events, and require operators to use cleaner equipment that already exists. Rolling those protections back—including protections for low-producing wells that make up a majority of active wells in both New Mexico and Texas—would create a sweeping industry loophole. Oil and gas pollution is like secondhand smoke from the industry: communities are exposed without consent, and the people closest to the source carry the heaviest burden. With the Permian spanning both Texas and New Mexico, pollution knows no boundaries, which is exactly why New Mexico and other states must codify these methane protections into state law and make them strong enough to withstand political rollbacks in Washington.”

Ramesh Bhatt, Chair of Colorado Sierra Club’s Conservation Committee, said: “At a time when Americans are struggling with energy prices, the Trump administration is going out of its way to allow industry to pollute with zero safeguards or commonsense methods to curb dangerous emissions. Methane and associated pollution from oil and gas operators will add billions more to our health costs while causing more climate chaos. Fortunately, the state of Colorado has recognized this folly and has become the first state in the nation to fully adopt the provisions of the 2024 federal methane rules. The state is acting to help protect the people and environment of Colorado while also reducing costs, but these federal standards are so important because pollution does not stop at the state’s border.”

Mahyar Sorour, Sierra Club Director of Beyond Fossil Fuels Policy, said: “It took many years and carefully considered input from health experts, community advocates, and even the oil and gas industry itself to craft the achievable, common-sense methane standards. Yet, the Trump administration, with pockets lined by the biggest polluters, has unceremoniously worked to roll back this and other standards meant to protect our health and safety. This giveaway to fossil fuel executives is foolish and short-sighted, and reveals who the Trump administration really works for—its political donors.”

Methane is a potent greenhouse gas that has more than 80 times the power of carbon dioxide over a 20-year period, driving approximately one-third of the warming our planet has experienced to date. Each year, the U.S. oil and gas sector emits large amounts of methane into our atmosphere. Methane is emitted from oil and gas sources alongside other damaging pollutants, such as smog- and soot-forming volatile organic compounds and air toxins like benzene and formaldehyde, human carcinogens.

According to EPA’s analysis, the 2024 standards are projected to avoid 58 million tons of methane through 2038, as well as 16 million tons of volatile organic compounds and 590,000 tons of air toxins.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5889. Nearly Half of EU Cities Do Not Yet Meet New Clean Air Standards: Report Shows Collaboration Is Key

BRUSSELS — Almost half of EU cities exceed air pollution limits set by a landmark EU clean air law, according to a new report from ClientEarth and World Resources Institute (WRI). The report raises concerns about the level of preparedness in cities across the bloc and outlines the changes needed across all levels of government to meet the new air quality standards, protect people’s health, and strengthen local economies.

The EU’s Ambient Air Quality Directive (AAQD) entered into force in December 2024, with stricter pollution limits to be met by Member States by 2030. According to the latest validated data from 2024, 181 of 394 cities analyzed (46%) exceeded the new limits.

ClientEarth lawyer, Emma Bud, said: “The new EU air pollution laws are a once-in-a-generation opportunity to protect Europeans from toxic air, but with almost half of cities falling short of the standards, alarm bells are ringing. We particularly need to transform the transport system in cities, reducing dependence on cars.  Urban authorities can lead the way on so many of these policies, but they need strong support to protect people, including children and the most vulnerable.”

The report explains that meeting the new standards—particularly on tackling dangerous small particle (PM2.5) pollution—will require systemic changes to how governments work together and address the root causes of air pollution across sectors, including transport, agriculture, energy, and urban planning. The key to this deeper level of change, the report argues, is strong collaboration between all levels of government, as well as across business and industry sectors. To achieve compliance, authorities will need to implement measures suggested by the AAQD, including:

Using case studies from across Europe, the report shows how strong support for city leaders is key to tackling urban air pollution:

Steven Bland, Strategic Lead, City Systems Europe at WRI, said: “Air pollution remains a major public health and economic risk in European cities—one that is compounded by heatwaves. Making real progress requires tackling pollution across major emitting sectors, embedding air quality outcomes into investment frameworks, and coordinated action from local, regional, and national authorities. The EU now has an opportunity to show that cleaner air isn’t just an environmental goal—it’s an investment in people and a stronger, more resilient European economy.”

#5888. Construction Materials Stand Out At Greenbuild 2026

Last week, I attended Greenbuild International Conference + Expo 2026 in the North Hall of the Javits Center here in NYC.

After being overwhelmed by shows like CES and InterSolar Europe, the smaller scale of Greenbuild was a welcome break. At the show, there were a few heat pumps and Copper brought their battery-backed induction range, but less emphasis was placed on renewable energy storage and energy efficiency than other events. Many of the people I talked to had not heard the emissions reduction requirements of New York City’s LL97, which was a major motivator of Build Expo in the Spring. I also did not see much media presence at the show.

However, what stood out at Greenbuild was an emphasis on green materials. Although some of these companies had appeared at other shows, I had more opportunity to discuss and really get into the details at Greenbuild. A few exhibitors stood out.

Durra Panel was the first product to really attract my attention. Throughout history, straw has gotten a bad rap in parables. In contrast to the “Three Little Pigs” folktale, Durra Panel is using straw to create a building material that is not only more sustainable but, in many ways, better than traditional materials.

The standard boards are 96% compressed straw and 4% recycled craft paper. The process uses heat and pressure to bond the straw together with naturally occurring lignin, with no added binders needed. In the process, the straw panel gains a 1-hour (60-minute) Fire Resistance Level (FRL). The panels are 99.9% VOC-free, but the resulting panel, which incorporates biochar, actually absorb VOCs from the environment where they are installed. Standard panels are 50 or 58 mm thick (~2”).

Due to the mechanical strength of the panel, it can replace a partition wall or form a walkable roof without additional reinforcement or studs. Compared to a stud wall, the embodied energy drops from 153 MJ/m2 to 12.6 MJ/m2. It simply needs a header and footer to be mounted as a wall. According to Durra Panel, these walls provide an 84% time savings and 21% cost savings versus traditional building materials.

In a place like NYC, where rented commercial real estate and offices frequently have partition walls added and removed with high labor costs, those savings could add up quickly. In addition, the walls can be dismantled and re-used, further reducing cost and environmental impact.

The technology was developed in Australia decades ago, but it is expanding globally. Due to the acoustic performance, it is often used to build recording studios. Durra Panel recently selected Brocton, Massachusetts for their first US manufacturing facility.

Roughly 40% of global CO2 emissions come from the built environment, more than all transportation combined. In addition, a large portion of agricultural straw is burned as waste, putting more CO2 back into the atmosphere than all commercial air travel, according to Durra Panel. As much as 3.7 Gt of CO2 could be sequestered if agricultural straw could be used in construction. Carbon is locked away for the life of the building. At the end of life, the panels can be recycled or shredded and composted.

There were also several companies touting timber-related products. However, even if forestry is managed properly, utilizing waste is still preferable to felling trees. Using agricultural waste for construction makes you wonder what other waste streams could potentially be put to use.

Up to 8% of global emissions come from concrete manufacturing. That is more than air travel and global shipping combined. However, concrete has clear mechanical advantages as a durable, long lasting building material.

ZeroTwelve is incorporating biochar as a replacement for a portion of cement. In the process, the company claims more carbon can be sequestered than emitted, making the concrete carbon negative. At the same time, the concrete can become stronger and more resilient, depending on the mix. In addition, the biochar gives the concrete a slate-like dark gray appearance, making it well suited for decorative applications like floors or poured concrete countertops.

Nearby Pozzotive showed another solution, replacing a portion of cement with powdered glass, or pozzolan, from post-consumer recycled glass in what they call “urban mining.” Currently, much of the glass that gets put in recycling bins ends up in landfills. By eliminating the need to sort by color and size, more glass can be recycled. And it can be manufactured locally with low energy requirements, close to where the waste glass is generated. In the process, 94% of CO2 emissions are eliminated vs. the cement being replaced. The resulting pozzolan powdered glass can replace up to 50% of cement. The resulting concrete is stronger and more chemical resistant. In addition, the powdered glass makes the concrete more reflective, potentially reducing heat island effect.

Beyond the cement itself, Glavel showed off their foamed glass aggregate. Made from recycled glass using renewable energy, the aggregate both replaces crushed stone in the concrete and provides a level of insulation to replace separate foam insulation. It also makes the concrete structure lighter while maintaining high compressive strength.

There were many more options for building materials at the show. Something like a composite may have lower initial carbon footprint and durability but create challenges at end of life. Some natural materials may have degradability benefits but durability challenges. Each has its tradeoffs, and what works for one application may not be ideal in another. However, there is clear potential for the different materials to fit different applications and priorities..

While at the show, I briefly met with “legend” David Gottfried, co-founder of the US Green Building Council and founder of the World Green Building Council. He was promoting his new book, Regen360, which is positioned as “An Operating System For A Regenerative Earth.” While I have just started the book, it poses frameworks used to ask the right questions and align what we do with what we value.

I will not say that any material that I saw at the show is the answer. But if we change the way we think about building and start asking the right questions, green materials have the potential to contribute to a net positive impact.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5887. What Europe Can Learn From China If It Wants To Save Its Industry

Something I learned from my trip to China: you can be fast followers if you’re willing to learn from the best.

Sometimes a bit of distance can improve your perspective.

Walking down the silent streets of Beijing I listened to the future of mobility. Statistics can’t convey the silence of the streets. When an old — usually German or Japanese combustion engine drove by, I heard and smelled China’s smoggy past.

As I checked my phone, together with your ID — your most precious possession in China — I learned France and Germany had agreed a deal to remake Europe’s auto industry.

Brussels-bubble-me might have thought this deal — and the slightly worse European Parliament copy of the deal a week later — was more or less what was to be expected. Seen through a rosy lens the deal maintains a degree of pressure on carmakers to electrify whilst introducing elements of ‘technological neutrality’ the political right so desired.

Perhaps lowering the pressure on carmakers while using local content rules to bar Chinese carmakers like BYD from the company car market could be a clever ploy? It might just buy European industry some time.

But seen from China the car deal is simply terrible and displays exactly the lack of leadership and consistency that Chinese policymakers so deride in western politicians. One of my interlocutors said, “you have politicians, we have statesmen”. I pushed back, spoke up for Europe, but painful as it may be, these days, those words have a ring of truth to them.

In China two-thirds of all new cars are electric. Its car factories churn out the most amazing vehicles. Software defined, developed in record time, with outstanding infotainment and AI features, and electric of course.

Trucks aren’t going electric in some distant future as truck manufacturer bosses in Europe like to say. 40% of sales in China are electric already, with battery swapping not just some utopia but a market reality with trucks queuing at stations waiting for a six minute swap.

Oil demand from road transport is in freefall. Urban pollution caused by vehicles is a thing of the past. And as a result of its clear eyed policies and generous support, as well as a little help by Trump and Putin’s oil shocks, Chinese carmakers are now conquering the world.

Underpinning China’s EV might is a world class battery industry. I was in the world’s battery capital, Ningde, once a small fishing town, now a city of nearly 3 million people. The city produces 300gWh worth of battery capacity — more than the whole of Europe. That factory boom town could have been in Germany or Spain, I mused.

The way the China EV story is often told this was all inevitable. But it never was.

China once too had a legacy, combustion engine and oil industry that was saying “electric will never work”. Yes, some of those companies were foreign but they all had joint ventures and were locally embedded just like Toyota and Ford are in Europe. Yes, some visionaries were thinking of leapfrogging, but they were a minority for a long time.

China looked at its interests and set out its course. Pollution, oil dependency, climate and industrial strategy all merged into one coherent strategy. Now it is reaping the rewards.

What a contrast with the stitch up back in Europe, which one Financial Times reporter labelled ‘self-destructive.’ The deal means Europe will produce fewer electric cars — just 10% extra compared to today’s sales share by 2030, and it all but abandons the idea of a European battery industry (10% EU batteries required in 2028). Instead it imposes local content rules to protect European producers of seats and wheels.

In an obscene example of political disorientation, the deal creates a new category of “zero emission” vehicles powered exclusively by synthetic e-fuels and biofuels. I asked my Chinese partners why they didn’t do biofuels. “We don’t like solutions that compete with food” was the simple answer.

China is all in on electrification. CATL is building up its presence in Europe with three sites in Germany, Spain and Hungary. They are even branching out into shipping and even aviation as it seeks to disrupt another industry. Soon medium sized containerships with range big enough to cover the Mediterranean will hit the water. Made in … China, of course.

When I noted to CATL engineers that the technical challenges involved sounded hard, the reply was, “yes, but we’re only getting started”. If only this would have been the attitude of European industry.

No-one I met here dismissed Europe or its auto industry. I accept that it’s hard for incumbents — companies or continents — to be ‘first’ or ‘disruptive’. But if we can learn one thing from China it’s that you can be fast followers if you’re willing to learn from the best.

China and its EV industry aren’t invincible. Its economy is heavily indebted (300% debt-to-GDP), stagnant and relies on exports for growth. The auto price war means almost no-one makes any money. Nor is its industry a monolithic bloc. Some make very average cars, others make excellent cars, CATL doesn’t make cars at all. The Chinese export model will work only as long as its trade partners accept it.

What is crystal clear though is European industry can’t meet Chinese competition with old gas guzzlers. Those days are over. The future is for those who master battery tech and software, including self driving. The race doesn’t have to be over. Just remember, five years ago China was nowhere.

But Europe’s leaders will need to display a lot more vision and consistency if they want their industry, or indeed Europe itself, to be at the table rather than on the menu in the years ahead.

By William Todts, Executive Director, T&E

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5886. Scaling European Battery Recycling via the Circular Economy Act

Ahead of the Circular Economy Act, a joint letter from T&E, Recharge, Clean Tech for Europe and EIES calls for concrete measures to support the effective scaling of Europe’s material recovery and recycling capacity.

Recovering materials from end-of-life batteries is a key means to secure much needed critical minerals (CRMs) resiliently and sustainably. With increased recycling, Europe can reduce imports of CRMs, in turn reducing external dependencies. However, Europe’s battery recycling industry is struggling to scale, with over half of Europe’s battery recycling capacity currently at risk. The Circular Economy Act must introduce measures to help scale Europe’s battery recycling capacity.

European battery recyclers lack the needed feedstock. Europe currently preprocesses most battery waste domestically but exports the majority of black mass — CRM rich concentrate from shredded lithium-ion batteries — for final refining abroad. Analysis shows at least 60% of Europe’s black mass is leaving the EU, with this likely only the tip of the iceberg, and lots more black mass leaving the EU, hidden in different customs codes, and unable to be traced. This structural leakage undermines EU strategic autonomy, shifts high-value jobs abroad, weakens circularity and environmental protection.

Faced with financial pressures and higher energy costs, European recyclers also need strong political and demand signals in Europe, in order to encourage investment in EU refining capacity. This will be key to ensuring we can process and keep valuable materials in Europe.

As a result, the upcoming Circular Economy Act must urgently address Europe’s black mass leakage problem by:

The CEA must also support the scaling of European recycling capacity, encouraging investment in EU refining capacity with:

The Circular Economy Act can achieve a shift in mindset, and deliver much needed tools to support Europe’s battery recycling industry, securing effective material recovery capacity in Europe.

#5885. Op-ed: Plug-in solar expands access to renewables, but needs a strong safety framework

By Michael Becker, Global Business Director, Energy Storage/Energy & Power, CSA | October 29, 2026

Access to plug-in solar is becoming increasingly available across North America. As more consumers explore technologies such as balcony solar and plug-in photovoltaic (PIPV) systems, these emerging solutions have the potential to make renewable energy more accessible and support broader participation in the energy transition. Solar remains one of the most accessible and promising renewable energy tools being added to the grid, and plug-in solar products may help expand that access to consumers who have traditionally faced economic barriers to participation.

As adoption grows, however, products designed to be convenient and accessible must still be treated as electrical products that interact with a building’s infrastructure. Product design, connectors, wiring, system integration and installation practices all play an important role in safety and performance. Expanding access and maintaining confidence in emerging technologies are complementary goals, and achieving both will require continued attention to the frameworks that support responsible deployment.

Clear safety standards, code-compliant installation practices and appropriate regulatory requirements will be essential to helping ensure that innovation can reach more consumers safely and responsibly. Solar-related issues are often associated not with the panels themselves, but with connectors, wiring and improper installation practices. A major misconception is that consumers can simply purchase a solar panel with a standard plug and connect it to a household outlet. Responsible deployment of PIPV systems depends on several complementary elements working together: products evaluated to appropriate safety standards, compliance with the National Electrical Code (NEC), qualified installation practices, and applicable state and local requirements.

No single element alone can provide the framework needed to support broad and responsible adoption.

An important emerging component of that framework is UL 3700, which is helping establish a product-safety benchmark specifically for plug-in photovoltaic systems. UL 3700 can provide manufacturers with a clearer benchmark for product development and evaluation, while giving regulators greater confidence as they consider how plug-in PV should be addressed within electrical codes and regulatory frameworks. Continued industry involvement in the development of UL 3700 will also be important as the standard evolves toward broader consensus and adoption. At the same time, UL 3700 is only one part of the overall approach. It does not replace NEC compliance, qualified installation practices or applicable state and local requirements that govern the deployment of electrical systems.

Emerging safety benchmarks are already moving from concept to practice. CSA has certified a microinverter to UL 3700, providing an early example of how the standard can be applied to products entering the market. The certification demonstrates how a consistent benchmark can be applied as new technologies enter the market, while helping support confidence among regulators, manufacturers and consumers as plug-in solar continues to develop.

The regulatory landscape for PIPV continues to evolve, with proposed changes to the NEC, state laws and local codes and ordinances aimed at facilitating the safe installation of these systems. As the industry works to establish appropriate requirements, keeping standards, codes and regulatory frameworks aligned with technological advancement will be critical to supporting the growth of PIPV products. Well-aligned requirements can help create a pathway for innovation while maintaining consistency in how emerging technologies are evaluated and deployed.

Technology itself continues to advance rapidly. Improvements in inverter technology are helping make solar more affordable while enabling greater integration of renewable energy into the grid. As inverters become more energy-dense and cost-effective, they can help lower the overall cost of PV systems and support broader adoption. New inverter capabilities can also provide valuable grid-support functions that help integrate more renewable resources and contribute to a more flexible and resilient electric grid. As these technologies evolve, standards, codes and regulatory requirements must continue evolving alongside them so that innovation and deployment can advance together.

Ultimately, greater accessibility brings greater responsibility. As plug-in solar technologies become easier for consumers to adopt, the safety frameworks that support them must keep pace with innovation. By developing appropriate product standards, maintaining NEC-compliant installation practices, utilizing qualified installers and advancing state and local requirements alongside technological progress, the industry can help expand access to renewable energy while maintaining the confidence of consumers, manufacturers, regulators installers and other stakeholders.

The statement “A major misconception is that consumers can simply purchase a solar panel with a standard plug and connect it to a household outlet.” is, in and of itself a misconception. If the plugin systems are safely designed, that is exactly what they are meant to prov ide the consumer–a solar panel with a plug they can connect to a household outlet. The fact that several millions of such systems have already been plugged in around the world without incident amply demonstrates their safety. All the hand-wringing about safety seems to be originating with existing vested interests that stand to loose significant revenues built on the existing over-complicated and over-regulated systems.

Helpful safety framing. One practical reader check is to separate product evaluation from deployment permission: UL 3700 does not replace NEC compliance, installation instructions, or state/local and utility rules, and effective dates vary by state. PluginForSolar.com maintains a U.S. state-by-state guide plus exact-system readiness checks so readers can verify those layers before buying.

#5884. Sure Looks Like Trump Loves EVs After All

Jaws dropped to the floor last year when the US Department of Energy handed US automaker Rivian a $4.5 billion loan guarantee to build an EV factory Georgia. So much for the President’s heroic efforts to stop the vehicle electrification movement in its tracks. To ice the decarbonization cake, federal laboratories have just brewed up a vast, scientific conspiracy to help private sector partners develop next-gen batteries that will help make EVs more affordable for the masses. Hold on to your hats…

If Georgia seems an unlikely spot for an EV ecosystem to arise, guess again. Despite his red-state status, Georgia Governor Brian Kemp has embraced the vehicle electrification movement as a critical economic development strategy for his state.

“Georgia leads the nation in EV battery and vehicle manufacturing investments, totaling over $31 billion and creating over 38,000 jobs since 2015 — outpacing even Michigan by over $10 billion in investments over the same decade,” noted the Georgia Network for Electric Mobility at the University of Georgia in a report last year.

Georgia’s electrification ambitions earned a fresh wave of publicity during the last months of the Biden administration, when Rivian nailed down plans to build a new factory in the state with a $6.57 conditional loan guarantee in its pocket from the Loan Programs Office of the US Department of Energy.

After Trump took office he promptly shut down the LPO, allowing all but a scarce few decarbonization projects to move ahead (here’s one example). Somehow the Rivian factory loan also survived the Trump chopper, though at the lesser amount of $4.5 billion, and a groundbreaking ceremony took place last year.

If all goes according to plan, Rivian will soon join electric bus maker Bluebird on Georgia’s roster of EV makers. Another potential opportunity surfaced last Saturday, when Audi CEO Gernot Döllner took to the campaign trail with US President Donald Trump.

That was not a typo. Last Saturday, Döllner appeared on stage at a campaign rally in Georgia with US President Donald Trump, during which the two of them announced the culmination of a years-long plan to build Audi’s first ever factory in the US, a $4 billion facility to be constructed in Peach County, Georgia.

The new factory announcement was somewhat premature, to put it kindly. The supervisory board of parent company Volkswagen has yet to sign off on the deal, and the news blew up in Audi’s hometown, Germany, where Volkswagen is laying off workers.

Audi has yet to release details about its model plans for the new factory, but over the summer  company Döllner articulated a plug-in hybrid strategy for the US market. So, there is Trump on the campaign trail, making the case for an automaker that aims to help electrify the US mobility business, if not in full then in part. Go figure.

In the meantime, a fresh wave of next-generation EV batteries is also moving ahead under Trump’s watch, in the form of BattMan,* which is catchy shorthand for the newly hatched Battery Manufacturing Accelerator under the umbrella of the Energy Department.

“DOE’s Strategy for American Leadership in Batteries establishes the Department’s priorities to build a fully integrated domestic battery supply chain by 2030, achieve U.S. leadership in battery technology and manufacturing by 2035,” the Energy Department explained in a press statement on October 29.

BattMan is a technology accelerator that builds on a long list of previous DOE efforts to push the envelope on next generation energy systems, going back to its roots in the birth of the nuclear era. “The accelerator will focus on lowering manufacturing costs, speeding the transition of novel battery technologies from laboratory discovery to manufacturing deployment, and boosting U.S. leadership in energy storage manufacturing,” DOE explains.

That’s…interesting! Ford and General Motors are among the EV stakeholders that pivoted into stationary energy storage after the market for EVs took a swan dive in the US last year, but DOE has more than stationary applications on its mind. Mobile applications  also make an appearance.

“From transportation and power grids to medical devices and consumer electronics, batteries are essential to modern life,” notes Assistant Secretary of Energy Audrey Robertson.

“U.S. battery demand across transportation and energy-storage applications is projected to nearly quadruple, increasing from 179 gigawatt-hours (GWh) in 2025 to 707 GWh in 2035,” DOE emphasizes.

The BattMan consortium launched with support from the DOE Office of Critical Minerals and Energy Innovation under the leadership of DOE’s Pacific Northwest National Laboratory. Coincidentally or not, PNNL also launched the DOE “Innovation Center for Battery500 Consortium” in 2016, an effort that specialized specifically in advanced batteries for EVs.

Other founding members in the new BattMan effort also claim experience in the EV battery field. In all, the list includes Argonne National Laboratory, Oak Ridge National Laboratory, the University of Washington, Binghamton University, University of Alabama, Arizona State University, and University of Michigan-Dearborn.

The National Laboratory of the Rockies (formerly the National Renewable Energy Laboratory) is another partner in the BattMan effort. NLR expects to contribute its extensive experience in materials assessment and characterization to bear, along with its track record in electrode design.

That’s no small potatoes. Validating battery materials for durability and performance is a time consuming, expensive, and somewhat nerveracking process, with innovators having to re-invent the wheel with each new development. NRL has the know-how and the equipment to speed up the process.

“NLR researchers will establish physics-based machine learning models to analyze how specific defects and heterogeneities might impact cell performance years into the future, generating reliable data earlier in the development cycle,” the lab explains.

“The consortium will also work closely with domestic companies across the battery and materials sector to test and validate manufacturing processes for commercially viable production,” NRL elaborated, calling attention to the technology accelerator part of the program.

“Ultimately, the consortium aims to develop self-optimizing manufacturing systems using real-time data, automated feedback, and digital twin models that link virtual and real systems to identify opportunities to improve performance,” NRL added.

If all goes according to plan, NRL will produce statistically accurate models based on microscopic measurements, making it possible for battery manufacturers to move forward without investing in specialized equipment.

AI will play a prominent role in NRL’s contribution to BattMan, though the lab uses Trump’s preferred “SI” terminology. Either way, Trump himself all but guaranteed an EV sales recovery in the US when he decided to bomb Iran last February. When that comes around, the next generation of EV batteries will be queued up and ready to roll.

*Not to be confused with the Danish stationary energy storage specialist BattMan Energy.

#5883. Ireland’s Grid Flexibility Assessment Shows Why Location Matters

Ireland’s electricity regulator has put some useful numbers on the country’s future need for grid flexibility. Its first national assessment identifies about 234 MW and 58 GWh of potential upward flexibility requirements on distribution networks in 2028. Batteries, managed EV charging and industrial demand response could all contribute, but the figures become more interesting at the level of individual substations.

The Commission for Regulation of Utilities’ Flexibility Needs Assessment identifies a potential requirement of 0.6 MW and 3 MWh at the Naas 38 kV/10 kV substation in 2028, concentrated in January. Reinforcement is planned by 2030, when the assessment identifies no corresponding flexibility requirement. This describes a modeled network need, without a commitment to purchase a service or install a battery.

A battery elsewhere in Ireland could provide useful balancing services, help integrate renewable generation and earn revenue in electricity markets while contributing nothing to the particular constraint at Naas. The electrical connection matters. A resource has to change power flows across the affected equipment, in the required direction, during the periods when that equipment is constrained.

Flexibility must affect the constrained electrical equipment at the right time.

The distinction applies equally to demand response. A large fleet of electric vehicles might represent considerable flexible electricity demand nationally, but only vehicles connected in electrically useful locations can relieve a particular transformer. Their charging must also be adjustable without compromising the journeys they need to make. The same questions apply to refrigeration, heating and other industrial or commercial loads.

Timing adds another complication. Naas’s modeled 0.6 MW and 3 MWh figures describe different aspects of the expected requirement. Dividing one by the other does not establish that the network needs a battery discharging continuously for five hours. The duration of individual events, recovery between them and customer operating requirements all affect which resources could help.

There’s an important reality check in Britain. UK Power Networks’ March 2026 network assessment reports that the utility stopped seeking flexibility tenders for certain single-transformer substations whose constraints typically lasted 24 hours. The available flexibility market could not address those requirements effectively on technical and commercial grounds. In those circumstances, reinforcement or another engineering intervention may be more appropriate.

As distribution networks accommodate more electric vehicles, heat pumps, batteries and distributed generation, assessing flexibility at the right electrical location becomes increasingly important. National capacity estimates provide useful context, but investment and procurement decisions need much more specific evidence. The Naas example starts the first article in a series connected to The Flexibility Mesh, an open 99-page strategy report on how physical resources, permissions, operating capabilities and commercial arrangements fit together.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing analysis examines the Naas case, the alternatives available to utilities and the conditions that turn flexible capacity into a deliverable network service. It also links to the interactive Flexibility Mesh architecture.

#5882. XPENG L05 Offers A Bit More

XPENG recently launched sales of the new L05 in China and began shipments to global markets. Having seen a camouflaged prototype in Munich a few months ago, I had the impression that it was basically a longer L03. Looking at the production model, other than a few added features and details, that initial impression seems to have been an accurate assessment.

Unlike some of XPENG’s models targeting more premium segments, the L03 and L05 are based on 400V architecture. 3C charging rate goes from 10-80% in around 20 minutes. Overall charge times will come out slightly ahead of a Tesla Model Y (also 400V), but not as fast as other models in China on higher voltage architectures.

In terms of length, the L03 measures 4650 mm, while the L05 increases to 4870 mm. That puts a Model Y in the middle at 4791 mm. The increase in size adds roughly 50 kg to the weight of the L05 across trim levels.

The 58.2 kWh and 71.1 kWh battery sizes in the Chinese L05 are essentially the same as the L03, but the range of the larger battery in RWD drops from 650 km CLTC to 635 km due to the larger size and weight. At this time, the L05 is not being offered with AWD in China, although that might change in export markets. The 183 kW (~245 hp) motor is also the same while the 0-100 km/h time increases from 6.6 s in the RWD L03 to 6.8 s in the larger L05. Both are a little quicker than the European Model Y Standard at 7.2 s. We’ll have to see how the range works out under WLTP.

While the L03 impressed with a larger passenger compartment than the Model Y in a smaller footprint, it did have a somewhat smaller rear cargo area. Of course, because Tesla uses a non-standard measurement for cargo volume, comparing manufacturer numbers is pointless. However, the L05 does have a larger cargo volume than the L03 under standard measurements, which would be even more if measured to the top of the squared-off roof, rather than to the top of the seatbacks. XPENG has shown it holding 9 carryon suitcases with the rear seats up, which should be more than enough for normal people. Overall, the L05 has 41 storage locations throughout the vehicle, including under-seat storage.

In addition, the L05 expands the passenger compartment even more over the Model Y. The rear seats now also have folding rear tray tables and a 17.3” entertainment screen folds from the ceiling. In addition, there are sunshades that deploy to cover the sunroof. Other than the center touchscreen increasing from 15.6” to 17.3” and a few new color options, the interior is essentially the same up front. That includes heated, cooled and massaging seats and an easily visible HUD (European models will likely also get a driver display). Ultra trims include XPENG’s excellent NGP intelligent driving system with VLA 2.0.

List prices for the L05 range from 144,800 RMB ($21,722 USD at current exchange rates) for the base 560 Plus up to 176,800 RMB ($26,522 USD) for the top 635 Ultra SE trim. An EREV version with 1310 km of combined range comes in at 154,800 RMB ($23,222) up to 166,800 RMB ($25,022 USD). However, XPENG is offering a launch discount of 5000 RMB ($750). For comparison, the L03 EV currently ranges from 123,800 RMB ($18,586 USD) to 156,800 RMB ($23,540 USD) in China.

However, a G6 comes in at the same 176,800 RMB list price as the L05 635 Ultra SE. That premium vehicle has more power and faster charging on an 800V platform with close to the same range. And it isn’t lacking in technology. At 4758 mm long, it only gives up a small amount of space compared to the L05.

Of course, those are Chinese prices. XPENG has recently been rolling its latest models with the latest technology out globally. When adding ~20% VAT, 20.7% EV countervailing duty, 10% base tariff, shipping, misc. fees… EU prices would be higher. If the L03 starts at €35,600 in Germany, the L05 could start around €38,000. For comparison, the Model Y starts at €39,900 in Germany (intelligent driving is a €99/month subscription). A G6 starts at €43,600 in Germany.

Personally, I would stick with the slightly smaller and sportier L03 if given the option. For me, the space is sufficient. In some ways, the L03 is a better Model Y competitor because it is not trying to be more than a Model Y. I could also see potential updates coming soon to the G6 to position it as more premium and provide added differentiation. However, If you like the L03 but want a bit more room, the L05 could be a good option.

He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5880. Ford Wants Utilities To Help Make Bidirectional EV Charging Cheaper For Everyone

Earlier this week, the Ford Motor Company dropped word that anyone with a home EV charger who buys its new Ford Fathom electric pickup truck will not have to shell out cash for an external, wall-mounted inverter for bidirectional charging. Ford is also slashing the cost of its home EV charging and power management equipment. That’s not all. The automaker is also on a mission to convince all 3,000 utilities in the US and Canada to help accelerate the uptake of bidirectional charging systems.

With bidirectional charging, EVs can do something that gasmobile can. They can feed electricity back into household wiring, transforming themselves into emergency backup generators without the noise, pollution, and fuel cost of gas or diesel generators.

EVs equipped with bidirectional charging can also provide ratepayers with more control over their utility bills. With a home EV charger in hand, ratepayers can recharge their batteries during off-peak periods to take advantage of off-peak discounts, and they can use those discounted kilowatts to power their home during peak periods when rates are higher.

Drivers can also recharge at off-site charging stations that offers discounted rates, and ferry those kilowatts back for use at home.

Either way, the home needs to be equipped with an inverter to convert the DC current of an EV battery into the AC current used in buildings.

Or not, as the case may be. As explained by Ford earlier this week, the forthcoming Fathom pickup will come with its own built-in inverter. By eliminating the expense of an external inverter, Ford aims to sweeten the EV affordability pot, and the company has tapped Fathom to showcase the strategy at an MSRP under $30,000.

The missing link is the availability of utility-sponsored incentive programs that reward ratepayers for participating in managed demand programs. That includes rooftop solar, home energy storage systems, and even hot water heaters, with EV batteries being the icing on the cake.

“The opportunity is significant for utilities. But there are more than 3,000 utilities across the U.S. and Canada with largely unique interconnection processes,” Ford notes, drawing attention to another bottleneck limiting rapid uptake.

The problem is that interconnection processes are typically designed around rooftop solar and stationary batteries. “Bidirectional EVs bring new considerations because they are vehicles first, not stationary equipment,” Ford emphasizes.

Rooftop solar and home batteries are commonplace, but bidirectional EV charging has yet to scale up. To help push things along, last summer Ford began strategizing on a scalable interconnection system with the leading utilities American Electric Power, BC Hydro, Consumers Energy, Exelon, and Pacific Gas and Electric Co.

The aim is to get a process in place before the Fathom starts production next year, with a focus on collaboration between utilities, regulators, and OEMs. Accounting for periodic changes in bidirectional technology is another goal

As described by Ford the working group has already settled on the idea that interconnection should be required only for EV drivers who plan to deploy bidirectional EV charging in concert with the grid. Drivers who only charge up, or who only use their battery for backup power as needed, would not need to go through the interconnection process.

If you’re wondering why Ford is the only US automaker to offer bi-directional EV charging with a built-in inverter, that’s a good question. One answer is Auto Motive Power, the California-based power management specialist that Ford acquired in November of 2023.

“As part of the deal, Ford is acquiring AMP’s technology, its team of nearly 150 employees, as well as the company’s facility in Santa Fe Springs, California,” Wards Auto reported in November of 2023.

“Ford and AMP determined the advantage of their technology is best realized with deep integration into Ford’s EV products, which will allow for optimizing both cost and performance while improving experiences for our EV customers,” Ford elaborated in an email message.

You can say that again. As described by Ward’s, AMP had three systems in hand leading to the Ford acquisition. One is a Tesla NACS-compliant onboard charger that supports bi-directional charging through integrated DC fast charge contactors. “It can also be used to tap into an EV’s reserve battery power when the vehicle is not in use,” Ward’s observed.

Another system involves a V-to-X (vehicle-to-everything) power management system that supports grid connections. Rounding out the three is “ampEMU,” an energy management tool that integrates an onboard charger and power distribution system, along with fast charging for up to 500 amps.

By the time of the 2023 acquisition by Ford, AMP was already on its way to carving out an A-list slice of the bidirectional EV charging market. In 2020 the startup announced four new members on its advisory board, including former battery expert Kurt Keltey, who served an 11-year stint in strategic positions at Tesla before moving on to become VP of Automotive at Sila Nanotechnologies.

Sila CEO and co-founder Gene Berdichevsky also joined the AMP advisory board in 2020 along with former Canoo exec Phil Weicker and another Tesla alumnus, Troy Nergaard.

Ford is playing its AMP technology close to the vest, but a poster with the “ampforenergy” handle on the SpeakEV forum in August of 2023 provided some details about the EMU unit:

“The ampEMU is a comprehensive energy management system that manages the complete energy supply chain in an electric vehicle, from the vehicle’s charging infrastructure to battery management. It is backed by ampLOGIC for automotive big data analytics preventing battery degradation and enabling optimizations. ampEMU is compliant with the CCS, Tesla NACS, and CHAdeMO.”

“AMP’s EMU utilizes the latest SiC technology from Wolfspeed to drastically reduce the power electronics packaging volume,” ampedforenergy continued.

“This extremely compact unit will save space and reduce the vehicle’s overall weight, thereby improving range. EMU has the entire AMP Ecosystem in one unit offering cloud connectivity (ampLOGIC), battery management, power distribution unit, and charging solutions highly integrated with each other,” the poster added.

Speaking of Tesla, what happens next will be interesting. Tesla CEO Elon Musk’s pet project, the Cybertruck, was marketed as a pickup truck and it flopped epically, leaving the pickup truck field wide open for other EV makers to take a crack.

Meanwhile, last year Ford suspended its Lightning F-150 full sized electric pickup truck, evidently to focus its marketing program on the mid-sized Fathom, which is entering the market at a more affordable MSRP starting at $28,350.

Interested? Sign up here for an invitation to pre-order.

#5876. DTE utility program funded a 132-MW Michigan solar project

DTE Energy’s 132-MW Fish Creek Solar Park near Sheridan, Michigan, is now operational. The $268 million development was fully funded by customers who voluntarily participate in DTE’s MIGreenPower program. The solar park can generate enough energy to power approximately 30,000 homes.

“Our customers expect us to keep the lights on and keep bills as affordable as possible, and bringing new generation like Fish Creek online is part of how we deliver on that commitment,” said Matt Paul, president and COO of DTE Electric. “Fish Creek adds more Michigan-made energy to our system while supporting local jobs, generating new tax revenue and delivering lasting benefits for the communities that helped make this project possible.”

Fish Creek will generate tax revenue, supporting regional infrastructure, such as schools, first responders, road maintenance and veteran and senior citizen services. The solar park also created hundreds of local jobs during construction and will provide participating landowners with supplemental income.

In addition, Bushnell and Evergreen townships each received $330,000 through Michigan’s Renewables Ready Communities Award, a grant available to local governments that host large-scale renewable energy projects. The townships plan to invest the funding in town hall and fire station renovations, along with other community improvements.

Fish Creek is part of DTE’s broader plan to meet Michigan’s growing energy needs with a mix of resources. DTE currently has 20 wind parks and 37 solar parks across Michigan, which together generate enough clean energy to power nearly 1 million homes. DTE’s 2026 Integrated Resource Plan includes an additional 15 GW of renewable energy by 2042.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5875. Volkswagen Q3 Loss Largest Since Dieselgate

The Volkswagen Group on Thursday posted its worst quarterly numbers in more than a decade. According to German news source DW, the company reported an operating loss of €5.86 billion for the period July 1 through September 30, 2027. A massive write down of its stake in Porsche was responsible for much of the loss, while its sales in China continued to decline at an alarming rate.

“The nine month results make one thing clear: We cannot afford to lose any time. We must act quickly and decisively,” chief financial officer Arno Antlitz said in a video statement. DW was quick to point out that the cockamamie tariffs on cars imported into the US from Germany were another significant factory in the dismal financial picture for Volkswagen. The US is Porsche’s largest market and those tariffs have had a devastating effect on its sales in America.

For the third quarter, Volkswagen reported an operating loss of €5.86 billion — four times greater then the loss is posted last year in the third quarter. That marks the company’s worst performance since the Dieselgate crisis in 2015. Revenue from sales fell by 6.3 percent year on year to €75.3 billion. Deliveries to customers, unit sales, and unit production numbers all dipped by roughly 15 percent as the company reduced production levels in an attempt to adjust factory output to match lower demand.

For 2026 so far, the company has had gross sales of €233.4 billion, which was virtually the same as last year, but sales volume was off 11 percent compared to 2025, so it is selling fewer cars but at higher prices.  Operating profit was just €0.1 billion, compared with €5.4 billion a year ago. But so-called “special items” totaling €8.7 billion — including a non-cash write down of €6.4 billion on Porsche assets —  led to that nearly negligible operating profit.

For the year, Volkswagen now expects its profit margin to be just 1 percent — far less than the 5 percent it projected at the start of the year. The only bright spot in the financial picture for Volkswagen is that sales of battery electric cars are up 39 percent year on year.

Volkswagen’s first joint venture in China was with SAIC in 1984. At that time, China knew next to nothing about manufacturing high quality automobiles. Now more then 40 years later, the tables have turned and China is making what are perhaps the highest quality automobiles in the world. Volkswagen, which was once the teacher, is now the student. If it is to survive, it may be because of the contributions of its Chinese partners — SAIC, FAW, Anhui, and Xpeng.

Together, they have created Vega, a new technology package comprised of software, electronics, and a dedicated EV platform, making Volkswagen the only non-Chinese automaker with a locally developed technology foundation represented in the Chinese market. While the package will first be used for cars made and sold exclusively in China, the company hints it may become part of an export campaign. Sharp eyed readers will immediately spot the irony of a company that is being hammered by Chinese imports in its home market becoming a Chinese import itself, at least in some markets.

The China Main Platform (CMP) is designed for battery-electric and plug-in hybrid models in the A-segment. It is expected to achieve a CLTC range of over 600 km, with an efficient energy consumption of around 11 kWh per 100 km. Charging from 30 to 80 per cent will take 25 minutes. For the premium segment, the China Scalable Platform (CSP) features 850 volt architecture, which makes charging from 10 to 80 per cent in just 13 minutes possible. Models built on the CSP architecture are expected to have a range of 800 km or more, with energy consumption below 13 kWh per 100 km.

Autonomous driver assistance systems are now critically important to new car sales in  China, and so Volkswagen is putting a priority on providing cutting edge ADAS technology to its customers in China. Volkswagen’s software subsidiary, now called Carizon, has developed its first in-house L2++ package known as HyperSense 8. This system will enter series production in seven new electrified models from the Chinese joint ventures FAW-Volkswagen, SAIC-Volkswagen and VW-Anhui before the end of this year.

HS 8 utilizes a powerful onboard computer that supports navigation assisted driving both on highways and in urban traffic. Automated parking assistance for all everyday and parking scenarios is integrated into the new system. Thanks to a robust training system the can simulate up to 10 million kilometers of driving a day, the ADAS system will be able to handle even rare and unexpected extreme situations safely, the company says.

Level 3 systems, which allow drivers to take their hands off the steering wheel permanently under certain conditions, are scheduled to reach series production by 2027. Volkswagen has already obtained an official licence for real world road testing in Hefei. Volkswagen intends to have Level 4 autonomy in its cars for the Chinese market by 2029, thanks to the third generation of its CEA electronic architecture (CEA 3.0) and a Volkswagen-developed proprietary control chip known as  C7H.

Four years ago, Volkswagen Group launched its strategic transformation under the motto “In China, for China.” Now, it is making a strategic pivot that is more globally oriented known as “In China, Beyond China.” China is transitioning from a sales market into a central research hub and an export node for the company’s worldwide activities..

In fact, the deployment of China-developed electronics in Europe may soon be on the horizon. Today, models from Volkswagen produced in China are being exported to the Middle East, Uzbekistan, Kazakhstan, and parts of Sub-Saharan Africa.

By the end of 2026, exports are set to expand to markets such as Mexico, Egypt and Vietnam, followed by more countries in the ASEAN region and South America. Beyond vehicle exports, the combination of the CMP platform and CEA electronic architecture serves as the technical basis for upcoming model ranges throughout the Global South, particularly South America.

German mechanical engineering has always been the standard of the world, but Volkswagen Group stumbled badly out of the gate when it attempted to integrate computer technology into its cars, starting with the ID. 3 in 2020. It taught China how to build cars and then China taught it how to master digital technology. Maybe the combination will allow Volkswagen to become profitable and thrive in the future? Or not…..

#5870. It’s Not Enough To Electrify More. We Must Electrify Better.

EVs and other electric products are emerging not only as a climate strategy but also as a question of affordability, economic competitiveness, public health, consumer experience, energy security, and resilience to volatile fuel markets.

At 5 a.m., an electric bus glides out of its depot after charging overnight, ready to carry children to school without spewing diesel fumes. As the city stirs, heat pumps have already brought homes and offices to a comfortable temperature, using far less energy than their gas boiler counterparts. By midday, solar power charges delivery vehicles, cools warehouses, and helps factories run their most energy-intensive processes. Then, as millions of people return home and plug in their cars, smart chargers quietly schedule charging for later, when electricity is cheaper and the grid less busy.

Scenes like these are beginning to emerge across the globe: electric buses in Delhi, electric heat pumps warming homes through Norwegian winters, electric trucks entering Chinese freight and construction fleets. The shift to electrification has begun.

Most people will experience this transition not as an energy-system revolution, but as a quieter journey, a more comfortable home, a lower bill, or cleaner air. But taken together, these everyday changes mark an epochal shift in the global economy: from one built on burning fossil fuels to one driven by clean electricity, efficient technologies, and flexible demand.

But what exactly is electrification? How can using more electricity benefit people and the planet? And what happens when millions of new electric vehicles, appliances, buildings, and factories connect to a power system already under strain?

Put simply, electrification means replacing technologies that burn fossil fuels directly—gasoline-fueled cars, diesel buses, gas boilers, industrial furnaces, etc.—with alternatives powered by electricity. (Ideally clean electricity, but more on that later).

This shift is moving center stage not only as a climate strategy but also as a question of affordability, economic competitiveness, public health, consumer experience, energy security, and resilience to volatile fuel markets.

Yet the case for electrification is more compelling—and more interesting—than simply switching fuels. To understand its potential, we first need to look at how far electrification has progressed, why electric technologies use less energy and are cheaper to run, and what needs to happen to ensure electrification brings about societal and environmental benefits. Here, we dive deep into the current state of electrification and how to accelerate it in the right way.

The answer depends heavily on where (and what) you look at. In some technologies and markets, electrification is advancing remarkably quickly. Across the wider global economy, however, electricity still supplies only about 20% of final energy use (the energy actually consumed in homes, businesses, vehicles, and factories).

Road transport is currently the clearest example of electrification gathering pace. Global electric car sales have increased by almost 600% since 2020, rising to 20 million last year and now accounting for more than one in four new cars sold. Transport is advancing faster than buildings and heavy industry, but most vehicles already on the road continue to burn fossil fuels.

Progress also differs sharply across geographies. China, sometimes dubbed “the first electro-state,” has pulled ahead, with electricity now supplying almost 30% of final energy use, versus roughly 22% in the United States and 21% in the European Union.

Today’s pace is still far from sufficient. According to the International Renewable Energy Agency, electricity must supply more than half of all final energy use by mid-century to hold global temperature rise to 1.5 degrees C (2.7 degrees F), the limit scientists say is necessary for averting some of the worst impacts of climate change. That must come with a profound shift in how electricity is generated, with renewables providing around 90% of global power. Electrification and power sector decarbonization must therefore advance together.

Part of the explanation lies in how dispersed the transition is. Power sector decisions are concentrated among utilities, developers, regulators, and governments, while end-use electrification reaches into millions of homes, vehicles, businesses, and production lines.

Meanwhile, some activities are simply harder to electrify than others. Electric technologies can already serve road transport, buildings, and many lower-temperature industrial processes. In many cases, they are already cheaper to operate than their fossil fuel-powered counterparts and can deliver lower lifetime costs even where the upfront price remains higher. Electric cars and buses, heat pumps, induction cookers, electric water heaters and many industrial motors are increasingly available and affordable.

But for industries like steelmaking or chemical production, and for sectors such as aviation and shipping, direct electrification is harder. These activities require extremely high temperatures, use fossil fuels as a raw material in the production process, or need more energy than today’s batteries can efficiently carry. In these cases, hydrogen, synthetic fuels, or other low-carbon alternatives may be more viable alternatives than electrification, while carbon capture may be needed for residual emissions that cannot easily be avoided.

“Electrify Everything!” might be a tempting slogan, but it’s unlikely to be pragmatic—or even desirable—as an economic or industrial strategy. The more sensible (if admittedly less catchy) objective is to electrify directly in the many areas it makes overwhelming sense to do so, while reserving scarcer alternatives for the trickier-to-electrify sectors.

Electrification is an enabler, not an end goal in itself. An electric car powered by a coal-heavy grid could be worse for the climate than a car that runs on gasoline. Its benefit, in other words, depends on how the electricity is generated. But an electric car, heat pump, or industrial process can become ever-cleaner over time as the grid decarbonizes through greater use of wind, solar, nuclear, or other clean energy sources. A gas-powered engine or boiler, by contrast, remains locked into burning polluting fuels throughout its life. The full benefit of electrification therefore only comes from expanding electric end uses while also making the power supply progressively cleaner.

Additionally, one of electrification’s most counterintuitive features is that electricity consumption can rise while overall energy use falls.

The reason is efficiency. Combustion engines waste as much as 80% of their fuel’s energy (mostly as heat), converting just 20%  into useful motion. Meanwhile, electric motors convert around 80% of their electrical energy into motion. Heat pumps go even further. Rather than creating heat by burning fuel, they extract existing heat from the air or ground, typically delivering several units of heat for each unit of electricity consumed.

Replacing technologies that burn fuel directly with electric alternatives—replacing molecules with electrons—can therefore reduce the energy required to make the same trip or heat the same home.

This produces a second apparent paradox: your electricity bill may get bigger while your total energy bill gets smaller. An electrified household uses more electricity, of course; but its occupants no longer buy gas for their cars or heating systems. Because EVs and heat pumps use energy far more efficiently than combustion engines and boilers, the avoided fuel costs can more than offset the additional power consumed, even if electricity itself becomes more expensive. For example, one recent analysis estimated that fully electrifying a typical European household’s heating and transport could cut its overall energy bill by more than half.

The case for electrification cannot — and need not — rest on emissions reductions alone (although you might think that preserving a livable planet is a sufficiently persuasive starting point!).

Transitions move faster when the new technology is not merely cleaner but visibly better for people and businesses. EVs are quieter, smoother, typically cheaper to run, and more responsive than gas-powered cars, with instant acceleration from a standstill. Heat pumps can provide heating and cooling from the same system. Electric cooking improves indoor air quality and offers greater temperature control. Electric industrial equipment can make production more precise, responsive, and efficient. And all these technologies can help reduce costs by using energy more efficiently.

But a better technology is not automatically a better proposition for everyone. Upfront costs, access to infrastructure, and the way electricity is priced can determine which households and businesses benefit. A wealthy homeowner with a driveway can buy an EV upfront and install a charger; a delivery driver in Nairobi may need affordable finance, battery swapping, and reliable public charging. A well-insulated building can use a heat pump efficiently; a poorly insulated one may require costly upgrades before a heat pump makes sense.

So while technology creates the potential, it’s the infrastructure, finance, and policy that determine who can realize it. In Rwanda, for example, policies like tax incentives alongside private financing and battery-swapping networks are helping motorcycle taxi drivers switch to electric bikes. The shift cuts daily fuel and maintenance costs for drivers while also improving the city’s air quality and supporting the country’s target of electrifying 30% of its two-and three-wheelers by 2030.

Electricity demand is already soaring, driven by increased cooling needs, data centers, industrial growth, and wider access to reliable power. Add millions of electric vehicles, heat pumps, and industrial machines without planning where and when they will draw power, and today’s bottlenecks will worsen.

Electrification is therefore not only a power generation challenge. It is also a grid and coordination challenge, requiring action on three fronts:

Consider a city planning both thousands of new homes and a large electric bus fleet. If the housing authority, transport agency and utility plan separately (as they so often do), transformers may be upgraded twice, depot connections delayed and residents left waiting for both housing and cleaner transport. Plan them together — alongside local solar, batteries and bidirectional charging and controls — and the same investment can support several public priorities while simultaneously creating on-demand local energy-storage assets.

Building clean generation is pointless if customers can’t connect to it. Building new grid infrastructure is wasteful if existing capacity remains poorly used. Strategic electrification requires both expansion and much sharper coordination across power, transport, buildings, industry and urban development.

Now picture 6 p.m. in that same, increasingly electrified city I mentioned at the beginning of this article. Thousands of families arrive home and all begin cooking dinner. Cars are plugged in. Heating or cooling is still running, shops are still open, factories are still producing and data centers are still computing. The problem is not necessarily a shortage of electricity across the total system. It is that too much demand arrives in the same parts of the grid at the same moment. Networks strain, expensive backup generation switches on and billions get spent building infrastructure needed for only a few hours each year.

A car may be parked all night but need only a few hours to charge. Buildings can pre-heat or pre-cool before peak electricity demand. Water heaters, refrigerated warehouses, batteries and some industrial processes can also run a little earlier or later, shifting demand — and all without disrupting comfort or output.

None of this requires people to reorganize their evenings or become amateur electricity traders. They simply want their cars charged, their homes comfortable and their bills lower.

Smart controls can optimize energy use automatically. A customer might opt in to a program run by their energy supplier, which temporarily adjusts their thermostat, EV charger and/or water heater when electricity demand is particularly high — typically without any noticeable loss of comfort or convenience. The customer retains ultimate control and can override the change while receiving a lower bill or payment in return. Done well, this creates a triple-win: lower bills for the customer, less strain on the grid and fewer expensive investments.

At a constrained time and place, a megawatt of demand that can be shifted in time may be more valuable than a megawatt of additional supply. It can relieve a bottleneck immediately, reduce renewable curtailment (when renewable energy production is intentionally reduced because the grid doesn’t have the capacity to handle it or because there is not enough demand for it), and avoid building infrastructure used for only a handful of hours each year.

First, the electricity system needs to know what flexible assets are available and when they can respond. A grid operator cannot rely on thousands of individual EV chargers, batteries or commercial refrigerators if it cannot see where they are, how quickly they can react, or how much demand they can shift. These many smaller assets also need a simple route into the market (often by being grouped together by an “aggregator”). And they need to be paid for the value they provide.

Second, utility incentives set by regulators matter. A utility that earns more for building new substations and cables — as is often the case — will tend to build. If it is also rewarded for using batteries, smart charging and flexible demand to solve the same problem more cheaply, it is incentivized to choose the best combination.

Third, the business model has to stack up. Batteries, smart controls and flexible industrial equipment will not scale on one-off grants or short-lived pilots. Investors need predictable revenues, customers need affordable finance for the upfront costs and service providers need confidence that market rules will survive long enough to build a viable business.

The next phase of electrification is less about inventing some technological silver bullet than about governments, regulators and system operators creating the market rules, incentives and revenue streams that allow existing technologies to scale. In practice, that means treating flexibility as a real system resource — and paying for the value it provides.

Back to our city example one last time, but 15 years on.

The buses are all electric, but that is only the most visible change. Depots charge mainly when power is cheap and plentiful. Energy stored in their batteries is available for back-up power during emergencies like storms. Apartment buildings share chargers rather than reserving e-mobility for people with driveways. Homes stay comfortable through heatwaves and cold spells because efficient buildings, heat pumps, thermal storage and smart controls work together, pre-heating or pre-cooling when clean power is plentiful, easing demand when the grid is strained and helping maintain essential services during blackouts. Restaurants and schools cook without indoor combustion and the resulting air pollutants.

The industrial district has changed, too. Electric boilers, motors and heat pumps use energy more precisely, while flexible processes such as refrigeration, water heating or pumping automatically run earlier or pause briefly when the grid is under strain. New factories intentionally choose sites where clean power and network capacity are available. Data centers and other large energy users secure connections partly by bringing storage, clean on-site generation and flexibility that benefit the wider system.

For consumers, much of this is invisible. Their car is ready in the morning. Their building remains comfortable. Their energy provider shifts flexible consumption in the background and returns some of the savings. During a power disruption, batteries in buses, homes and commercial buildings help keep critical services running.

For the economy, the shift is deeper. Money once spent importing, subsidizing, and burning oil and gas is redirected into productive assets at home: clean generation, grids, efficient buildings, vehicles, storage, software and industrial equipment. Instead of continually paying for imported fuels, countries build lasting domestic capacity, strengthen industrial resilience and gain greater control over their energy future.

The prize is therefore larger than emissions reduction. It is better products and services, cleaner air, lower costs, stronger energy security and greater national control over the energy supply.

Electrification, then, is not simply about replacing one fuel with another, or adding ever-more demand to the grid. It is about moving from an energy system built around inefficient, polluting combustion to one built around clean electricity, efficient technologies and demand that can respond intelligently to the system around it.

Electrify more, yes. But above all, electrify better.

EVs and other electric products are emerging not only as a climate strategy, but as a question of affordability, economic competitiveness, public health, consumer experience, energy security and resilience to volatile fuel markets.
