#5864. Misconceptions about Mobility Growth

Higher speed is the main cause of mobility growth. When people go faster, they will travel further. This is the shortest summary of the history of passenger mobility.

An illustrative example is the high-speed rail connection between Paris and Lyon. After its opening in 1981, commuters were able to travel daily between these two cities. This was not possible before.

More generally, the rise in both ownership and speed of the car after the second World War, allowed for increasing distances from home to work, to school, shops, hospitals and friends.

The average distance a person travels per day tripled since 1960: from 10 to 30 (Dutch data). This growth is entirely caused by car use, being from door-to-door faster than both public transport and the bicycle.

Many empirical studies underpin that speed is the main cause of mobility growth. Total travel time is roughly constant: a person in a city or country travels on average 1.1 hour per day. When travel time is constant, higher speed inevitably leads to longer trips.

However, conventional wisdom makes us believe in other causes of mobility growth. Such as, rising income, decreasing costs of car driving, working from home or changing preferences in favour or against cars. The dominance of speed is overlooked in standard views by traffic engineers and politicians alike. Even statistics on average speed in urban and rural areas are scarce. This omission hides important consequences for transport policy. Here are three:.

Road building in congested areas does not solve congestion. Directly after opening, there is less congestion and car speed increases. However, after only a few years this gain disappears, because extra road capacity attracts extra traffic. One of the empirical studies showing this, is aptly titled “The Fundamental Law of Road Congestion”.

Secondly, when average car speed does not increase anymore, the growth in car mobility will end. This is the reality in rich countries with a mature motorway grid. Average car speed stabilized in the Netherlands since the turn of the century, and consequently car mobility per person even decreased by 3% between 2005 and 2019 (the last year before the Covid lockdowns). The UK, Spain, Belgium, Sweden and Luxemburg also show a drop in car mobility per person over the same period. Other countries will follow. Neglecting car speed as the main driver behind traffic growth results in traffic forecasts that are systematically too high.

Finally, cars in large cities ride less than half as fast as the country average. Therefore, citizens of metropolises travel half as many kilometres by car than others. Trip distances are shorter in urban areas, destinations are nearby, and the share of public transport is manyfold that in rural areas. Therefore, good accessibility can be achieved by urban density, in a mix of housing, workplaces, shops and parks. Urbanisation is the cheapest way to improve accessibility, while reducing car use, at the same time.

So, conventional wisdom on mobility growth conceals that road building will not solve congestion, that urbanisation reduces the need for mobility, and that car growth is ending. Unsurprisingly, these misconceptions favour vested interests from car and road industries. The neglect of speed as the main driving force behind mobility growth, hinders effective policies towards affordable and sustainable accessibility.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5860. The Zombie Engine: How Brussels Bureaucrats Are Breathing False Life Into Combustion

If you poured rapeseed oil into a combustion engine and declared it a “zero-emission vehicle,” you would be laughed at. But in the EU Parliament, this fiction is being drafted into law.

If you poured rapeseed oil into a combustion engine, watched toxic exhaust billow from the tailpipe, and declared it a “zero-emission vehicle,” you would be laughed at. Yet inside the corridors of the European Parliament, this exact fiction is being drafted into law.

Under heavy pressure from the fossil fuel lobby and legacy carmakers, EU lawmakers are preparing to endorse a bizarre new regulatory beast: the VEEF, or “Vehicle Exclusively running on Eligible Fuels.” In plain English, these are standard combustion-engine cars promised to run exclusively on liquid biofuels or e-fuels. In exchange for that promise, Brussels plans to grant them a legal tailpipe emission score of zero.

It is regulatory alchemy at its worst. Cloaked in the seductive language of “technological neutrality,” this proposal creates an escape hatch for an obsolete technology, all while setting up ordinary European drivers for an expensive logistical nightmare.

It is just another desperate tactic to extend the life of the combustion engine by any means possible.

To understand the absurdity of the biofuel car, look at what actually happens in the engine. It’s still the exact same combustion. A piston firing on biofuels produces the same toxic cocktail of nitrogen oxides, carbon monoxide, and fine particulates that has choked European cities for decades. Labeling these cars “zero-emission” launders the pollution through a regulatory loophole and continues to foster emission-induced pathology.

Nor does it save the climate. The average biofuel blend available today delivers a modest 20% to 40% emissions saving compared to fossil fuels. When you factor in the indirect land-use change of first-generation crop biofuels ploughing up forests to grow food for engines, crop-based biofuel cars actually emit 23% more greenhouse gases than a standard petrol car.

Biofuel cars are being sold to the public under the guise of affordability, yet running them is punishingly expensive. Hydrotreated vegetable oil (HVO) biodiesel costs around €13 per 100 kilometers to run, compared to roughly €7 for an equivalent electric vehicle. Pushing drivers toward these cars locks households into volatile, high fuel costs at the exact moment renewable electricity prices and battery costs are tumbling.

Then comes the phantom fuel problem. Europe possesses a strictly limited supply of biofuels. The continent produces only enough advanced petrol biofuel to power roughly one million cars.

The Parliament’s compromise envisions allowing food-crop fuels until 2032, before shifting new biofuels models to run strictly on advanced wastes and residues from 2033 onward. But because sales skew heavily toward petrol while advanced biofuels skew toward diesel, the arithmetic collapses immediately. If carmakers sell one million petrol VEEFs cumulatively, European biofuel supplies would run dry immediately. Millions of drivers would be left with nowhere to fill up.

How does Brussels propose to stop a driver from pulling up to a regular pump and filling their “zero-emission” car with cheaper fossil petrol?

Through a bureaucratic and technical monster. The proposals require that manufacturers install complex onboard systems to ensure fuel exclusivity: fuel sensors, tamper-proof hardware, and engine-throttling software to strand your car if the wrong liquid is detected. Pumps would require cryptographic digital handshakes with the car’s chassis number to authorize a fill-up. On top of that, it could require two separate, parallel, continent-wide pump networks; one for pre-2033 crop fuels, another for post-2033 advanced fuels.

For an EU Commission committed to deregulation and “simplification,” this is pure loophole-hungry nonsense.

Naturally, the industry knows this. The fuel-combustion lobby is already quietly pivoting to their real goal: “mass balancing.” Under this proposal, carmakers wouldn’t need to put pure biofuels into your tank at all. Instead, by using the standard blended biofuel mix of today, they could manufacture “virtual” clean cars, allowing them to keep selling dirty combustion engines while selling fewer electric vehicles.

This is not just a technical Eurocrat debate, it is an industrial fork in the road. More than €200 billion has already been committed to building Europe’s electric vehicle and battery supply chain. That capital was deployed on the promise of a clear, stable regulatory path to zero emissions by 2035.

While China dominates the global battery supply chain and electric vehicle sales surge worldwide, European policymakers are entertaining a bizarre scheme to keep 19th-century technology on life support.

The VEEF loophole solves no climate target or air pollution, helps no driver, and builds no clean industry. It is simply a regulatory Trojan horse designed to buy legacy polluters a few more years of business-as-usual. When the vote arrives, lawmakers must slam the door shut on this costly diversion.

If you are looking for a terrifying Halloween costume, a zombie combustion engine masquerading as zero-emission is about as scary as it gets.

#5858. Chinese-linked solar developer loses appeal over Texas grid interconnection

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The U.S. Court of Appeals for the 5th Circuit said Texas has a “free lane” to enforce restrictions on foreign-owned companies’ access to the state’s electric grid.

Daniel Skees, a partner at the law firm Morgan Lewis who works on energy and infrastructure, told Utility Dive in an interview that even though the particular law at the center of the ruling is unique to Texas, the decision has implications for other markets and could be appealed to the U.S. Supreme Court.

“I think it’s a big deal because we’re seeing a lot more action at the state level dealing with issues related to foreign ownership in critical infrastructure sectors, and I think there had been a feeling for a while that ultimately this stuff might be preempted by CFIUS review,” Skees said. “This is really a nice, clear opinion that goes through really just a basic CFIUS preemption analysis and says, ‘no, this is aimed at something different.’”

The ruling will likely give states that are considering similar foreign-entity restrictions assurance that such laws could survive legal challenge, he added.

Skees coauthored an analysis published this week that noted the ruling coincides with emerging federal restrictions on foreign-made grid equipment, making early coordination on energy regulatory and foreign-investment diligence particularly important for developers, utilities, investors and other stakeholders.

GH America Energy first filed its lawsuit in 2024 against Electric Reliability Council of Texas officials after being notified that a pending interconnection application for its Blue Star Solar Project in Val Verde County was being cancelled due to lack of compliance with the Texas Lone Star Infrastructure Protection Act of 2021. The company was in the midst of pursuing a number of renewable energy projects in the state when the law came into effect, and it claimed that that denial caused it to sell two other projects at a substantial loss.

GH America Energy could not be reached for comment.

Mark Stover, executive director of the Texas Solar+Storage Association, said his organization shares the goal of the Texas Legislature to protect the grid from foreign interference and remains engaged with policymakers regarding LSIPA and other measures.

“Protecting infrastructure, information technology, and operational technology is critical,” he wrote in an email. “LSIPA is an important grid security tool and it is the responsibility of industry to follow the law. Our members are actively engaged in compliance and we support open communications among industry, the State of Texas, and the federal government regarding approaches to grid security.”

#5852. Eugene Becomes 5th Oregon City to Adopt Home Energy Score Requirement

Energy Audits Support Emissions Reductions and Energy Efficiency Upgrades

Eugene, Oregon — In a 6-1 vote on Wednesday, October 28th, the Eugene City Council adopted the Residential Energy Performance Disclosure Requirement ordinance, a policy commonly referred to as a Home Energy Score requirement. The vote places Eugene among four other Oregon cities, Portland, Hillsboro, Milwaukie and Bend, that have similar Home Energy Score ordinances. The vote comes in the midst of a larger trend of cities adopting a variety of policies to aid community members navigating home retrofits and efficiency upgrades as support for building electrification grows in Oregon, and nationwide in the face of efforts to undermine existing climate policy at the Federal level.

“We applaud the City Council for taking this common sense step and establishing a home energy score requirement in Eugene. This policy will ensure that home buyers have the information that they need to make informed choices when making what is often the most important financial decision in their lives,” said Bethany Cotton, Conservation Director with Cascadia Wildlands. “The vote is an important step forward to tackling building emissions, reducing energy costs, and meeting the City’s Climate Recovery Ordinance goals to reduce emissions by 50% by 2030.”

The adoption of the ordinance is the first major step from the City to reduce building emissions since the Ninth Circuit’s April 2023 decision on the Berkley building electrification requirement, which invalidated the City’s February 2023 residential building electrification ordinance. The passage of this policy in Eugene also marks more than 1 million Oregonians living in a jurisdiction with a Home Energy Score disclosure policy, representing nearly 1 in 4 Oregon residents (24.4%).

“The Ninth Circuit’s disastrous decision in 2023 set Eugene, and cities across the region, back for years on climate action and energy efficiency. While this policy is just a small step, it is a critical signal that the City is ready to begin moving decisively to regaining lost time and transitioning away from polluting fossil fuels,” said Dylan Plummer, Deputy Director of the Sierra Club’s Clean Heat Campaign. “We look forward to supporting Eugene and other local governments to continue to push the ball forward on common sense policies to require building decarbonization.”

The ordinance requires that a home energy score assessment be conducted and posted when a home is listed for sale. These assessments review the average energy cost of a home, evaluating existing appliances, heating systems, windows, doors and insulation, as well as the climate emissions associated with the home, and score the house’s energy use on a scale of one to ten. The reports include recommendations for how to improve a home’s energy efficiency and reduce emissions, and include key information about existing incentives and subsidies at the local, state and federal level.

“Eugene Water and Electric Board customers can access significant incentives to support home electrification and energy efficiency including zero interest loans and cash subsidies for weatherization and heat pumps for space and water heating. Sadly, these programs are deeply underutilized because many folks are not aware they exist,” said Meredith Tufts, Coalition Coordinator with the Fossil Free Eugene Coalition. “The Home Energy Score requirement will ensure that every new home owner has the information they need to take advantage of these programs to reduce their energy bills and climate pollution.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. #5848. AEP increases investment plan 30% on generation, transmission spending

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

American Electric Power’s contracted large loads jumped 22%, to 84 GW, mainly on a 10.4-GW data center in Ohio being developed by SB Energy, company officials said Wednesday.

American Electric Power increased its five-year capital expenditure plan to $100 billion, up 30% from its previous $78 billion plan, driven by expected generation and transmission spending, company officials said during an earnings call on Wednesday.

AEP, based in Columbus, Ohio, plans to spend $20 billion on gas-fired generation and $37 billion on transmission through 2031, up from $13 billion and $33 billion under its last investment plan, respectively, according to company presentations.

“Contracted customer demand is growing faster than the operating output of our existing fleet, creating a clear durable need for new baseload dispatchable generation,” Trevor Mihalik, AEP executive vice president and CFO, said on the earnings call. AEP also expects its utilities will spend $8 billion on renewable energy projects over the next five years.

During the earnings call, William Fehrman, AEP chairman, president and CEO highlighted a roughly $5 billion, 765-kV transmission project in Ohio and a $4 billion fuel cell project in Wyoming as examples of the company’s investment opportunities. The projects were included in AEP’s updated capital spending plan.

“Construction is well underway on the Wyoming fuel cell project, which is underpinned by a 20-year power purchase agreement with Google,” Fehrman said.

AEP plans to use Bloom Energy fuel cells for the 702-MW project, which it expects to bring online in 2028.

And in Ohio, AEP expects to start building the Piketon transmission project early next year to support a 10.4-GW data center campus planned by SB Energy, Fehrman said. That data center was the biggest driver in AEP’s increase in contracted load in the third quarter, according to Mihalik.

The project’s three 765-kV transmission lines will each provide about 3.2 GW of capacity for the data center complex, and they have targeted in-service dates of Dec. 31, 2028, 2029 and 2030, AEP said in its quarterly filing with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission. The data center’s initial 800 MW of load is expected to be served with existing transmission capacity and does not require additional regulatory approvals, AEP said.

SoftBank and its affiliates will pay for the transmission project, including an 11.35% base return on equity, which may increase to 11.99% or decrease to 10.71% depending on the final project costs and inservice dates. The transmission project requires approvals from the PJM Interconnection, the Public Utilities Commission of Ohio and the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission, AEP noted.

Meanwhile, AEP utilities are preparing to add about 9 GW in gas-fired generation through 2032, according to the company’s presentation.

AEP is continuing to consider creating “gencos” — or generating companies — to serve large loads, Fehrman said.

“Should a genco structure emerge as a viable pathway in any given state, particularly West Virginia and Indiana, we could ultimately look to house a portion of the plan or future gas plants in that framework,” he said.

AEP’s total retail electric sales increased 11% in the third quarter and 8.7% in the first nine months this year, mainly driven by data center development, the company said in its SEC filing.

AEP’s net income increased to $1.2 billion on $6.8 billion in revenue in the third quarter, up from $972 million and $6 billion in the year-ago period, driven by data center sales and warmer weather, according to the SEC filing.

AEP’s utilities have about 5.6 million customers in Arkansas, Indiana, Kentucky, Louisiana, Michigan, Ohio, Oklahoma, Tennessee, Texas, Virginia and West Virginia.

#5843. BAAJA gives federal regulators ‘bigger hammer’ to break transmission blocks

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“I think the cost-sharing elements are by far probably the most important part of this bill,” Rep. Josh Harder, D-Calif., told Utility Dive.

BAAJA’s motion to proceed is scheduled to receive a cloture vote in the Senate on Nov. 9, according to the Bipartisan Policy Center. It increases FERC’s power by eliminating the federal right of first refusal for incumbent utilities, stripping their automatic right to build new regional lines, and requiring FERC to issue a rule requiring each transmission planning region to file a consolidated interconnection and regional transmission planning process.

The legislation also states that FERC can require public utilities to make cost-effective grid upgrades and determine a penalty for utilities that fail to comply within a timeline decided by FERC — including a potential reduction of their return on equity.

FERC must establish “rate treatments for regional and interregional transmission planning and cost allocation,” the bill says, and require tariffs that allocate the cost of doing so to customers that benefit.

“I think the cost-sharing elements are by far probably the most important part of this bill, and my hope is that can unlock a lot more collaboration as opposed to what we have now, which is kind of folks pointing the finger at each other,” Harder said. “We have seen, at least at the FERC level, essentially no new interregional transmission built in a long, long time.”

“That's unacceptable,” he added, “and it's just not doing the job to lower prices for folks. So I think it's going to make some folks uncomfortable, but it's absolutely necessary to incentivize transmission getting built.”

Chirag Lala, vice president of research and chief economist at the nonprofit Center for Public Enterprise, and co-author of a recent paper that proposed a federally coordinated macrogrid, said in an October brief about BAAJA that the legislation “gets transmission right” but noted that the interregional planning mechanism lacks a “‘forcing function’ in the event regions fail to submit a planning process — only a six-month extension, after which the outcome is unclear if no process is submitted.”

Advait Arun, senior associate for energy finance at the Center for Public Enterprise and co-author of the macrogrid paper, told Utility Dive in an email that in addition to including a “forcing function,” BAAJA could potentially be amended to add “more firepower” to the federal government’s transmission finance programs, such as the U.S. Department of Energy’s $2.5 billion revolving Transmission Facilitation Program fund.

Arun also suggested an alteration to the “federal support restriction,” which currently blocks DOE’s Office of Energy Dominance Financing — formerly known as the Loan Programs Office — from financing projects which have already received some other kind of non-tax credit federal commitment.

“One worry I have here is that there is no money in any of these proposals,” he said about the Build More Agenda. “Ultimately, a lot of the private investment that these suggestions aim to catalyze through targeted streamlining of regulations will still be at the mercy of a temperamental macroeconomic picture.”

However, Harder noted that the U.S. is facing a “lack of fiscal headroom that we're going to have at a national level over the next couple of years,” adding, “I think that a lot of policymakers on both sides of the aisle are going to be looking for pro-growth policies that don't demand enormous new spending."

When asked if BAAJA might be amended in the House, Harder said, “This is a pretty delicate negotiation that's gone on for over two years, and anytime you have something of this magnitude, it's certainly a little bit balanced on a knife.”

Harder acknowledged that tribal organizations have voiced opposition to the changes the bill makes to the National Historic Preservation Act, and there is “some disagreement about what they would actually mean in practice, so I think that is something that might end up being clarified. But … the vast majority of this bill, I think, will probably remain as-is.”

The Build More Agenda suggests expanding on programs like DOE’s Utah Frontier Observatory for Research in Geothermal Energy, which “has helped demonstrate a commercial pathway for enhanced geothermal,” and streamlining regulations such as requirements for nuclear plant developers to “use specialized nuclear-grade materials … in non-safety structures of a plant where using commercial-grade materials would be just as safe.”

In regard to public perception of the safety of nuclear energy, Harder said, “I think we've come a long way for sure, and I think one of the real pressures is that people see the cost of electricity has skyrocketed. And if we really want to electrify our economy and move it towards 100% clean energy, then nuclear — and geothermal as well, that clean, firm power — have got to be a really important bedrock.”

Some moves from the Trump administration to encourage nuclear development, Harder said, “frankly would have been better in legislation.” He said he would like a “2.0” of the Accelerating Deployment of Versatile, Advanced Nuclear for Clean Energy Act of 2024, to “incorporate some of the changes that the Trump administration has made at the [Nuclear Regulatory Commission].”

“But the way that I would characterize it is, move it from a zero-risk approach — where any new nuclear project presents more of a risk than the status quo — to a real risk-based approach,” he said. “You can't have any regulatory agency that doesn't want to build anything at all ... And that's the way that unfortunately the NRC has been for decades.”

#5839. SnapNrack increases domestic manufacturing with new Texas factory

Solar mounting and racking manufacturer SnapNrack, a subsidiary of Sunrun, will host a ribbon-cutting ceremony on Nov. 4 to celebrate the opening of its new facility in El Paso, Texas. The new operational site has onshored SnapNrack’s assembly and distribution operations from its former facility in Juarez, Mexico.

By bringing assembly and distribution directly into Texas, SnapNrack eliminates international customs friction and cross-border transfer delays. The El Paso facility leverages the I-10 and I-25 interstate corridors alongside the region’s “southern gateway” rail network to distribute products across the country.

Manufacturing and assembling racking solutions within the United States directly supports solar installers and project developers in meeting domestic content requirements.

#5837. Drive Forward or Turn Back? Four Reasons to Vote for a Strong Automotive Package

The coming weeks will be crucial for the future of the EU’s car and van CO₂ standards and Clean Corporate Vehicles Regulation — a choice that will shape Europe’s car market for the next decade.

Driving forward means a competitive, EU-made EV industry and clean and affordable mobility; turning back means locking in energy dependence, industrial decline, and polluting, expensive mobility paid for by citizens and companies.

Households could save €70 a month by switching from petrol to electric. Large companies will save even more. Strong CO₂ standards mean affordable electric cars. Electrifying corporate fleets will speed up supply.

Weak CO₂ standards will delay the moment electric cars cost the same as petrol cars. Citizens are stuck in a fossil fuel trap: diesel already costs €30 extra every time they fill their tank.

Europe is going electric: 38% of new cars sold in August were fully electric. Strong CO₂ standards keep European carmakers in the race. Made-in-EU requirements mean more jobs and investment.

Weak car CO₂ standards and corporate fleet targets means 22 million electric vehicles won’t be built in EU factories (T&E based on ACEA). Clinging to the combustion engine won’t protect jobs or investments from our competitors.

Cars and vans produce 16% of the EU’s CO₂ emissions. A strong automotive package puts them on track, keeping 720 million tonnes of CO₂ out of Europe’s air by 2050.

Weaker rules on cars CO2 standards would add 1.4 billion tonnes of CO2 by 2050. After Europe’s summer of climate disasters, this rollback would threaten our climate objectives and future generations.

Electric cars saved €2.9 billion in oil imports last year alone. The EU is more independent by going electric with locally sourced renewables that strengthen our energy security. Weakening CO2 standards could mean 900 million more barrels of imported oil by 2035.

Giving combustion engines on the road longer will cost Europe €74 billion. Paid by households, straight to oil-rich countries.

#5836. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5835. EVs Sales Are Rising And Another Gasmobile Bites The Dust

The automotive press is practically aflame with rumors that Ford is replacing its Ford Escape crossover SUV with an electric version, also to be named Escape. That has raised quite a ripple across the esoteric field of car-naming. Normally when a model retires, so does the name. The US auto industry is littered with has-beens, wanna-be’s, never-was, and just plain exhausted cars that retired along with their names. Hmmm….perhaps Ford is sending a not so subtle signal about the future of EV sales in the US…

By now, everyone knows that EV sales cratered last fall, after US President Donald Trump and his Republican majority in Congress engineered the “OBBA” tax bill to eliminate federal tax credits for EVs.

Still, EV sales did not actually stop in their tracks, let alone reverse course. A fresh wave of off-lease, used EVs helped keep the momentum up earlier this year, and some automakers (looking at you, Subaru) stayed on track with plans for new EVs in the US.

Plug-in hybrids also factored in as a sort of gateway drug, with Audi CEO Gernot Döllner among those looking forward to strong EV sales in that category.

EV stakeholders must smell blood in the water as Election Day 2026 approaches. With polls indicating that  Trump will lose his chokehold on federal EV policy, news about EVs has been tumbling through the Intertubes over the past several weeks. Among the examples, Slate Auto opened its first showroom in Chicago, and General Motors unveiled its gen-4 EV platform.

Somewhat bizarrely, the Trump administration also contributed to the news cycle with the launch of the new “BattMan” battery collaboration earlier this week. Under the umbrella of the US Department of Energy, BattMan hooks up federal laboratories with academic and private sector partners to push the envelope on next-gen batteries for transportation applications as well as stationary energy storage.

Of course, Tesla also weighed in with its “flying car” stunt, but that was just a stunt. Over in the world of street legal EVs, Ford has been working the publicity machine to gin up interest in the forthcoming Fathom all-electric pickup truck, which is on track for volume production early next year at an MSRP starting at the affordability benchmark of $28,350 with a built-in inverter that cuts the cost of bidirectional charging, too.

The Fathom illustrates how much blood, sweat, and tears go into naming a new car. It’s a mid-sized pickup like the popular Maverick, but Ford went off in a new direction with the Fathom, considering hundreds of options over a months-long naming journey before settling on a maritime-adjacent reference to deep understanding.

The Fathom will be the first EV to roll off Ford’s new “Universal EV” manufacturing platform, and the automaker has indicated that the next vehicle will be a compact SUV. The consensus has settled on something resembling the Escape crossover SUV, all the more so because Ford has been laying plans to retire the gasmobile…but maybe, not the name.

That’s where the ears of the automotive press have pricked up, drawing attention to the Escape landing page that depicts the rear end of a forlorn, rain-splattered Escape heading off into the dustbin of automotive history along with its dual exhaust system.

And, here’s where things get interesting. The text on the landing page simply notes that the Ford Escape is “tapping the brakes,” which has been interpreted as a pausing message, not a forever message.

However, on the same page Ford provides a good reason why the 2026 Escape will not fly. The car is not for sale in California and several other states.

“The 2026 Ford Escape® is not certified for emissions requirements with the California Air Resources Board,” Ford states in a drop-down link. “Therefore, it is not available for purchase by customers intending to register their vehicle in states that have adopted California’s certification requirements for the 2026 model year, specifically California, Massachusetts, Oregon, New York, Washington, and Vermont.”

Ford goes on to suggest two other gas-powered SUVs that are street legal in California-compliant states, the 2026 Bronco Sport SUV and the 2026 Ford Explorer SUV.

So, is Ford really banking on the Escape brand to boost its EV sales in the US? Maybe! In August, the Detroit Free Press summarized a swirl of rumors around the idea of an electric Escape, citing insider sources including a Ford dealer with access to the company’s program codes.

However, in the same report, Reuters cites an inside source who indicated that in 2029, Ford plans to introduce a new entry-level SUV in hybrid and gasoline powertrains.

“At the Las Vegas meeting, the executives, the dealer said, assured dealers that the automaker is committed to hybrid powertrains going forward,” Reuters emphasized.

Hmmmm…so much for an all-electric escape. Back in May, the Canadian Ford dealer MSAFord also raised some questions about an all-electric replacement for the Escape in a blog post (breaks added for readabilty):

“Production of the Ford Escape and Lincoln Corsair ended at the Louisville Assembly plant last December, leaving a giant hole in The Blue Oval’s lineup as it pertains to small crossovers.”

“This is particularly true of the Escape, which disappeared at a time when new vehicle shoppers are increasingly looking for more affordable options, too.

“However, back in December 2022, a report surfaced from AutoForecast Solutions, claiming that the Ford Escape would live on as an all-electric crossover, though it has thus far remained unclear if that plan is intact ever since”

Readers, what do you think? Is half an EV better than none? Full EV sales have yet to surge, but plug-in hybrids have become more popular on the heels of US President Donald Trump’s witless decision to bomb Iran last February.

EREV (extended range EVs) are another halfway-there option that will factor into an uptick in EV sales. The Scout Motors branch of Volkswagen, for example, has stated that EREVs account for the majority of its reservations so far.

Then there’s the pure EV players, including robotaxi stakeholders alongside Slate and Rivian, which is forging ahead with plans to construct a new factory in Georgia.

As for industry leader Tesla, that’s a whole ‘nother can of worms. CEO Elon Musk has earned a fair share of bad publicity in recent years, as depicted in the new “Musk” documentary. However, the film wrapped sometime last year, leaving Musk’s latest hi-jinks for the sequel.

Photo: Ford says buh-bye to the Escape gasmobile, but reports of an all-electric replacement for the crossover SUV may be somewhat premature (cropped, courtesy of Ford).

#5834. Electric Hydrofoils Save Energy, But Do They Earn Their Price?

Hydrofoils have an obvious appeal. Lift a boat’s hull clear of the water, reduce drag and move passengers faster while consuming less energy. I’ve written before about the proposed electric hydrofoil ferry between Vancouver, Bowen Island and Gibsons, where floating logs, coastal conditions and uncertain passenger demand make the economics difficult. Hydrofoils have been commercially available for decades, but their operating niche remains narrower than their technical appeal suggests.

A different opportunity emerged when Antwerp pilotage operator Brabo ordered an Artemis EF-12 electric hydrofoil pilot boat in August 2024. The vessel combines electric propulsion with submerged foils and active controls. Artemis has also demonstrated ship-contact manoeuvres using its EF-12 workboat platform, while the EF-12 Pilot design entered sea trials in 2026. The handling benefits are relevant to pilot transfers even when the boat isn’t operating at full speed.

The financial question is whether a hydrofoil can spend enough of its working life taking advantage of its design. Antwerp pilot boats work around locks, docks, ship movements and changing assignments. Reaching a high maximum speed is of limited value when the vessel spends much of its day manoeuvring or travelling through constrained waterways.

I tested that question using a Python-based operating model with synthetic assignments and three hypothetical sets of port-speed permissions. Depending on how much suitable high-speed water was available, the EF-12 spent zero, approximately 6% or approximately 13% of its underway time foilborne. These are modeled scenarios, not measurements of Brabo’s operations or actual Antwerp speed authorizations.

The resulting financial comparison was unflattering to the hydrofoil. In the central case, the EF-12’s modeled fifteen-year discounted cost was approximately €5.32 million, compared with €3.81 million for a conventional diesel tender, a premium of about 40%. Both figures include purchasing new vessels, rather than comparing a new hydrofoil with the marginal cost of keeping an existing diesel boat.

Electric propulsion still brought substantial energy benefits. The hydrofoil’s problem was that lower purchased energy did not compensate for the assumed capital cost and the limited opportunity to operate in its most efficient regime. Its active controls and potential ride-quality benefits remain relevant, but the available evidence did not justify assigning them a financial value.

An alternative electric approach also deserves consideration. Norway’s Moen Marin and Pascal Technologies have developed an air-cavity electric personnel vessel that reduces hull resistance without lifting most of the vessel above the water. It performed better financially than the hydrofoil in the exploratory comparison, although the existing craft was designed for aquaculture work and would require appropriate adaptation and certification before serving as a pilot tender.

The lesson resembles the one I drew from the Vancouver hydrofoil ferry proposal. Hydrofoils can be excellent machines, especially where sustained high-speed passages and suitable sea conditions allow them to exploit their advantages. In congested harbours, their additional complexity and capital cost need much stronger justification.

Different routes, operating permissions and vessel prices could produce different results. For commercial fleet decisions, the relevant benchmark is the work the vessel actually completes and the total cost of providing that service.

#5833. SEG Solar plans HJT cell plant next to existing Texas facilities

SEG Solar, a global solar manufacturer, has signed a land agreement for a planned $950 million, 4-GW heterojunction (HJT) solar cell plant at the Tomball Industrial Campus in the greater Houston area.

The facility is expected to create more than 700 local jobs and is planned to come online in two 2 GW phases scheduled for 2028 and 2029. SEG reportedly plans to manufacture HJT cells, modules and other key components are made in one place.

The facility will be located adjacent to SEG’s existing 4 GW Tomball #1 module manufacturing facility and its 4.6 GW Tomball #2 module manufacturing facility, which is currently under construction. The company hopes to produce modules with HJT technology to help solar projects meet domestic content requirments.

“Bringing HJT cell manufacturing to the U.S. will give customers a more domestic supply chain with clearer sourcing and a single company standing behind the product,” said Jim Wood, CEO of SEG Solar. “As the only U.S. company dedicated to integrated domestic vertical manufacturing, SEG will continue to strengthen our U.S. operations and innovation. American-made cells, American-made modules, one company accountable.”

SEG opened its second Texas plant in August, and began assembling HJT modules from it the following month.

#5832. GE Vernova in $9B deal to buy high-voltage manufacturer as gas turbine orders slow

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The acquisition of Trench Group will help GE Vernova capitalize on rising electricity demand, company executives said.

GE Vernova delivered “strong financial results” in the third quarter, despite a slowdown in gas turbine orders and slot reservation agreements, CEO Scott Strazik told investors during a Wednesday earnings call.

Total gas turbine gigawatts under contract rose to 122 GW from 116 GW last quarter – an increase of just 6 GW, compared with double-digit GW quarterly increases earlier in 2026.

The company expects its companywide order backlog to exceed $200 billion by next quarter and to have at least 130 GW of gas power equipment under contract by year-end.

Strazik said GE Vernova expected solid growth in turbine orders and reservations in 2027, if not at the breakneck pace of the first half of 2026.

“We certainly continue to see real growth in our gigawatts on contract throughout 2027, but that maybe the quarterly profiles in 2027 are more likely to feel like 3Q and 4Q” of 2026 than like the first half of the year, which saw aggressive growth, he said.

Strazik touted GE Vernova’s planned acquisition of Trench Group in a $9 billion deal the parties expect to close in late 2027. Trench Group has more than 800 utility and hyperscaler customers for its bushings, instrument transformers and coils, according to GE Vernova.

The deal includes approximately $5 billion in backlog and framework agreements, some of which reach into the next decade, Strazik said.

The transaction is poised to boost GE Vernova’s fast-growing Electrification segment, which makes and markets a range of medium- and high-voltage equipment to utilities, independent power producers, infrastructure owners and heavy power consumers.

Trench already supplies GE Vernova with high-voltage direct-current equipment, and the acquisition will allow the larger company to offer “better integrated solutions” while compressing delivery timelines and “enhanc[ing] supply chain certainty for both businesses,” Strazik said.

“Lead times across the industry are increasingly important,” he said. “By bringing Trench's manufacturing footprint and capabilities inside Vernova, we expect greater visibility into production planning and improved ability to align this component supply with our customers' project schedules.”

Trench’s new North Carolina factory will be the first U.S. manufacturer of bushings rated up to 765 kV, “giving us an important capability within our U.S. footprint,” Strazik added.

GE Vernova expects to see continued growth in its gas power order book in 2027, Strazik said.

The company is now taking reservations for delivery in 2032 amid continued strong demand from a mix of about 80% “traditional customers” and 20% data centers, he added.

A favorable mix of higher-priced equipment and a greater proportion of aeroderivative turbines in the order book pushed third-quarter pricing more than 20% higher than in the fourth quarter of 2025 and kept full-year 2026 order pricing on track to rise 50% compared to last year, Strazik said.

But per-kilowatt pricing will likely dip next quarter due to fewer aeroderivatives and more 50-hertz units — which are primarily sold in Europe, Africa and Asia — in the sales mix, he added.

In a Wednesday note, analysts for William Blair noted the lower slot reservation agreements and softer pricing per kW, but said that agreements decreased directly due to conversions and pricing is lower due to “expected revenue mix.”

“While the reversal in trend was initially concerning, management noted no slot exits,” they said. “We believe investors should focus on the pace of new signings … we currently view this [pace] as normalization, not a peak.”

Julien Dumoulin-Smith, a senior energy-sector analyst for Jefferies, said Wednesday that the impacts of GE Vernova’s “soft guidance” for 2027 turbine activity could be blunted if the company continues to deploy cash in transactions like the Trench Group acquisition, which it expects to boost its longer-term fortunes.

GE Vernova’s struggling Wind segment turned a profit in the third quarter thanks to a 16% increase in orders, mostly driven by demand for onshore wind equipment and services in North America.

Offshore wind deliveries also increased in the quarter as GE Vernova nears the finish line on its U.S. offshore wind obligations. In September, the company and the developers of Vineyard Wind 1 announced that they had settled a lawsuit that threatened final delivery of the 806-MW installation off the coast of Massachusetts.

But Strazik and Kenneth Parks, GE Vernova’s chief financial officer, cautioned against reading too much into the quarter’s results.

“We expect to continue to perform very well in the North America onshore wind market, but it's a very small market right now. And although our orders grew year-over-year, that's off of a very small base,” Strazik said. “I'd like to see where the U.S. market goes over the next year and continue to evaluate how we invest in this business for value and/or continue to evaluate how we create value in this business.”

U.S. wind turbine orders neared 3 GW in the second quarter as developers raced to meet construction-start deadlines to qualify for the federal investment tax credit, according to data from Wood Mackenzie.

That credit is now mostly closed to projects that have not begun substantial construction, and U.S. wind projects must be placed in service by the end of 2030 to retain eligibility. Wood Mackenzie expects 39 GW of onshore wind capacity to come online by then.

Still, GE Vernova remains cautious, Parks said.

“We keep saying it's hard to call the inflection in this U.S. market based upon all the dynamics at play, but we will be ready to actually benefit from that based upon all the work that the team is doing today,” he said.

Voltus CEO Dana Guernsey compared new “bring your own new capacity” contracts to the power purchase agreements that “revolutionized” utility-scale solar and wind development.

#5831. Fuel From Bamboo Costs Just $2.75 Per Gallon

At CleanTechnica, we are passionate champions of the “electrify everything” movement promoted by Tony Seba and Mark Jacobson, among others. However, being realistic, internal combustion engines will continue to play an important role in transportation by land, sea, and air for the remainder of this century and perhaps beyond.

Almost any plant that grows could potentially be turned into a biofuel with a lower emissions footprint than gasoline or diesel. Currently in the US, millions of tons of corn are used every year to make ethanol, which is then blended with gasoline. In theory, the ethanol helps lower emissions, but the reduction, if any, is modest and it decreases fuel economy, so motorist are actually using more fuel than they would if they used ordinary, unadulterated gasoline.

The other part of the equation is that the conversion process itself requires a fair amount of energy and makes its own greenhouse gas emissions, which politicians conveniently choose to ignore. Ethanol, then, is mostly a scam that rewards farmers, but has few other benefits. While the science is sound, the rewards are far less than claimed. However, farmers are an important political group and no one wants to tell them that corn for ethanol is no longer a thing.

Researchers at Worcester Polytechnic Institute think they have found a better way. In a study published September 24, 2026 in the Royal Society of Chemistry journal Sustainable Energy And Fuels, Michael Timko, the head of the chemical engineering department at WPI, and  Timothy Woodard, PhD, claim that bamboo may be the perfect source material for making biofuels. In the published study, they write:

“Corn stover, an agricultural residue widely available in the U.S., has been one of the most studied biomass sources for biofuel production.6 Yet its relatively modest yields and high ash content have created significant challenges for scaling up production. Alternative feedstocks such as switch grass have been explored, but these offer only incremental improvements in performance relative to corn stover, particularly in ash content and growth rate. These limitations highlight the importance of identifying and evaluating less conventional biomass sources that may offer superior attributes for fuel production.

“Bamboo has emerged as a strong candidate due to its rapid growth, high productivity per unit area, and low input requirements. Some bamboo species reach their full height within a few months and mature within four years. Bamboo’s deep root systems improve soil stability, promote subsurface carbon sequestration, and support water conservation. It can also be grown on marginal or degraded land, reducing competition with food crops and offering economic opportunities for rural communities.

“Critically, bamboo also possesses an exceptionally low ash content compared with most lignocellulosic feedstocks, which minimizes inorganic fouling and catalyst poisoning during thermochemical conversion and improves the quality of the resulting biocrude. Furthermore, the presence of ash complicates thermochemical conversion to products, with frequently reported adverse effects on yields.

“The characteristics of fast growth, low fertilizer and water use, and low ash content make bamboo stand out as a promising source of biomass that can achieve economic competitiveness that has not been obtained with other feedstocks. Unfortunately, despite these advantages, bamboo remains largely underexplored as a feedstock for liquid fuel production, especially in thermochemical conversion processes such as hydrothermal liquefaction (HTL).”

Here’s the best part. The researchers believe biofuel from bamboo could be commercially viable at a price of $2.75 a gallon. That’s less than gasoline at the moment and less than half the cost of diesel. What’s more, when compared to diesel, the resulting emissions are up to 26 times lower. So let’s see:  Less than half the cost and only 5 percent of the emissions? Think anyone would take that deal? Oh, you betcha!

Bamboo is a grass, and one of the fastest-growing plants on Earth. Some species grow several dozen inches in a day. Because of this fast growth, the US could make as much fuel from bamboo as it makes ethanol from corn while using only 13 percent as much land.

“I think this research is especially timely and important because fuel prices are high and the transportation sector produces more greenhouse gas emissions in the U.S. than any other,” said professor Timko in a press release. “Bamboo would use different land than we are using for food production, so there is no food versus fuel trade-off. Farmers win, consumers win.

Using bamboo for fuel can reduce greenhouse gasses in two ways, the researchers maintain. First, the process to convert it into biocrude is more efficient than it is for other feedstocks. Second, bamboo itself captures and stores large amounts of carbon both above and below ground.

Unlike annual crops used for many biofuels, bamboo is a perennial grass that can produce large amounts of biomass with relatively low agricultural inputs. Compared with other perennial grasses, bamboo has very low ash content (less than 1% by dry weight), meaning that the conversion process generates negligible waste.

The economic modeling conducted by the researchers indicates that bamboo-based fuel could sell for as little as $2.75 per gasoline equivalent under mature commercial-scale conditions, accounting for the money required to build and operate the fuel plant, suggesting strong economic competitiveness as the renewable-fuel industry expands. Considering the ability of bamboo to store large amounts of carbon in its underground rhizomes, producing fuel from bamboo would generate a greenhouse gas intensity of 3.5 grams of carbon dioxide-equivalent per megajoule of energy, the researchers report.

“The U.S. has enormous land area under cultivation for corn ethanol, a fuel which is often blended into gasoline,” said professor Timko. “Freeing up at least some of that land could allow for more food production and reduce the burden on the environment. Bamboo may eventually offer industry a way to generate low carbon liquid fuels at scale. With further research and development, bamboo could be a compelling option for decarbonization in sectors that are difficult to electrify, including heavy duty transport and aviation fuel supply chains.”

The researchers also point out that bamboo can be cultivated on former industrial land or heavily farmed properties where other crops and vegetation struggle to grow. Their research found the growth rate of bamboo (22–44 tons per hectare per year) “is at least three times that of corn stover and at least twice that of switch grass.

Based on the lowest growth rate, an idealized circular [bamboo] plantation with a 30 mile radius corresponds to an estimated 16.1 million dry tons of biomass per year. Ash content, a critical parameter for processing efficiency and waste generation, was less than 1 percent in bamboo, compared with 4.8–7.3 percent for corn stover and 2.2–11.9 percent for switch grass.

With such data in mind, expect the current US administration to impose a heavy tax on bamboo based biofuel to protect the profits of the oil industry and corn growers. Nothing must be allowed to challenge the hegemony of fossil fuels or the profits of Big Ag. And please don’t tell the head doofus that the WPI research was funded in part by Pacific Northwest National Laboratory and the National Science Foundation. If he found that out, his head might explode!

Steve writes about the interface between technology and sustainability from his home in Florida or anywhere else The Force may lead him. He is proud to be "woke" and believes weak leaders push others down while strong leaders lift others up. You can follow him on Substack at https://stevehanley.substack.com/ but not on Fakebook or any social media platforms controlled by narcissistic yahoos.

#5830. Trump admin ‘pauses’ leases for five offshore wind projects already under construction

The U.S. Dept. of the Interior has immediately paused the leases for all large-scale offshore wind projects under construction in the United States due to “national security risks identified by the Dept. of War in recently completed classified reports.”

“The prime duty of the United States government is to protect the American people,” said Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum. “Today’s action addresses emerging national security risks, including the rapid evolution of the relevant adversary technologies, and the vulnerabilities created by large-scale offshore wind projects with proximity near our East Coast population centers. The Trump administration will always prioritize the security of the American people.”

The Dept. of the Interior claims that the blade movement and reflective towers create “radar interference called ‘clutter.'” Allegedly, this is a national security risk.

Dominion Energy released the following statement in response to the pause issued for the Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project:

“The Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind Project (CVOW) is essential for American national security and meeting Virginia’s dramatically growing energy needs, the fastest growth in America. This growth is driven by the need to provide reliable power to many of America’s most important war fighting installations, the world’s largest warship manufacturer, and the largest concentration of data centers on the planet as well as the leading edge of the AI revolution.

Stopping CVOW for any length of time will threaten grid reliability for some of the nation’s most important war fighting, AI and civilian assets. It will also lead to energy inflation and threaten thousands of jobs.

CVOW is American-owned and benefits all of our Virginia customers. Our customers are paying for the project after a careful review of project costs and benefits by Virginia state regulators in 2022. These same state regulators, along with numerous federal agencies, oversee our cyber and physical security program, which is among the strongest in the energy industry.

The project has been more than 10 years in the works, involved close coordination with the military, and is located 27 to 44 miles offshore, so far offshore it does not raise visual impact concerns. The project’s two pilot turbines have been operating for five years without causing any impacts to national security.

CVOW enjoys bipartisan support and is within months of generating a massive 2,600 MW to support the fastest growing part of America’s energy grid. This growth serves the largest concentration of critical infrastructure in the world.

Virginia’s All-American, All-Of-The-Above-Energy Plan requires a range of power generation assets, including natural gas, advanced nuclear, and renewables. Virginia needs every electron we can get as our demand for electricity doubles. These electrons will power the data centers that will win the AI race, support our war fighters, and build the nuclear warships needed to maintain our maritime supremacy. Virginia’s grid needs addition of electrons, not subtraction.

We stand ready to do what is necessary to get these vital electrons flowing as quickly as possible.”

#5829. Electric Vehicle Council Celebrating 10 Years of EV Progress in Australia

The Electric Vehicle Council has been tracking the progress of electric vehicle uptake in Australia over the last ten years. Their latest report is a celebration of achievements. It is 144 pages long, and you can read the entire report here. I will just be hitting the highlights and providing some comments along the way.

In a demonstration of how fast the EV transition is moving in Australia, the EVC report projections to the end of the year are already hopelessly out of date, with a surge over the last three months looking set to continue through to the end of the year. Nevertheless, the report makes encouraging reading.

Both BEV and PHEV sales have doubled comparing H1 2025 with H2 2026. A new EV is sold in Australia every 100 seconds as Australians seek to cut costs and gain energy security. The ratio of BEV to PHEV sits at 2:1, similar to many other countries. The report warns of a possible “fragility” in Australia’s EV sales due to increasing demand for public charging and the possible introduction of a road user charge.

The report says that so far installation of public charging has kept up with demand. This is due to the fact that most Australians charge at home. There are 4268 AC and DC public charging locations available, an increase of 18% year on year and 11,600 public AC and DC connectors, a 31% increase year on year.

Political threats might also derail the electric train of progress. Australia’s right wing political parties are threatening to remove the New Vehicle Efficiency Standards and the Fringe Benefit Tax exemption should they gain power. The battle is not yet won. Intense lobbying by vested interests in the auto industry (FCAI) has led to significant misrepresentation in the media. The FUD is ever with us.

The report notes that the FBT exemption has been reviewed and extended to 2029. This may be long enough for EVs to enter the second-hand market where most Australians buy their cars. Australia has set a target of 5 million BEVs on the road by 2035 — “uptake must keep accelerating” if the country is to achieve this target. At the time of this report’s publication, there were 612,000 BEVs on Australian roads.

The top 5 best-selling plugin EVs in the Australian market over the past 12 months have been: Tesla Model Y (20,400), BYD Sealion 7 (12,500), BYD Shark 6 PHEV (9,500), Geely EX 5 (6,800), and Jaecoo J5 (6,000). I don’t expect this to change as we rush towards the end of the year. CleanTechnica readers would have been familiar with this lineup from regular reports. For the latest one, see here.

A comparison with CleanTechnica’s figures and those of the EVC will demonstrate how rapidly sales are increasing. YTD figures of each model listed in the CleanTechnica report are almost double that in the EVC report.

There is quite the disparity between the takeup of vehicle classes, with buses leading the way. Thirty two percent of new buses sold in H1 2026 were battery electric, compared to passenger vehicles 26%, vans and trucks a miserable 1%. I am very surprised at this, as small business and its need for last-mile delivery would benefit from reducing their costs for transport. According to the report, there are 16 different vans and 30 trucks to choose from. Julie Delvecchio, the CEO of EVC, calls this “the single biggest unclaimed opportunity left.” Perhaps Australia needs some low-emissions zones in our cities? Sydney, Melbourne, and Brisbane would certainly benefit from this measure.

Utes make up a sizeable portion of the Australian market. At the moment, there are only 5 plugin utes available (mainly plugin hybrids like the BYD Shark 6). When this part of the market shifts to battery electric, I expect the game will be over for diesel.

The EVC supports a federal (as opposed to state by state) road user charge, which is appropriate and enables the benefits of EVs to be shared with all Australians. In other words, “a fair go.” As an EV driver, I am not opposed to an RUC in principle.

A series of videos about EV charging to educate and build confidence for the public have been produced by the EVC — you can get a taste here.

So far in 2026, ANCAP (Australasian New Car Assessment Program) has declared that 6 out 7 of the safest cars in Australia are EVs. Despite the squealing of legacy automakers about the NVES (New Vehicle Efficiency Standards), carmakers are easily meeting their emissions reduction targets. There is now serious consideration of EVs as a part of Australia’s energy system (V2G). We are moving beyond the early adopter phase and into the early majority — how soon we start the steep ascent up the “S” curve remains to be seen. Sales will certainly be helped by reduced prices of EVs. The BYD Atto 1 (aka Dolphin Mini, Seagull) is now the cheapest car of any drivetrain — less than AU$20,000.

The EVC report tells us that the same is now happening in the van segment. Electric vans are quickly approaching price parity with diesel. Again, events have overtaken the EVC report — three new vans have just launched with prices below AU$50,000, undercutting brands from Korea and Europe. This looks like the beginning of a price war in this sector. The absolute price leader is the LDV eDeliver 5 from SAIC. Then comes the Geely Farizon V7E. The best legacy automakers can offer is the Ford Transit City. The specs are very similar.

Comparing the eDeliver 5 to similar diesel configurations, it appears that the only advantage for diesel is double the range. For city runs, the electric vehicle is the best option. With fuel insecurity, increased prices at the pump, and lower maintenance, I expect there to be a surge in this sector. Year to date up till August, 46 eDeliver 5’s and 83 Farizon V7E have been sold.

Ten years have gone by since the first EVC report (I have been driving electric for 7 of those). Sometimes it feels like it has gone quickly, and sometimes too slowly for those participating in the rEVolution. The first EVC report cited an AEMO (Australian Energy Market Operator) prediction that 25% of new vehicles bought by Australians would be battery electric by 2035. We have already achieved that, 10 years early! To quote Chau Le, chair of the EVC, “If this is the transformation over the last 10 years, then the next decade will be nothing short of electrifying.”

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5828. Xcel Energy rolls out $70B, 5-year capital plan focused on renewables

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“We believe that we can, in fact, protect the planet” while serving growing demand, CEO Bob Frenzel said. The spending plan calls for adding 9.2 GW of renewables and 2.3 GW of storage.

Renewable energy will account for nearly three-fourths of new generation built under Xcel Energy's five-year, $70 billion capital plan that the company unveiled Thursday during its third-quarter earnings call.

The new plan — up 17% from its previous $60 billion spending plan — calls for building 9.2 GW of renewable generation, 2.3 GW of energy storage and 3.3 GW of natural gas generation. Xcel expects the renewable portion of the buildout would cost about $21.7 billion. The plan also includes 2,400 miles of new transmission and $4 billion for wildfire mitigation.

“These investments are critical to serve growing electric demand, to keep customer bills low, to deliver clean energy, and to ensure system safety and reliability,” Chairman, President and CEO Bob Frenzel said, later adding that Xcel “continue[s] to lead a clean energy transition” and believes its commitment to clean energy will attract additional data centers to its service territory.

About $7 billion of the capital plan will go to data center projects already under contract at Xcel. Xcel could spend an additional $10 billion for projects not included in its spending plan, according to Frenzel. That spending would cover the more than 15 GW that is included in pending utility requests for proposals, high-voltage transmission projects and infrastructure needed to serve the 4 GW of new data centers the company expects to contract before the end of next year, he said.

“As we think about future data center growth, our contracting principles are based on our longstanding commitment to ensure that the needs of our customers and our communities are met,” Frenzel said. “We believe in responsible development. We believe that existing customers should not subsidize new large loads.”

To finance the updated capital plan, Xcel will take on $26 billion in debt and issue $8 billion in equity, about 40% of which is already contracted, CFO Brian Van Abel said. The company also agreed earlier this month to sell gas and electric assets in Michigan — including 9,000 electric customers and 6,000 gas customers — valued at $150 million to Upper Peninsula Power Co. and SEMCO ENERGY Gas Co. Van Abel said the sale should close by the end of 2027 and that the price was confidential.

Xcel’s net income jumped 83% to $960 million in the third quarter, up from $524 million a year ago, the company said. Favorable weather, increasing electric sales and this year’s “heavy rate case calendar” all helped Xcel increase its third-quarter earnings, Van Abel said.

A July order by the Minnesota Public Utilities Commission approved a 2.9% annual rate increase expected to generate $211 million over two years. Xcel filed a rate stability plan in Minnesota on Oct. 9 that would freeze rates at this level in 2027 and 2028, as an alternative to the rate case it is due to file in 2027. In Colorado, a September decision by the CPUC approved a 1.7% annual rate that will bring in $184 million. The company expects a final decision on a rate case in New Mexico by the end of this year.

Xcel cannot yet estimate its total liability for the 2024 Smokehouse Creek fire pending litigation initiated by the Texas Attorney General's office that is expected to continue into 2027, according to the company's quarterly U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission filing. The company has so far settled 358 claims related to the fire for $410 million; claims by 141 plaintiffs remain unresolved. The Smokehouse Creek Fire burned more than 1 million acres in the Texas Panhandle. Liabilities associated with the fire could exceed Xcel's $525 million insurance policy, according to the SEC filing.

Large-load tariffs and other measures to weed out speculative projects are taking shape. Public backlash to data centers, shifting state-level policies and the utility profit model are complicating those efforts, stakeholders say.
