#5827. HomePower 1000 Plus v2: Portable Power Put To Use

I have been using the Jackery HomePower 1000 Plus v2 in a wide range of applications over the past few months around my apartment in Queens, NY. The flexibility and portability make it especially useful for people living in apartments. I had previously written about the Jackery HomePower 1000 Plus v2 when it launched. You can read more about the technical specifications in that article.

Overall, the battery charged in about an hour. It starts charging at a little over 1000 W and then drops to a little over 800 watts as it charges. The first thing I plugged into the battery was a fan, which used about 50 watts. Then I plugged in my laptop, which charged at another 50 watts. I was able to work comfortably for a day off the battery with capacity to spare.

Keeping on the cool side, the battery was easily able to run my 12,000 BTU window air conditioner on a warmer day. Being in a century-old pre-war building, insulation could leave something to be desired. In addition, my corner apartment with large windows and 10-foot ceilings contributes to the load. Air conditioners can be a challenge to power, as the power consumption tends to spike when the compressor kicks in, but it did not pose a challenge to the battery. On a day in the mid-80’s (Fahrenheit), the battery was able to keep the indoor temperature in the mid-70’s all day with capacity to spare. Also, as my wife likes plugging her 1750 W clothes iron into the same circuit as the air conditioner, adding the battery could reduce the potential to trip the breaker when the compressor kicks in. It would be useful to maintain comfort during a power outage.

In terms of keeping things cool, the battery tends to use a little over 3% of its capacity per hour on my high-efficiency 14.1 cubic foot Midea refrigerator during the day with the door opening periodically. With the door staying shut, it would be closer to 2% per hour. That could potentially keep food from spoiling for a couple of days during an outage. While Jackery makes the FridgeGuard specifically for this application, the Jackery HomePower 1000 Plus v2 could come in handy for added capacity during an emergency.

The battery would also come in handy for people with fish tanks to keep the pumps running during a power outage. I have an aquaponics setup with a few small fish to grow herbs and start seeds in the Spring. The battery can easily run the filters, pumps and heaters, as well as the powerful lights for the plants. However, for someone with exotic fish in a saltwater aquarium, battery backup could be essential to keeping the fish alive during a power disruption.

In addition, I was able to run my Vitamix blender with the HomePower 1000 Plus v2. The powerful motor can draw over 1000 W, but it doesn’t take long to blend up a smoothie or frozen beverage. As such, you could easily make frozen margaritas at a backyard party using the battery to power the blender.

In terms of cooking, the battery could easily handle my rice cooker and Instapot pressure cooker for extended cooking times. I was able to cook an 8 lb. pork roast in the pressure cooker. It also had enough power to run my air fryer and microwave. However, with the air fryer drawing as much as 1700 W, it depletes the capacity quickly, even though the load is within the 1800 W continuous rating of the battery. A kettle or coffee maker does not pose much of a challenge. You could also potentially run an electric grill, although I did not have the opportunity to try it yet. This could make a cookout more convenient, in addition to reducing risks associated with fire-based grills in dry areas.

After all that, I needed to clean up. I was able to run my dishwasher off the battery. Heating water is clearly one area that consumes a lot of energy, but I was able to run the dishwasher for a full cycle with room to spare. I was also able to run my portable washing machine off the battery. While the loads were not massive, the voltage fluctuates significantly while it is running. It did not create an issue for the battery. Of note, these are efficient NYC apartment-sized appliances. Many Europeans use similar-sized appliances, but middle-American McMansions tend to use more power-hungry models. Your mileage may vary.

Shifting to a work environment. I was easily able to charge my laptop, run the router, charge my phone, etc., with capacity to spare. You could easily keep an office running during a power outage. Or set up a remote office for the day, returning to charge at night.

I also used the battery to run my Bambu Labs X1-C 3D printer. Overall, the load is small enough to produce large prints that last hours using just the battery for power. However, when plugged in, the battery can ensure consistent power through multiple prints. I used a power strip to turn on and off the power to the battery on several occasions. There was not a flicker or even the slightest variance in the print layers. If you have ever had a power disruption during a multi-hour complicated print, the benefit is clear. Moving forward, I wouldn’t use the printer on a large print without the battery. In addition to serving as a high-capacity UPS, I was also able to time shift the electricity consumption of the printer.

Using the Jackery App, I was able to easily set up the battery to use a TOU utility plan. By entering the times and rates, it can even calculate the savings. In addition, you can program the battery to retain a set amount of capacity for backup, seamlessly switching to input power when that capacity level is reached.

For Con Edison small business TOU rates, peak electricity supply from 8 AM to 10 PM is $0.5443/kWh from June 1 to September 30 and $0.2680/kWh all other months. Off peak is just $0.0199. Of note, this does not include the separate service charges, delivery charges and taxes, which on my last utility bill worked out to be $0.3378/ kWh on top of the electricity supply charges. Electricity is expensive in NYC. However, many small businesses rent and do not have access to the panel to take advantage of the potential TOU savings from a larger battery at that location. Smaller batteries can be implemented at point of use, such as a workstation. As such, a battery like a HomePower 1000 Plus v2 at each workstation could save roughly $0.50 per day in the summer and $0.25 in the winter. Payback on the investment could come in a few years. If a business changed locations, they could easily take the batteries with them. However, the ability to provide UPS battery backup could also easily pay for itself quickly if work could continue uninterrupted, work in progress could be saved and sensitive equipment could be protected.

To take it a step further, the HomePower 1000 Plus v2 can directly take DC solar input. This does not feed back into the grid, but it could provide power for different uses. This could be useful when camping or even with a more permanent solar setup to power an appliance, workshop or small office. While it would not replace the capability of balcony solar, the ability to run appliances off small-scale solar that does not feed into the grid could provide some of the benefits while the balcony solar regulations are being figured out.

Overall, I found the battery to be more useful than I originally anticipated. I tended to look at the power rating of different devices and assumed that they would pull that for the entire duration and drain the battery quickly. However, many cycle on and off, making 1024 Wh of battery go much farther than I would have thought. In addition, Jackery’s power and capacity ratings seem to be conservative, letting me use it right up to the rated limits without issues. And the portability lets me conveniently try it in a wide range of applications. Living in an apartment with efficient appliances, it also fits many of my potential use cases. And it gives people living in apartments who do not have the ability to modify the panel many of the benefits of battery storage. However, if the HomePower 1000 Plus V2 turns out to be  not enough for your needs, Jackery also offers expansion batteries to increase the capacity, as well as offering larger capacity models. While travel conflicts did not give me the chance to try it out for camping yet, I hope to use it outdoors soon.

Of course, Jackery also offers much larger batteries. And they have some even smaller batteries coming. But that is for another article.

Jackery supplied the HomePower 1000 Plus v2 for review, but did not pay for the review. The thoughts here are my own.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5826. States must take a critical eye to gas plants proposed over clean energy

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The key question for regulators is no longer whether utilities can simply find a reason to install another gas turbine, write experts from Environmental Defense Fund and Minnesota’s CURE.

Will Scott is the North Carolina policy director of the Environmental Defense Fund. Hudson Kingston is the legal director at CURE, a nonprofit rural environmental and energy democracy organization in Minnesota.

The data center boom has become a cash cow for investor-owned utilities across the country. Many utility companies are seeking approval for costly new gas plants and other fossil fuel infrastructure based on uncertain projections of future demand for data centers and other large customers. If those projections fall short, ratepayers are left with 30 years of payments for projects that are increasingly expensive to build and slow to develop. We can’t let phantom projections for new data centers leave regular ratepayers with the bill.

Historically, state regulators have given utilities significant latitude to pursue new generation. Yet, as heating and cooling bills become painfully unaffordable for families and local businesses, utility regulators are increasingly skeptical of proposals that could leave them paying for decades of dirty and expensive fossil fuel infrastructure, when clean, renewable energy and batteries provide a reliable, homegrown and affordable alternative. Indeed, a recent study found that batteries are now cheaper than gas peaker plants, globally.

While customers are forced to pay increasingly higher energy bills, utility shareholders are guaranteed profits on centralized fossil energy projects. The bigger and more costly the construction, the more money energy corporations make. Once new energy infrastructure is approved by state regulators, utilities can start recovering the costs through customer rates even before the project is built and in use. Because utilities make profits off the infrastructure they build, and not the energy they sell, executives have a financial incentive to keep building to benefit shareholders.

To be clear, getting ahead of potential demand is not inherently problematic. Investing in clean and affordable energy generation to meet everyday customers’ future needs for things like home heating and cooling and electric vehicle charging would lower prices and improve reliability. The issue is that utilities are using that incentive and uncertain future demand to justify spending customers’ money on new fossil fuel infrastructure that will raise prices and pollution.

Amid this surge in data center proposals, regulators must be proactive and diligent in protecting customers from imprudent utility spending on fossil fuels, particularly when clean energy offers an affordable alternative.

In the Midwest, Minnesota Power proposed large amounts of new gas generation as part of its plan to meet future electricity demand. Instead of granting the utility carte blanche to build more gas infrastructure, as is common in these proceedings, Minnesota’s regulators have directed the utility to explore less costly alternatives, including a scenario that adds wind, solar and storage instead of expensive and polluting gas power plants that would run afoul of the state’s carbon-free standard.

Wind and solar have been cheaper than gas plants for nearly a decade in the U.S. Clean energy keeps getting more affordable while the cost of constructing new gas plants has increased by 66%, and the time required to bring them online grew by 23% between 2023 and 2025. Despite that, in 2025 utilities reported plans to build 24 GW of gas plant capacity, representing a 570% increase over 2023. Utilities justify this build out of gas plants on the basis of projected data-center demand, rather than the current needs of households and local businesses. Not all regulators are buying it.

In North Carolina, regulators rejected a Duke Energy $584 million gas turbine that the company argued is needed to meet the energy needs of anticipated large customers, particularly data centers. Regulators found that Duke’s anticipated load projections were not justification enough to saddle customers with another expensive gas project, that the project put too much risk on residential ratepayers and that the utility had not adequately examined potentially cheaper alternatives, like batteries.

With profits driving spending decisions rather than what’s in the best interest of customers, utilities wielding monopoly power often deliberately squeeze out more affordable energy sources, such as wind and solar — which are also faster to bring online — when forced to split ownership of those facilities with project developers.

In fact, some utilities are actively working against cheaper energy options. Duke Energy was found to have artificially capped energy storage and solar energy in its modeling to justify the need for new, lucrative gas plants.

In Minnesota and North Carolina, we’ve seen what happens when state regulators use their authority to stand up to utilities and protect residents from unnecessarily high energy costs. Both states offer examples of how regulators can push back against costly gas projects and put the interests of ratepayers first. Speculative demand should not be the burden of customers, and customers shouldn’t have to pay for gas when solar and wind are cheaper.

The question for regulators is no longer simply whether utilities can find a reason to build another gas plant. It is whether utilities can prove that customers actually need it, whether clean energy and batteries could do a better, more cost-efficient job, and whether families and businesses should unnecessarily shoulder decades of debt risk to cover short term demand projections driven by data center developers.

Get the free daily newsletter read by industry experts

Major work by regional planners is focused on flexible access to existing transmission. Some say greater emphasis on advanced transmission technologies can quickly and cost-effectively create room for new large loads.

Large-load tariffs and other measures to weed out speculative projects are taking shape. Public backlash to data centers, shifting state-level policies and the utility profit model are complicating those efforts, stakeholders say.

Voltus CEO Dana Guernsey compared new “bring your own new capacity” contracts to the power purchase agreements that “revolutionized” utility-scale solar and wind development.

#5825. PJM’s Emergency Order Should Be a Wake-Up Call for Michigan: Build More Power Before the Next Crisis

By Terence Parker, Michigan resident and Solar Mounts LLC team member

The U.S. Department of Energy’s October 12, 2026, emergency order directing PJM Interconnection to use additional generating resources should be a wake-up call for Michigan and the entire PJM region. Effective October 13–16, the order authorizes specified power plants to operate as needed for reliability and allows backup generators at large facilities to help reduce grid demand during emergencies.

This does not mean blackouts are inevitable. However, it highlights the serious challenges facing our electricity system and the need to add capacity before emergency measures become routine.

As a Michigan-based company, Solar Mounts LLC, a designer and manufacturer of commercial and utility-scale solar mounting systems, recognizes the potential implications for electricity prices, air quality, local businesses, and families across the state.

Rising Costs and the Consequences of Delayed Generation

PJM’s capacity auctions have exposed the difficulty of securing enough electricity for future demand. Prices reached $329.17 per megawatt-day for the 2026–2027 delivery year and $333.44 for 2027–2028, with the latter auction falling short of PJM’s stated reliability requirement.

Source: Vicinity Energy’s Analysis of PJM Capacity Auctions

Capacity costs are only one component of electricity bills, but rising prices signal growing pressure on consumers. We need more power before shortages force increasingly expensive emergency responses.

The October 2026 order permits designated generating units to exceed specified operating limits when necessary for reliability. It also authorizes PJM to direct backup generation at data centers and other large facilities, potentially including diesel generators as a last resort.

Running fossil-fuel plants more frequently can increase emissions, depending on the units involved. Operating diesel generators will worsen local noise pollution and expose operators to high diesel prices. If these measures become more common, we should ask why cleaner generation and storage are not coming online faster.

Weather Uncertainty Makes Planning Urgent

NOAA’s October 15, 2026, winter outlook anticipates warmer-than-average conditions across much of the northern United States, including the Midwest, influenced by a strong El Niño.

Source: NOAA’s 2026–2027 U.S. Winter Outlook

Seasonal forecasts cannot predict every period of extreme demand. Aging infrastructure, maintenance, changing weather, and growing electricity consumption reinforce the need for advance planning. Temporary authority to run additional fossil-fuel resources cannot replace long-term investment in generation.

Solar, Wind, and Batteries Can Help Close the Gap

Utility-scale solar, wind, and battery storage can help meet demand on timelines that many conventional generation projects cannot match. Suitable solar projects may be built within 18 months, depending on permitting, equipment, and interconnection. New nuclear facilities generally require substantially longer development periods.

Solar and wind will reduce emissions when they displace electricity from fossil-fuel generators. Batteries store surplus electricity for periods of higher demand. Note: actual emissions benefits depend on which generators are displaced and when electricity is delivered.

Faster interconnection reviews are also essential. Qualified solar projects shouldn’t remain in queues for years because of avoidable administrative delays. Better coordination among PJM, utilities, regulators, developers, and state governments, alongside necessary transmission upgrades, will bring capacity online sooner.

Solar Development and Agriculture Can Coexist

At Solar Mounts LLC, we witness concerns about installing solar on productive farmland here in rural Calhoun County (home to Solar Mounts LLC). However, well-designed projects can include restoration plans that allow agricultural use to resume when leases end. Solar leases can also provide farmers with supplemental income when crop prices fail to keep pace with production costs.

Agrivoltaic systems combine electricity generation with compatible crops or livestock. Thoughtful design and community engagement can balance renewable energy development with agricultural productivity, rural traditions, and farmland preservation.

Opportunities extend beyond prime farmland. Parking lots, commercial properties, brownfields, landfills, and other underutilized sites can accommodate solar generation. Solar carports provide electricity, shade, and weather protection, while community solar expands access for people unable or unwilling to install their own panels.

Solar Mounts LLC supports practical, site-appropriate solutions that expand generation while respecting farmers, landowners, and rural communities.

Hyperscale Data Centers Must Help Provide Their Own Power

Growing data center demand, particularly in Virginia and other parts of PJM’s territory, is adding pressure to an already constrained grid. Consequently, developers of new hyperscale facilities should demonstrate how they will secure adequate electricity and pay their fair share of supporting infrastructure. Dedicated generation, renewable energy contracts, batteries, flexible demand, and grid upgrades can all contribute.

Requiring every facility to build a gas-fired peaker plant is not necessarily the best answer. Gas turbines face equipment and supply-chain constraints, while additional fossil-fuel generation carries emissions and operating costs. A combination of solutions tailored to each location is more practical.

Existing facilities should also reduce peak demand where possible. Energy efficiency remains one of the most immediate ways businesses can limit electricity consumption.

Solar Mounts LLC sees the opportunity to expand commercial and utility-scale solar infrastructure across Michigan and the country. We encourage this expansion by supporting faster approvals, better grid planning, and a serious commitment to adding power generation and energy storage before shortages worsen.

Reliable electricity underpins our economy, jobs, and daily lives. Repeated emergency orders, rising capacity costs, and greater reliance on high-emission generation are not sound long-term strategies.

We shouldn’t wait for the next crisis to build the power system we already know we need.

#5824. The Share of Solar Generation with Co-located Battery Storage Is Increasing

Utility-scale solar developers are increasingly co-locating battery storage with new solar photovoltaic capacity. As of September 2026, over 16% of new utility-scale solar capacity added this year is co-located with operable battery storage, compared with 2% in 2017, according to data reported to EIA by operators.

Reported plans for solar additions scheduled to come online in the last three months of this year show that the share with co-located battery storage could increase to 19% this year. Longer-term plans indicate that this trend will continue, with the portion increasing to more than 30% in plans submitted for 2027 and 2028.

When operators co-locate battery storage with solar, they can direct solar electricity output to the grid or to the battery, depending on the wholesale prices. Wholesale prices in regions with large solar participation tend to fall when solar contributions are high and rise as they decrease. Plants with both solar and battery storage can manage the combined output of their generating resources to maximize their value in a changing wholesale price environment.

We publish unit-level generator inventory data based on our annual and monthly data collections, including information on design attributes for all utility-scale plants. Although battery storage and solar units at the same plant often enter commercial service at different times, the EIA-860 and EIA-860M data collection program tracks these developments and provides a monthly summary that can be used to identify co-located technologies.

Southwest states have higher-than-average solar capacity shares relative to their total generating capacity and also have higher levels of co-located utility-scale solar and battery capacity. As of September, 13% of the total U.S. utility-scale generating capacity is solar photovoltaic, and 18% of that is co-located with battery storage. The share of solar generation co-located with battery capacity in California, Arizona, New Mexico, and Nevada is more than double the national average.

Principal contributors: Glenn McGrath, Alex Mey

The EIA collects, analyzes, and disseminates independent and impartial energy information to promote sound policymaking, efficient markets, and public understanding of energy and its interaction with the economy and the environment.
